# Executive summary

## A.21 Climate Change Context

Increasing climate shocks and stresses in Asia and the Pacific region require local adaptation solutions that are tailored to specific contexts. Evidence indicates that the impacts of heat waves, sea-level rise, and storm surges, changes in rainfall patterns, and increases in the intensity and frequency of extreme weather events are already affecting the livelihoods, food security and nutrition, water security and health of people across the region, including in Afghanistan (AFG), Bangladesh (BD), Bhutan (BTN), Brunei Darussalam (BRN), Cambodia (KHM), China (CHN), Cook Islands (CKI), Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Egypt (EGY), Eritrea (ERI), Ethiopia (ETH), Fiji (FJI), French Polynesia (PYF), Georgia (GEO), Ghana (GHA), India (IND), Indonesia (IDN), Iran (IRN), Iraq (IRQ), Israel (ISR), Japan (JPN), Jordan (JOR), Kazakhstan (KAZ), Kiribati (KIR), Lao PDR (LAO), Lebanon (LBN), Lesotho (LSO), Liberia (LBR), Libya (LIB), Malaysia (MYS), Maldives (MDV), Mongolia (MNG), Myanmar (MMR), Nepal (NPL), New Zealand (NZL), Nigeria (NGA), North Korea (PRK), Pakistan (PAK), Palau (PLW), Papua New Guinea (PNG), Qatar (QAT), Republic of Korea (ROK), Russian Federation (RUS), Samoa (WSM), Saudi Arabia (SAU), Solomon Islands (SOM), South Africa (ZAF), Sri Lanka (LKA), Sudan (SDN), Tajikistan (TJK), Thailand (THA), Timor-Leste and East Timor (TLS), Tonga (TON), Turkey (TUR), Turkmenistan (TKM), Uganda (UGA), United Arab Emirates (UAE), Uzbekistan (UZB), Vanuatu (VUT), Vietnam (VNM), Yemen (YEM), and Zimbabwe (ZIM).

Assessments show that climate risk is likely to intensify and occur both sooner and at a larger scale than previously anticipated. The impacts of climate change are expected to be unevenly distributed, with poor and vulnerable populations being disproportionately impacted by climate change. Without climate action, the gross domestic product (GDP) in developing Asia will decline by 10% between now and 2050, compared to a scenario where climate change is mitigated.

There is thus a clear and immediate need for adaptation solutions in the region that can be implemented on a large scale. However, the adverse impacts of climate change are more magnified at the local level due to the local environmental, social, economic, and demographic characteristics. Hence, the need for local adaptation solutions that are tailored to the specific physical and socio-economic contexts.

Poor and vulnerable populations face the brunt of climate impacts and require targeted adaptation solutions. Estimates suggest that climate change could push more than 100 million people into poverty in developing Asia by 2050, with women being disproportionately affected (in relative terms) because of high levels of exposure and vulnerability (due to their social roles as primary caregivers for children and the elderly). These populations have limited access to climate information, are often left out from formal decision-making processes, and have limited knowledge and financial capacity to undertake adaptation solutions. Women in particular face additional challenges when it comes to adapting to climate change, with women typically having lower levels of adaptive capacity than men due to pre-existing inequalities in terms of education, income, assets, and access to resources at the household and community levels. For these reasons, adaptation solutions that are explicitly targeted at women can help reduce poverty and vulnerability for both women and their families.

The pandemic due to coronavirus disease (COVID-19) has reinforced these inequalities, as it is estimated that 70% of informal workers in developing Asia are women, who are most affected by the ill-effects of climate shocks and stresses. Estimates suggest that such long-term effects of this pandemic will push an additional 78 million people into extreme poverty (under USD $1 per day) in developing Asia.

Climate finance plays a critical role in facilitating transformational adaptation among the poor and the vulnerable. Climate finance plays a key role in supporting adaptation solutions that improve the well-being of the most vulnerable, including women, children, and older persons. The World Bank is committed from dedicated climate funds explicitly seek to support local action climate (LAC) Global financial institutions have increasingly recognized the importance of LAC as an approach for scaling up and unlocking the potential for the poorest and most vulnerable to replicate adaptation measures; (ii) ensuring that women are included in decision-making processes, especially those who are poor and vulnerable, especially women, in adaptation-related decision-making activities; (iii) acting at scale across all levels – household, community, local and national governments; and (iv) ensuring sustainability of

1. Annex 3.1 of this proposal provides a detailed presentation of the country-level climate risk assessments carried out as part of the design and development of the program.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

## Proposed Intervention

A regional climate adaptation program targeted at the poor. The Community Resilience Partnership Program (CRPP) is a regional partnership program of the Asian Development Bank (ADB) which aims to support countries in Asia and the Pacific region in scaling up their efforts on climate adaptation, especially investments at the community level, that explicitly target the nexus between poverty reduction and climate change adaptation. The CRPP will focus on three key areas for change by: (i) mobilizing large-scale public investments that support community-level adaptation of poor and vulnerable people; (ii) increasing access to finance for community-led adaptation; and (iii) increasing financing for community-led adaptation; and (iv) increasing the meaningful participation of poor women and other marginalized groups in decision-making processes, including through improved procedural and distributive justice so that the people most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change can engage in a fair process and receive a fair share of the benefits of adaptation efforts.

The CRPP will support countries to meet their commitments under the Paris Agreement, the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction (SFRDR), and the ADB’s Climate Change Strategy. The CRPP responds to COP26's goal of scaling up adaptation to protect communities and natural habitats, and mobilizing finance for adaptation at scale. It also responds to the call by the Global Commission on Adaptation and to the decision of COP 27 which calls for scaling up of arrangements in support of transformational action on adaptation. The CRPP is aligned with the recommendations of the United Nations' Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) and the Global Commission on Adaptation, as well as the recommendations of the World Bank’s “Global Commission on Adaptation,” which aims to spur financing for adaptation measures at the local level in developing countries. The CRPP is also aligned with the core recommendation of the United Nations’ Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change 2022: Impacts, Adaption and Vulnerability,” which highlights that high adaptation gap exists in low-income population group and that for adaptation efforts to be effective, they must be concentrated on the most vulnerable people. The CRPP will also support countries in implementing the Sendai Framework and calls for integrated, multi-sectoral solutions that address social inequalities and cut across systems.

The CRPP is designed as a flexible, demand-driven program with three components: (i) financing locally implemented adaptation; the CRPP will directly contribute to a number of CGIFS results as outlined above. The CRPP will also support countries in implementing the Sendai Framework for Disaster Warning Systems (DFWS), Food, Ecosystems, Infrastructure, and elements of locally led adaptation principles.

A programmatic approach for delivering pre-proposal adaptation solutions at scale. The CRPP is operationalized through the Community Resilience Financing Partnership Facility (CRFPF) which was established by ADB in August 2021 and comprises two separate but interlinked components: the CGIFS Fund (FF) focusing on financing locally implemented adaptation; and the CGIFS Investment Fund (IF) focusing on implementing local adaptation measures at scale. The CRPP is a facility of the CGIFS, which is managed by ADB. However, the CGIFS is not a separate legal entity, but a facility administered jointly with and by ADB. (See Figure 1)

The TF provides technical assistance and grant resources financed by development partners and secretariats to support countries in developing their CGIFS proposals. The CGIFS TF will focus on achieving three outputs: (i) Knowledge and action research on climate risk-informed pre-proposal adaptation solutions at scale; (ii) Development of a pipeline of CGIFS proposals for climate adaptation investments at community-level strengthened; and (iii) Inclusive and pro-poor adaptation investment projects identified and prepared. The TF includes a dedicated Gender Window which will focus on supporting countries in developing, implementing investments that explicitly strengthen the resilience of women, particularly poor women, to climate change impacts. The CGIFS TF will also support countries in empowering women to build their resilience. The TF will have a budget of US$27 million with roughly 15% earmarked for the Gender Window. To date, Government of United Kingdom, the Nordic Development Fund, and

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

The French Development Agency has confirmed financial support for the TF, among a total of US$68 million. The TF will be funded by GCF.

The IF will be a program to provide grant and loan financing to seven selected DMCs, namely Cambodia, Indonesia, Lao PDR, Pakistan, Papua New Guinea, Timor-Leste, and Vanuatu, to implement local adaptation projects that are designed to address the needs of the most vulnerable groups in their population. The IF will deliver three climate change related outputs that are the focus of this proposal (4) improved access to water resources; (5) improved food security and nutrition for the most vulnerable populations; and (6) climate resilient pro-poor livelihoods investments implemented, and (6) pro-poor climate adaptation and resilience projects. The IF will be implemented in accordance with the procedures established by the respective governments following ADB’s procedures. The GCF is requested to provide USD 403 million of grant financing for the IF, which will be used to finance the outputs of the IF of this program, while the ADB will provide USD 55 million of grant and loan financing.

The program will utilize GCF resources to co-finance 10-15 projects implemented under the IF only, for outputs 4, 5 and 6 as shown in Table 7 below.


## Figure 1: CRPP Program Architecture

| Outputs | Outcomes |
| --- | --- |
| 1 | Improved access to water resources |
| 2 | Reduced food insecurity and malnutrition |
| 3 | Improved food security and nutrition for the most vulnerable populations |
| 4 | Climate resilient pro-poor livelihoods investments implemented, and (6) pro-poor climate adaptation and resilience projects. |
| 5 | Increased adaptive capacity of local communities and ecosystems to such actions as: (i) climate adaptation and resilience; (ii) climate change and environmental management |
| 6 | Improved access to water resources |

Source: ADB

The GCF funds for the CRPP IF will be ring-fenced within the ADB GCF Trust Fund which was created pursuant to the Accreditation Master Agreement. Usage of GCF funds for individual projects will be based on criteria outlined in this proposal which will be included in a dedicated CRPP IF Implementation Guidance Note. The CRPP IF will provide grant and loan financing to seven DMCs, with grant and loan financing have been selected based on a combination of factors including: (i) high levels of vulnerability; (ii) high levels of poverty; (iii) high levels of exposure to climate change risks; (iv) investments targeted at the poorest populations; (v) advanced levels of decentralization that promotes local ownership, and; (vi) strong climate and environment management. Such actions as: (i) climate adaptation and resilience projects; (ii) climate change and environmental management partnership strategy for the pipeline of investments.

Value added of a regional program. The ADB recognizes that transformational change often benefits from a coordinated approach, and coordinated response that can be pursued through programs. By adopting a programmatic approach, it will be able to demonstrate stronger coordination and strategic alignment with GCF priorities. This will allow the CRPP IF to more effectively achieve its objectives of strengthening community resilience, and thereby allow delivering sustained climate results and impacts in the GCF result areas efficiently, effectively and at scale. The programmatic approach

will bring efficiency, higher implementation effectiveness, and facilitate shared learning and knowledge generation between participating countries and at a regional level, all of which will be captured in the program’s monitoring and reporting arrangements. The proposed inclusive governance structure of the CRPP TF will facilitate engagement with national partners and international development partners (that would be financing the CRPP TF) towards the successful implementation of the CRPP.

### 3. Climate Impacts Benefits

Climate resilient sustainable development. The anticipated impact of the CRPP is that climate resilience will be mainstreamed into development processes, thereby reducing vulnerability to climate risks from current development pathways that are failing to adequately respond to current and future climate risk. This will lead to a more equitable distribution of benefits and opportunities for all people, bringing about a paradigm shift towards climate resilient sustainable development. As such, the outcome of the CRPP is that the capacity of governments, civil society organizations, and other key stakeholders in seven countries in the Asia and Pacific region is strengthened. This outcome marks the pathway towards achieving the programs impact and will be the result of investment in adaptation options that build resilience to climate risks and enhance development outcomes. The CRPP TF will support processes for large-scale transformations and a core enabler of climate resilient development. Table 1 below provides an overview of the CRPP’s expected impacts, the pathways leading to these impacts, and the types of activities that would be financed by GCF under the CRPP IF and their associated outputs.

Unique programmatic concept for scaling up local adaptation measures in the region. The CRPP is unique in its ability to develop localized climate adaptation solutions tailored to the needs of the poor and vulnerable people living in seven countries across Asia and the Pacific, including women, children, youth, social development, gender equality, livelihoods and governance to deliver on adaptation outcomes. The CRPP IF will support the implementation of the GCF’s Adaptation Strategy 2019-2030 and its ambition of providing climate finance of USD80 billion between 2019-2030; of which USD34 billion will support adaptation*. As a multi-country program that comprises a varied but strong portfolio of projects, the CRPP IF will provide an enabling environment for adaptation and actions for adaptation measures through downstream investment in adaptation. The CRPP IF will also facilitate cross-learning and knowledge sharing on climate financing across different geographic and contexts. This programmatic approach allows for the acceleration of adaptation at scale by providing a platform to support the development, testing, scalability, replication and sustainability of adaptation solutions that are demonstrably effective over time. It will also provide an enabling environment for the identification and reduction of costs for key functions such as monitoring, evaluation and learning and gender mainstreaming.


*ADB has set targets of providing 80 billion in climate finance from its own resources over the period of 2019 to 2030 and of ensuring that 75% of this funding is directed towards adaptation. This target was announced at the GCF’s Fourth Replenishment Conference on 4 October, 2019. Achieving a Proportionate, Inclusive, Resilient, and Sustainable Asia and Pacific.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 8

## Investing in information technology systems to facilitate rapid information flows to smallholder farmers and other vulnerable groups in order to help them better understand how they can best manage their livelihoods in the face of climate change related shocks and stresses through tools such as index based insurance, early warning systems, etc. Such investments will also provide a financial buffer against the impacts of climate hazards. Note: GCF resources will not be used for direct payments to individuals.

## Climate resilient agriculture supply chains

### Investments in transport and logistics infrastructure, including cold chain storage and handling facilities, post-harvest processing and storage of perishable goods, as well as investments in research and development, innovation and capacity building for improved food security and nutrition. Such investments will help reduce the value of food as a result of climate change, promoting food security and resilient livelihoods.

## Pro-poor climate adaptation infrastructure investments

### Ecosystem based infrastructure

#### Investments to support services that promote improvements in adaptive capacity. Ecosystem-based infrastructure can provide multiple benefits by reducing exposure to climate hazards and thereby reducing the high mortality rates associated with extreme events such as floods and landslides, while also providing co-benefits for biodiversity conservation and ecosystem services.

### Flood and landslide protection infrastructure

#### Investments in flood and landslide risk reduction measures based on rural and urban communities can limit exposure to climate hazards and thereby reduce the high mortality rates associated with extreme events such as floods and landslides. Such investments will help reduce the vulnerabilities by reducing casualties associated with the impacts of extreme events.

### Multipurpose emergency shelters

#### Investments in adequately designed emergency shelters that are constructed close to where the most vulnerable people live, can provide a safe haven for them during extreme weather events. Such investments will help reduce the vulnerabilities by reducing casualties associated with the impacts of extreme events and supporting transitions in activity and urban and infrastructure systems.

# PROJECT-PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1. Climate Change Context

### 1. Climate Change Context

Climate risk is a key threat to sustainable development. Countries in Asia and the Pacific region are highly exposed to the damaging impacts of climate change. Prolonged heat waves, sea-level rise, changes in precipitation patterns, and increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events are major impacts on economies, natural and physical assets, and are compounding development challenges. Between 2014-2019, the region experienced 35 tropical cyclones, which caused USD$8 billion in damages and resulted in 67 deaths. The region experienced 41% of all flooding events and with a total of 1.5 million people affected, accounting for 93% of people affected by floods worldwide; followed by tropical cyclones accounting for 70% of the world's flood-related deaths.

### Climate risk is likely to intensify and occur both sooner and at a larger scale than previously anticipated

Climate change is already affecting many aspects of society and the environment, with impacts projected to alter the frequency, intensity, extent, duration, and timing of extreme weather events, and is likely to result in unprecedented extremes. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) Working Group I Fifth Assessment Report (AR5) concluded that there was high confidence that global warming reaching 1.5°C in the near-term would cause unavoidable impacts in many regions. The AR5 also stated with very high confidence that climate change will continue to impact all aspects of society and the environment, and that the risks associated with climate change are likely to increase by 20-30% by 2050; and in Laos PDR due to increased rainfall in its sub-basins, the Mekong River will experience a 1.4 m rise in water level by 2050.

## Indonesia (NDC ID: PDR 142; Pakistan 152; PNG 149; Timor Leste 111 and Vanuatu 142)

The higher the country score, the higher vulnerability to climate change and other global challenges in combination with its readiness to improve resilience.

### Adverse Impacts of Climate Change will be largely manifested at the local level. This is because, the underlying drivers of poverty and vulnerability, including socio-economic, ecological, institutional and governance are inherently context-specific and complex.

Adaptation solutions will be most effective when they are based on a deep understanding of the local context (e.g., granular knowledge on climate variability and change in weather patterns) and their interaction with factors contributing to exposure [such as land use changes] and vulnerability. This includes an understanding of how these factors interact within the community, and of how such interaction leads to adverse impacts on people, assets, and livelihoods.

### Poor and vulnerable populations face the brunt of climate impacts

Estimates suggest that climate change could push more than 100 million people globally in developing countries below the poverty line by 2030. In Indonesia, for example, a recent study found that between 5% to 7% of the population are already exposed back into poverty if food price surge or if farm incomes suddenly decline due to prolonged drought from El Niño events. Similarly, in Pakistan, an estimated 14 million people were pushed below the poverty line after being exposed to relatively low frequency and drought events and in Cambodia, a event occurring once every five years could push up to 20% of the population into poverty.

### Women and girls are impacted differently by climate change

Women typically rely on fewer and more vulnerable assets and livelihoods, depend on different sensitive sectors for livelihoods, have limited access to information, have limited decision-making power in households and communities, and are often left out from formal decision-making processes, which increases their vulnerability to climate impacts. Women also represent the majority of small-scale and subsistence farmers who are most vulnerable to the impacts of anthropogenic climate change.

Women in Asia and the Pacific represent the majority of small-scale and subsistence farmers who are most vulnerable to the impacts of anthropogenic climate change. In many cases, the effects of climate impact require the poor to adopt negative coping strategies including reducing education, health, and nutrition expenditures; increasing child labor; and reducing food consumption. These impacts could disrupt ecosystem services and lead to severe effects on livelihoods which in turn would affect human health, migration dynamics and the potential of conflict.

### Women and girls are impacted differently by climate change

Women typically rely on fewer and more vulnerable assets and livelihoods, depend on different sensitive sectors for livelihoods, have limited access to information, have limited decision-making power in households and communities, and are often left out from formal decision-making processes. All of these factors contribute to increased vulnerability and lower adaptive capacity, and are closely related to socially created gender functions, systemic discriminations, and the power imbalance between men and women.

The NDCs Data Gini Index summarizes a country’s sensitivity to climate change. Higher the country score, higher the vulnerability to climate change and other global challenges in combination with its readiness to improve resilience.

change and understand the structural causes for them, it is fundamental to refrain from categorizing women as instantly weak, passive receivers of protection and support. In the past, this approach has prevented women from being seen as active agents in their own lives and communities. Climate change adaptation measures designed on the assumption that women are powerless further expose them to climate change shocks and disasters, as well as hinders the realization of women's rights to full and equal participation and decision-making power. It is therefore important for adaptation planning processes to be gender-sensitive and inclusive, with a focus on the identification of appropriate adaptation measures and ensure that they are implemented effectively and efficiently. This can be achieved by involving women in all stages of climate change adaptation planning, which in turn can give them recognition within the community and promote their position in the family and wider society. It is also important to recognize the different vulnerabilities experienced by men and women between men and women in terms of their vulnerability and role of resilience building within communities are not equally reflected in existing adaptation policy and practice in CRPP countries, and this require specific attention.

The COVID-19 pandemic has further highlighted the need for building resilience of the poor and vulnerable population. The pandemic is having a disproportionate impact on the poor and the vulnerable, including women and children. People who live in poverty are more likely to be exposed to the virus as they migrate internally to deal with seasonal lean periods being forced to make difficult choices to deal with both food insecurity and health risks. Women are also disproportionately affected by the pandemic due to higher risk of losing jobs or income, dropping out of school, increased unpaid care work, and domestic violence. The pandemic has also led to a significant increase in poverty, with an estimated increase in the number of people living on less than $1.90 per day from 20% in 2018 to 34% in 2020.

Box 1: CRPP climate change context headline messages from the Working Group 2 contribution to the IPCC Sixth Assessment Report (AR6-WG2)

Headline message from AR6-WG2: IPCC AR6-WG2 contribution included:

- There is high confidence that warming of more than 1.5°C in the near-term, would cause unavoidable increases in multiple climate hazards and present multiple risks to ecosystems and human systems, with some impacts occurring at rates or magnitudes that are unprecedented or even times higher than currently observed.
- It also highlights that climate change will compound fundamental challenges around water and food insecurity poverty and inequality, and increased frequency and severity of extreme events. Even at 1.5°C and more warming, there is high confidence that climate change will continue to present a challenge for human and ecological systems in some places and for some people.
- There is high confidence that climate change will compound challenges associated with poverty, inequality, and marginalization linked to gender, ethnicity, low income or combinations thereof.

The vulnerability of communities and populations at different spatial levels is exacerbated by inequity and marginalization linked to gender, ethnicity, low income or combinations thereof.

Source: IPCC 2022[46]

## Context

The paragraphs below provide brief description of climate risk context of CRPP IF countries, with more details provided in Annex 2.1.

**Cambodia:** Cambodia is ranked 140th out of 181 countries in the ND-GAIN Index™, due a combination of a combination of high vulnerability and low capacity. The country has a tropical monsoon climate, with two distinct seasons, a rainy season from May to October and a dry season from November to April. Average temperatures are relatively stable throughout the year, ranging between 20°C and 35°C. Rainfall is highly variable (annual average rainfall ranges from about 1400 mm in the north to more than 2600 mm along the coast), most of which falls during the Monsoon. However, there is high annual variability due to the influence of the Monsoon and the El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO).


---
**Page 16**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 12

The country has a varied topography, with mountainous and hilly regions, the central plains, and a coastal plain along the Mekong River, which flows directly from north to south.

The Tonle Sap Lake, an outlet of the Mekong, is located in the northwest region, and covers almost 10% of the country's surface area during the monsoon peak. The central alluvial plains of the Mekong and Tonle Sap are characterized by a complex system of rivers, canals, and lakes that form an intricate hydrology with fluctuations with water levels between seasons, and are critical for agriculture and fisheries.

The climate in Cambodia is tropical monsoonal, with a distinct wet season from May to October. The country experiences extreme weather events such as floods, storms, and periodically droughts. These extreme weather events have increased over the past decades (EM-DAT, 2021); DESINVENTAR, 2021) with annual damages of US$100 to 250 million (UNDRR, 2019). The country is one of the most vulnerable countries in terms of climate change impacts. Cambodia ranks as one of the world's most flood exposed countries in the world and has experienced a number of major floods over recent years, including the 2013-14 droughts. During the 2016 El Niño drought event, 2.5 million people were affected, and three-quarters of the wet season paddy rice production experienced a loss of more than 25% in yield (World Bank, 2017). The country is also vulnerable to climate change impacts such as sea level rise, increased flooding, and droughts (UNDRR, 2019). These weather-related impacts particularly affect rural people because of the reliance on rainfed agriculture. In addition, the poor are more likely to be affected by these events than richer households in Cambodia have a high probability of falling into extreme poverty even when exposed to relatively small shocks.

Recent observations show that the climate of Cambodia is changing (UNDP, 2019; Cambodia 2nd NCA, 2015; World Bank, 2019). There is a clear warming trend, with 0.2°C per decade reported since the 1970s (World Bank, 2019). The observed increase in temperature has led to an increase of annual rainfall trends are more uncertain, with some areas experiencing increases and others decreases; temperatures are increasing of increasing variability and intensity. There is a general increase in precipitation over Cambodia for future periods of 5 to 2°C by mid-century (various multi-model ensemble values, for RCP2.6 and RCP8.5, relative to baseline 1986-2005 period; World Bank, 2020). There is also a general increase in the number of heavy precipitation events over Cambodia (UNDP, 2015). It is difficult to model rainfall changes due to the influence of Monsoon dynamics, but most models indicate an increase in the frequency and intensity of heavy precipitation events. There is more confidence from the climate models that heavy precipitation events will increase, which would lead to increased flooding risk (World Bank, 2020). This could lead to a significant increase in flood damage leading to increased floods (Ghosh et al., 2019), with greater areas of land and more people at risk of flooding (USAID, 2018; World Bank, 2020). The projected increase in heavy precipitation events will also lead to more frequent and severe flooding events (Deuring et al., 2019), which indicates large possible impacts on the fisheries sector. For the coastal areas, there is a general increase of sea level rise over Cambodia for future periods of 5 to 2°C by mid-century (various multi-model ensemble values, for RCP2.6 and RCP8.5, relative to baseline 1986-2005 period; World Bank, 2020). This could lead to increased flooding risk in all Southeast Asia, and Jim et al. (2018) also project large yield reductions for the country. These could be amplified by the possible increased annual likelihood of droughts* and/or by increases in heat and humidity levels (World Bank, 2020). The projected increase in sea level rise will lead to more frequent and severe impacts, driven by a projected increase in vector borne disease (WHO, 2015)). Finally, there will be impacts on the water resources sector. For example, the World Bank (2020) estimates that Cambodia’s agricultural sector estimate that climate change will reduce Cambodia's total GDP by 1.5% to 2.3% and 3.5% to 10% by 2050.

**Indonesia: Indonesia is ranked in the top-10th of countries in terms of natural hazard risk (59 out of 191) by the 2020 INFORM Risk Index. In particular, Indonesia has high exposure to floods, tropical cyclones and tsunamis. The country’s vulnerability to climate change is also high. According to the World Bank (2020), they estimated the costs of climate change in Indonesia could be more than 6% of GDP annually by 2100; more than double the global average loss, Despite high exposure to natural hazards, Indonesia rates remarkably lower on the INFORM Risk Index compared with other countries. This is due to a combination of factors including low population density and relatively low levels of economic consumption*. Prior to COVID-19, an estimated 61.8 million people, or 23.5% of the population, were living in poverty.

Poor and vulnerable households in rural and urban areas are particularly exposed to flood risk as they frequently reside on low lying lands prone to flooding and suffer from the lack of access to good quality infrastructure. The number of people living in coastal areas is expected to increase significantly, with an estimated annually affected by flooding in Indonesia is estimated at 1.5 million people and expected annual urban displacement due to sea level rise is projected to be around 200,000 people per year. This will amplify these existing flood conditions^13^and the risks are expected to increase with climate change, with large numbers of people living in coastal areas being at risk from increased storm surges and sea-level rise that will amplify coastal flood risk by 19-37% by 2050 (measured by Expected Annual Damage)^14^. Indonesia is particularly vulnerable as it has the largest number of people living on low lying land, with an estimated 6.8 million inhabiting the lower elevation coastal zone^15^. It is estimated that by 2030 around 0.5-8 million people could relocate to higher ground in response to sea level rise and increased flooding^16^. The number of people living in areas exposed to a 1-in-100 year flood risk will increase from 9.5 – 14 million by 2030. This is expected to result in an additional 800,000 people being displaced by 2100^17^. Estimates suggest that the number of Indonesians exposed to river flood risk will increase for all regions except Sumatra and Kalimantan. Even with a moderate climate change scenario, Indonesia is expected to experience an increase in flood risk across countries face an increase in the frequency of extreme river flows. What would historically have been a 1-in-10 year flow, could become a 1-in-50 year event or a 1-in-25 year event in most of South, Southeast, and East Asia^19^. In rural areas, the dependence on agriculture for livelihoods is of particular concern for climate change impacts. For example, in the case of droughts such as East Nusa Tenggara, rural households have a historically much higher dependence on agriculture (more than 80% as of 2010), and here food security related concerns such as malnutrition is very high^20^. The average area under rice cultivation per household in East Nusa Tenggara is approximately 3.6 ha^21^. Rice is a major staple in Indonesia and its production is vulnerable to changes in rainfall seasonality and weather conditions, with the country experiencing significant yield losses during El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO), where drier conditions are experienced during El Niño events and wetter conditions during La Niña events. The impact of climate change on rice production will be exacerbated by increased frequency and intensity drought with El Niño events, which are expected to increase in frequency and intensity through warmer global temperatures^22^. This is further compounded by the fact that Indonesia has a significant proportion of surplus water. By 2025, this is projected as approximately 20% and 31% by 2050^23^. Crop water demand will increase in response to climate change with an estimated increase in crop water demand for rice of around 40% between now and 2050. This will be further exacerbated by the fact that there will likely be insufficient water for the second growing season, March to June, when chillies and tobacco are grown^24^. Delayed rainfall can increase the risk of serious bio-cultivars with such events projected to occur more frequently in response to climate change. The impact on rice production is expected to be significant in the wet season from 18% today to 30-40% by 2050 (for the main rice producing areas of Java and Bali) due to increased frequency and intensity of droughts^25^. This will also have an impact on yields during dry season July) and 25% during wet season in September and October^26^. Further, USAID assumes that

### Laos People's Democratic Republic (Lao PDR): Lao PDR is a landlocked country in Southeast Asia. A large part of the country is mountainous, but there is a large central area, as well as low-lying alluvial plains along the Mekong River. It is ranked 142nd out of 181 countries on the ND-GAIN Index, and as the 36th most vulnerable country to climate change. The poverty rate in rural areas is about three times higher than urban poverty; it is almost three times higher in the central provinces where the capital city is located.

Lao PDR has a tropical monsoon climate. The rainy season between May and October, a cool dry season from November to February, and a short hot, dry season in March and April. The central region experiences high temperatures and high average annual rainfall (which is controlled by the El Niño Southern Oscillation). The country's vulnerability to climate change is driven by climate dynamics and the El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO). The combination of the monsoon climate, high vulnerability to ENSO events, and a lack of infrastructure makes Lao PDR particularly vulnerable. This is notably from floods, storms, landslides and droughts (UNDP, 2017). The whole country experiences regular flooding during the rainy season, with the central region being most affected by these natural disasters since 2009 (EM-DAT, 2021; DESINVENTAR, 2021). Typhoons Ketsana in 2009 and Hainsu in 2011 caused severe flooding. The country has experienced a series of droughts from 2015 to 2017, with flood disasters affecting over 600,000 people and led to losses of USD 371 million (UNDRR, 2019). There are also reports that the number of extreme weather events has increased in recent years. The loss of hundreds of thousands of rice, fruit crops (Sutton et al., 2019) including in the central region. Historical damage data indicates that annual expected losses range between 3.5% and 4.1 percent GDP (World Bank, 2017).

Disasters disproportionately impact the poor, and the rural poor are particularly vulnerable to ENSO-related shocks (Sutton et al., 2019). Rural areas experienced widespread food insecurity due to the reliance on rainfed agriculture in the country. The poor are also more likely to be exposed to extreme poverty even when exposed to relatively low frequency flood and drought events (AEB, 2017).

Recent observations show that the climate of Lao PDR is changing. There is a clear warming trend, with 0.1 to 0.3°C per decade since 1958 (World Bank, 2017). The country has experienced an increase in annual rainfall trends are more uncertain (Liao et al., 2013; World Bank, 2019, Westra et al., 2014), although there are trends of increasing variability and increasing extreme rainfall events.

Future climate change will exacerbate existing risks. The projected temperature increases for Lao PDR under the RCP8.5 scenario are similar to those from the global multi-model ensemble for RCP2.6 and RCP3.5 respectively, relative to a baseline 1986-2005 period (World Bank, 2020). World Bank (2020) also states that the country is projected to experience an increase in annual rainfall trends with most climate models project increases in annual rainfall by the mid-century (2050s) and there is more confidence in this projection than for temperature. The country's vulnerability to climate change will be driven by its geography, as well as its exposure to extreme weather events. Climate Modelling for the country’s rivers – from northern watershed, through the central region, and in the Mekong river basin – shows that the risk of flooding is likely to increase (UNDP, 2017). The country also faces a high risk of flooding, and higher value at risk (USD4,2019). These effects will be exacerbated by climate change. The country's vulnerability to droughts has been increasing in recent years (Dunne et al., 2013). There are also projected direct and indirect impacts of climate change on agriculture such as reduced yields in rice PDR (Sutton et al., 2019), noting these could be amplified by the possible increase in pests and diseases.

Pakistan: Ranked 18 out of 191 countries by the 2020 Inform Risk Index®. Pakistan faces some of the highest climate related disaster risk levels in the world. It has high exposure to flooding, including, riverine,

Flash floods, droughts as well as exposure to tropical cyclones, droughts and heatwaves. Disaster risk in Pakistan is also driven by its social vulnerability. Pakistan's vulnerability ranking (37%) is driven by its high rainfall variability and low adaptive capacity. Geographically, the country can be divided into three regions: **the Indus Basin**, which is a hotspot for storm surges and cyclones; **the coastal zone**; and **the mountainous region**. This area has very high vulnerability (37%), as it is particularly low-lying and flat, with around 4,750 km² below sea above mean sea level. Around 3 million people live in this area, which is projected to increase by a factor of six over the next century. The population could increase six-fold to reach 30 million people by 2060. The coastal provinces of Sind and Balochistan are particularly vulnerable as they have high vulnerability (45%) and are located on the coastlines of Pakistan's provinces and the highest urban-rural poverty gaps. Coastal erosion and storm surge waves are a major challenge in the coastal zone, degrading land quality and agricultural yields. Cyclones do arise more frequently than other tropical cyclone regions, with 10-20% of all tropical cyclones occurring over Pakistan; for example, a strong cyclone hit Pakistan in 1989, killing some 630 people, and there have been major storm surges caused by cyclones. For example, Cyclone Yemina had significant impacts on lives and property.

Recent observations show that the climate of Pakistan and Sind region are changing already and many of the issues described above are likely to intensify due to climate change, affecting many marginal and dependent communities. The average temperature has increased by 0.5°C since 1961 (compared with a global average increase of 0.5-1°C of warming), and higher numbers of heatwaves and hot days.

Data from the World Bank show that Pakistan experienced an increase in the number of heatwave days per year, compared to the global average. By mid-century (2060) a further rise of 1.5°C to 2.5°C (central estimate), relative to a baseline period of 1986-2005, is projected for Pakistan. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change sources report that rainfall in the coastal area has declined by 10–15% since the 1960s. However, heavy rainfall events have increased over the past decades and are expected to increase further. There have been experiences highlighting the rise of frequency and intensity of flood and drought events.

The observed changes at Karachi indicating an increasing sea level rate of about 1 mm/yr during the 20th century. Under low emission scenarios, it is likely that the coastal areas will experience significant increases in sea levels over the next century. Recent analysis has also found that climate change could lead to a rise in sea levels by up to 35 cm above baseline period (medium confidence). The projections that the average intensity of tropical cyclones, the proportion of Category 4 and 5 tropical cyclones will increase significantly with an increase in the frequency of tropical storms. This combined with rising mean sea levels will contribute to a significant increase in storm surge events. These changes are likely also be exacerbated by an increase in the average intensity, magnitude of storm surge and precipitation rates of tropical cyclones.

These changes in climate are likely to have a significant impact on water resource availability, heat stress, land degradation, affecting agricultural effects and rural agrarian livelihoods, in a country where Agriculture employs 42.5% of Pakistan's workforce and contributes 21% to gross domestic product (GDP). The majority of the population lives in rural areas, with around two-thirds living on less than $2/day. A large proportion of small-scale farmers (20%), landless farmers (10%) and agricultural labourers (12%). This is a particularly vulnerable group as they are often unable to adapt to climate change. The report by the UK Met Office (2014) estimated that without adaptation, around one million people will face coastal flooding in Pakistan over the next 50 years. Without intervention, saltwater intrusion from sea level rise will also be exacerbated and could lead to a significant increase in salinity levels.

## B

### Timor-Leste: Floods, droughts, landslides and cyclones are major natural hazards in Timor-Leste.

According to the 2020 World Risk Report, in terms of exposure and vulnerability to natural hazards, Timor-Leste ranks as position number one. The main reasons for this ranking include high population density, lack adaptive capacity and lack coping capacity[16]. In terms of climate disruptions and adaptation, Timor-Leste is ranked 8th out of the 20 countries with the highest exposure.

The overall country score of 43.8 ranks Timor-Leste as position number 111 - the 47th most vulnerable, and the 59th least prepared for climate change impacts[16]. The main drivers of vulnerability are droughts both occurring during periods of extreme rainfall; significant flood events were recorded in 2001, 2003, 2006, 2013, and 2018. Floods also cause regular flooding in Timor-Leste. As a particular case study, the major drought from 2015-2017 which caused sharp reductions in agricultural yields, with a decrease of 40% in maize production[16]. The country is highly dependent on agriculture and livestock; 98% of food is planted in the country, with 50% of households reporting loss of livestock, and 50% suffering from food insecurity. In addition to this, the country has a high population density (247 people per km²) and approximately 90% of the population are reliant on the agriculture sector[16]. Most agriculture is subsistence or semi-commercial in nature, with limited access to irrigation systems due to low productivity growth and unpredictable rainfall coupled with extreme climate events[16].

Staple crops in Timor-Leste are sensitive to rising temperatures and the impacts associated with extreme rainfall, changes in the seasonality of rainfall, seawater inundation and salination of coastal aquifers. The country is also highly vulnerable to droughts which are exacerbated by a lack of water storage and amount of rainfall, and increasing variability. The supply of water (particularly water scarcity during the dry season) will be affected by climate change impacts on rainfall patterns[16]. The intensity and duration of livelihood conditions and food insecurity issues will be affected with both increases in extreme events, as well as changes to seasonal patterns. For example, the months of May-June are typically the driest months of Jun-Sep, largely corresponding to the current dry season, are likely to become even drier, and there is a risk that the wet season may be delayed or shortened[16]. The intensity of cyclones in the rainy season may be delayed, with increased rainfall occurring during a shorter rainy season[16]. The intensity of cyclones has been increasing over the past 50 years, with an average increase of 2.3% per decade since the beginning of the century. Under RCP4.5 there is a clear signal of increased heavy rainfall, with an average +14.5% increase in the number of days exceeding the 99th percentile by mid-century, and a 38.6% increase in days for which precipitation exceeds the 99th percentile[16].

The El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) exerts a strong influence over rainfall variability in Timor-Leste. El Niño conditions bring drier conditions and shorter, delayed wet seasons, often causing droughts, while La Niña

**B**

Nino conditions cause higher rainfall even in the dry season and are linked to higher tropical cyclone activity.

For the period 1950-1999, DMI recorded its low record low rainfall totals during the El Niño events of 1982–83 and 1997–98, and some provisionally for 2014. A significant increase in droughts during El Nino years is false starts to the rainy season, where the initial onset of rains is followed by an abnormal dry period before the season relays starts. These false starts are more common with La Niña events than El Niño events.

For example in Manusatu, farmers reported frequent false starts during previous El Niño events, and having to pay for irrigation water. The 2014–15 El Niño event was no different. In the dry season of 2013/14, rainfall was down by 20% in the absence of adaption, noting that much of this loss could be offset by supporting agricultural extension services and providing irrigation water to farmers.

The impact of extreme events is also not captured well under current climate models. This is because they do not attempt to capture any increase in losses from extreme events. The evolution of ENSO under climate change is still poorly understood, and the frequency and intensity of extreme events are difficult to predict for individual countries. Although changes in the ENSO cycle are still not well captured by climate models, the latest research indicates that El Niño events may become both more frequent and more severe over the course of the century.

## Vanuatu: Disasters resulting from natural hazards are a very significant contributor to issues of social deprivation and impede economic development in Vanuatu, costing on average an estimated 6% of GDP each year. The country is highly vulnerable to climate change due to its small size, low-lying coastal context, and precarious natural resource base mean it is amongst the most vulnerable nations to climate change.

The island nation of Vanuatu has a population of around 250,000 people living in close proximity with exposure and vulnerability to the impact of flood, tropical cyclones, and storm surge which can strike at any time. The country consists of over 80 islands, with the largest being Efate Island, which is home to the capital city Port Vila.

According to its Second National Communication to the UNFCCC around 20-30 cyclonic passes on Vanuatu each year, and in recent years, there has been an increase in the frequency of category 5 tropical storms. Vanuatu killing 11 people, destroying or damaging over 17,000 buildings and displacing thousands.

The economic damages associated with the incident were estimated to be equivalent around 64% of GDP.

The economy of Vanuatu, like surrounding island states, is based predominantly on the service sector (67% of GDP value added), followed by agriculture (22%) and industry (1%). However, there is significant local dependence on imports for food security. The country has a population of around 250,000 people.

Fishing – both artisanal (in which a large proportion of the population participates) and commercial - and tourism are also important sectors in Vanuatu’s economy. The country has a high level of economic dependence on imports for food security equivalent to over US$50 per capita in 2111 given its high vulnerability to climate change.

The impact of extreme events such as cyclones, storm surges, and floods is significant for agriculture and on tourism for livelihoods means that impact of extreme climate events in Vanuatu can be devastating. The frequency and intensity of these extreme events are expected to increase due to sea-level rise which will cause devastating damage to agricultural products and known climate risks include the action of sea level rise to enhance the damage caused by cyclone-induced storm surges, and the possibility of increased wind speeds associated with tropical cyclones.

The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) has projected that global mean temperature across the globe points to a general trend of reduced tropical frequency but increased intensity and duration. The IPCC also projects an increase in the number of extreme weather events such as droughts, encroachment on coastal areas, but due to the projected increase in the frequency of extreme sea-level events, it is expected that the impact of these events will be more severe than previously thought.

The projections for sea level rise are associated with increased risks of flooding, loss

**B**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND WATER INTRUSION AND FOOD SECURITY

## Proposed Interventions to Scale Up Adaptation Solutions Targeted at the Poor

### Box 2: The CRPP's approach directly responds to the IPCC's call for integrated adaptation and poverty reduction solutions that address social inequities, differentiates responses based on climate risk and cut across sectors, and enables inclusive decision-making processes.

The CRPP proposes a forward-looking approach towards implementing adaptation solutions through large-scale investment programs targeted at poor communities. The CRPP calls for integrated multi-sectoral solutions that address social inequities, differentiate responses based on climate risk and cut across sectors, and enable inclusive decision-making processes. This approach is designed to enhance adaptation in multiple sectors. There is also high confidence that climate resilient development is enabled when poverty reduction, food security, water security, energy access, health, education, gender equality, risk reduction, equity and justice, and when decisions making processes, finance and actions are integrated across governance levels, sectors and timelines.

The table provides an overview of the specific climate hazards and risks that are expected to interact with factors contributing to exposure and vulnerability cause the specific risk/impact related activities will address.

3. Related Interventions

The paragraphs below describe selected ongoing climate change adaptation projects in the target countries, financed by the Adaptation Fund, GEF and other Environment Facility (GEF) contributing partners. These proposed under CRPP and/or have a potential to be supported by projects financed by the CRPP. Any investments under the CRPP directed towards the replication and scaling up of these interventions will be subject to the availability of funds, project readiness, and feasibility studies that take into account the content specific nature of adaptation interventions.

**Cambodia:**

The trend of adaptation investments in Cambodia toward large-scale funds reflects the overall focus on climate change adaptation at national level. The government has been adapting the agricultural sector for protecting livelihoods and improving food security and income generation through the implementation of the Climate Change Adaptation Strategy, the Livelihood Chains Sector Project and Public-Private-Sector Partnerships for Ecologically-Sound Agriculture and Renewable Energy (P3E). In addition to these large-scale projects, several small-scale projects are also being implemented. These include the USAID-funded Climate Resilient Livelihoods in Rice-Based Communities in the Tonle Sap Region and the Adaptation Fund's recently completed project on Enhancing Climate Resilience of Rural Communities Living in Proximity to the Tonle Sap Lake. The government has been implementing a number of projects through enhancing the resilience and productivity of target crops, and increasing agricultural competitiveness by improving access to markets and services. These investments have targeted climate resilient rural livelihoods activities in the poorest and highest climate risk populations in the country. However, there is still a need for further investment in small-scale adaptation projects, while filling in the funding deficits. Investments that have an explicit and targeted focus on women's empowerment are also needed to ensure that the benefits of investments from agriculture has been greater for men than for women, but this is not necessarily reflected adaptation interventions.

**Indonesia:**

The Adaptation Fund is financing three projects in Indonesia, two of which are highly targeted towards vulnerable populations. These include the Pekalongan City Resilience Project (a Sustainable Integrated Watershed Governance in Indigenous People of Armataq Agung Customary Area and a Community-Based Climate Change Adaptation Project for the Pekalongan Ecosystem) and the third, Sakeeng-Saving-Sustaining-Turning Resilience: 3S Approach to Building Climate Resilience in the Central Region of East Java Province. The City of Pekalongan has an urban focus. Investments in Sakeeng reflect the emerging realization that there is a clear threat posed by climate change to poor rural populations in remote and isolated parts of the country that do not have access to basic services, such as water supply and sanitation. In contrast, there is a clear recognition that local and context specific solutions are called for in such an environment. The investments under Sakeeng will be implemented through community-based adaptation. The Adaptation Fund in Indonesia represent only a small fraction of the local-level adaptation financing needed to address climate change impacts on vulnerable populations. However, 10% of the population have a power purchasing parity of USD$1.9 a day or less. The poorest and most vulnerable populations are those who live in remote areas with poor access to services and infrastructure. There is an urgent need for investments in projects that have the capacity to reach a significant number of people living with high climate vulnerability, while also ensuring that these investments are implemented through adaptation financing directly to the end users. More specifically, the Pekalongan City Resilience Project, which seeks to address urban flood risk, presents an opportunity for replication via the transfer of

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 PAGE 29

## B

### Knowledge capacity to other cities on the Island of Java, thus strengthening the ability to generate transformational change through delivering complimentary investments.

#### Lao PDR.
Current climate change adaptation financing in Lao PDR from large scale adaptation funds is targeted towards activities which generally focus on urban and peri-urban settings, such as the GCF funded project “Enhancing Climate Change Resilience of Urban Areas in Vientiane” (ECRUA). The Integrated Water Resource Management and Ecosystem-based Adaptation in the Xe Bang Fier River Basin Project (IWRM) is also a large scale adaptation fund that focuses on climate change resilience of the most vulnerable rural and emerging urban human settlements in Lao PDR. These projects are important, but they do not address the needs of upland communities. The Climate Smart Agriculture alternatives for upland production systems in Lao PDR are focused in the northern highlands where poverty is almost three times higher than in urban areas. Furthermore, the poverty head count rate in the central provinces is 20% compared to only 15% in urban areas. With the current focus of adaptation investment either in urban settings or in the rural northern highlands there is the potential of neglecting large swaths of important agricultural communities in the central provinces where poverty and vulnerability are also very high, and where the impacts of climate change due to climate change. However, there is a clear opportunity to capitalize on the nascent national framework for adaptation in Lao PDR to address these gaps by scaling up existing initiatives that can replicate and scale up similar initiatives to other geographies and sectors with high climate risk. Some examples include: (i) the use of agroforestry systems as part of integrated land management, wetlands for flood protection and watershed management in rural areas, by expanding on existing activities such as those supported under the GCF funded project “Enhancing Climate Change Resilience of Livelihoods and Agriculture sector”. This can be complemented by replacing climate smart agriculture activities with agroforestry systems. (ii) The use of community-based natural resource management to learn to, the funder, central provinces that are highly exposed and vulnerable to the impacts of climate change.

### Pakistan.
There is an identifiable gap in large scale climate adaptation investments in Pakistan that remain in coastal zones, and specifically protecting living and rural livelihoods from extreme climate events. Much of the focus of investment from large-scale adaptation funds in Pakistan has been in the provinces of Sindh and Balochistan where there are high levels of poverty and vulnerability to climate change, with a focus on urban climate change resilience to water scarcity, caused by floods and droughts in RaviPunjab and Havelian. However, there is also an urgent need for investments that can protect coastal livelihoods in the coastal zones of the Indus River Delta. Interventions in Pakistan will not only fill a funding gap in terms of geographical distribution, but also directly complement existing investments by GCF on climate change adaptation in Pakistan. These include the GCF funded project “Enhancing Climate Change Resilience of Urban Areas in Vientiane” (ECRUA) and the provision of climate information services for livelihood decision-making (from federal to farm level). Based on the above, it is recommended that the proposed project should be supported by the Pakistan’s 90% coastal is located in the provinces of Sindh and Balochistan, which have the highest number of people living in poverty. The GCF has already provided a large scale adaptation fund approved GCF funding for the Recharge Pakistan: Building Pakistan’s resilience to climate change project. This project will provide examples of community-based natural-based solutions which can be potentially scaled up in other parts of the country.

### Papua New Guinea.
Papua New Guinea is yet to receive adaptation funding from GCF, although the recently developed National Adaptation Plan process was supported by the GCF Readiness and Preparatory Support Programme. The programmatic large-scale approach proposed by the CRPP would be a good fit for PNG as it has been designed with a focus on climate change resilience and considered high climate risk, and where over 85% of the population live in rural areas where access to

B

markets, services and income-generating opportunities is limited.[1] There is a great deal of scope to scale up transfer and technology and capabilities from investments in other large-scale adaptation funding mechanisms such as the Green Climate Fund, which has already provided support for climate change-related floods on the North Coast and Islands region of Papua New Guinea, and Adaptation of Small-Scale Agriculture for Improved Food Security of Resilient Communities in Papua New Guinea. These projects have demonstrated that smallholder farmers can be supported to adapt to climate change in Western Highlands Provinces for Biodiversity, Human Livelihoods and Well-being, which respectively focus on the adaptation of small-scale agriculture, forest conservation and biodiversity protection, climate resilient crop varieties, and improved information and decision-support systems for livelihoods. Climate-smart investments in these areas can provide a strong foundation to support rural communities and provide the potential for delivering resilient livelihood interventions at scale and capitalise on the smaller-scale successes that have been achieved.

The Timor-Leste Project is one such example of a climate change adaptation financing project forward for building the adaptive capacity of subsistence and small-scale farmers that make up over 70% of the population in the coastal provinces of East and West Timor. The country has a high level of national poverty (USD 1.90 PPP/day)[2] gender inequality at extremely high levels, with the country ranking 158 out of 169 countries on the Gender Inequality Index[3], and one of the most culturally and geographically diverse in Southeast Asia. The Timor-Leste Project is a sectoral approach that focuses investment in adaptation solutions that are explicitly pro-poor and gender empowering and that are driven by local communities.

Timor-Leste: Climate change adaptation financing by large scale funds to date in Timor-Leste has leaned towards the installation of localized protective measures for rural infrastructure (GCF funded Solar Water Pumps, GEF funded Coastal Protection Works) and small-scale climate-smart agriculture and larger scale protective works (GEF Funded Strengthening Community Resilience to Climate Induced Diversification, and the Timor-Leste Project). The Timor-Leste Project is a sectoral approach that focuses on diversification, and climate resilient value chain development would balance the portfolio of investments in the country. This will provide a more sustainable livelihood for rural communities and address and address high vulnerability by climate change to the country’s important agricultural sectors where over 80% of the population rely as a source of income and for their food security. This need has been acknowledged with the inclusion of agriculture, fisheries and forestry in the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) development through progressive rural communities in Timor-Leste Project. This is a sector that will require significant investment to build adaptive capacity at scale, but also requires access to financial resources as part of a pragmatic approach, also leaves open the opportunity to channel further investments into other sectors and areas where there are high levels of vulnerability.

Vanuatu: Due to its status as a small island developing state, with high climate risk. Vanuatu is a prime example of how climate change adaptation financing can be used to address the impacts of Climate Change in the Coastal Zone in Vanuatu, which is due to start phase II implementation with GCF co-financing. These investments represent large contributions to financing of climate risk management activities and are expected to have significant benefits for the country by strengthening national and local government frameworks for climate risk reduction. To date, GCF contributed USD 10 million to the Vanuatu Climate Change Adaptation Project (VCCAP) which supports the country through the Climate Information Services for Resilient Development in Vanuatu project. The current phase of VCCAP is expected to be completed by mid-2018 and will focus on extending the application of the enhanced CEM mechanisms and products from on-going GCF funded projects, such as the Pacific Adaptation Technical Assistance (PATA) Project, which has been developed by developing national level mechanisms that can support the delivery of targeted and timely adaptive finance. The PATA project is a key example of how climate change adaptation financing can be used to adapt will enable those people that are on fragile livelihoods that are frequently and increasingly disrupted by climate related shock and stresses to take steps now before and after the impact of these shocks and stresses, in order to build their resilience and increase their income potential and take advantage of new opportunities specific to their individual needs.

### B.2 (a) Theory of Change Narrative and Diagram

#### I. CRPP Program and Objectives

The CRPP aims to build the resilience of poor and vulnerable people to the impacts of climate change in 7 countries in Asia and the Pacific region. This is considered a critical step towards achieving the desired impact of the GCF’s adaptation portfolio, which is to reduce poverty and vulnerability at scale.

#### II. Goal Statement: If the CRPP Trust Fund and the CRPP Investment Fund are established and properly implemented, then the climate resilience of poor and vulnerable people in Asia and the Pacific region will be more resilient and sustainable because:

1. The enabling environment for knowledge, finance, and implementation of pro-poor local adaptation interventions is strengthened at scale.
2. Food security of poor and vulnerable communities is increased.
3. Exposure of poor and vulnerable people to climate shocks and stresses is reduced.

The CRPP’s anticipated impact is closely aligned with the GCF adaptation paradigm objective of reducing poverty and vulnerability at scale, and the CRPP’s outcomes encompass all four of the GCF’s adaptation result areas.

#### III. 2. Barriers to Strengthening the Climate Resilience of Poor and Vulnerable People in Asia and the Pacific Region

Research that has formed the basis of the program design has highlighted that recognizing the climate resilience needs of poor and vulnerable people in Asia and the Pacific region requires large-scale adaptive interventions that are explicitly targeted at the poor, promote empowerment, especially of women and girls, and that focus on local level solutions. In the absence of such interventions, future climate impacts (occurring under the RCP 8.5 scenario) from climate change will result in increased poverty, hunger, growth and sustainable development in Asia and the Pacific region.

Assessments and consultations undertaken by the CRPP have identified a number of barriers to implementing such interventions, including:

1. Lack of suitable, locally relevant, and accessible climate information to inform resilience investments.
2. Limited access to finance for adaptation at local level.
3. Weak coordination between sectors.
4. Weak threat coordination within and across sectors.

These barriers are common to all seven CRPP countries.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 32

It is recognized that the current systems for social protection are not yet fully prepared to deal with new trends and adequately calls for adaptive systems that includes early warning systems.(1) There is also a major knowledge gap, particularly in terms of regular climate monitoring planning and analysis including how best using data, facilitating and scale-up good practice. For example, the Vanuatu Framework for Climate Services developed in 2016 recognize the need to build capacity at all levels of government and civil society organizations to address the need to improve the capacities of both providers and receivers and development of platforms.(2)

### Limited access to adaptation finance for implementing local solutions at scale

The governance structure of climate change adaptation finance is often not aligned with where the measures required are not just incremental adaptation (e.g., to make infrastructure resilient), but transformative adaptation that requires a significant shift in policy, planning, and implementation. In such cases, there may be limited access to finance for implementing local solutions at scale. This is because stakeholders and implemented and monitored by local communities. Moreover, there is a typical preference for incremental adaptation measures that are easier to implement, align with national priorities, yield assured near-term benefits, and generate positive streams of direct or indirect incomes. Investments in climate adaptation are further stymied in some cases by their nature as a public good, which means that the private sector may not be willing to invest unless there is a clear benefit to downstream communities and neighbouring countries. Thus, there is a need to address this issue through innovative financing mechanisms that can provide long-term, reliable, sustainable and predictable long-term finance, it will not be possible for governments to deliver the scale of adaptation required without access to adequate funding. Without such funding, even if climate change adaptation is identified as a core priority in national development plans, there may still be limited progress.

### Implementation of Climate Change Policy of Pakistan notes that financing for climate change adaptation is often insufficient and does not always align with the priorities set out by policymakers actions.

The Vanuatu Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction Policy (2016-2030) recognizes that money is one of the most vulnerable resources in climate change and disaster risk management. The COVID-19 pandemic has further increased pressure on domestic finances, thereby making it even more difficult to finance transformative adaptation.

### Weak institutional and technical capacity to mainstream climate adaptation in policy and planning and implementing adaptation solutions

Factors such as inappropriate institutional architecture, lack of coordination between different levels of government and other stakeholders (including local private sector and civil society organizations) lead to policy failures that make a country vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. The lack of institutional capacity has been identified as one of the main challenges at national /local regional level in planning and implementing adaptation solutions. This is because there are limited resources available for its four priority actions.(16) While the political will for adaptation exists and a policy and legal framework is often in place, there is typically limited institutional capacity to steer pro-poor development as a climate change adaptation strategy. The lack of institutional capacity is also reflected by the way in which policies make and planners. While the National Adaptation Planning process is building capacity at all levels of government and civil society organizations, it is important that this capacity-building is not only focused on the technical aspects but also includes the political and social aspects of pro-poor policy, planning and budgeting, including decisions on fiscal transfers, which will in turn help to ensure that adaptation solutions are designed with a focus on poverty reduction in the face of increasing climate shocks and stresses. There is also limited knowledge on innovative adaptation solutions at all levels.

### Negative perceptions - risk aversion around community-level actions

Under-investment in climate adaptation at the local level is reinforced by a typical preference for the use of limited public resources to finance national level projects that aligns with the existing political economy (i.e. so as to support existing money, budgets and institutions). This means that there are often few opportunities to capture economic, social and environmental co-benefits that local adaptation generates, such as alleviating poverty, improving livelihoods, and enhancing resilience. As a result, many of these benefits are less well-targeted national interventions, rarely identified and captured to encourage greater

#### 3. Pathways of Change

The CRPP theory of change described in this section represents the theory that underpins the design of the proposal and sets out the basis for the classification of the activities and outputs that will help to overcome the barriers identified above and achieve the desired outcome and impact. The theory of change postulates that in order to achieve the three key program objectives, which are: (i) strengthening the enabling environment for promoting and implementing pro-poor local adaptation solutions at scale; (ii) increasing access to climate information and knowledge products on climate risk informed pro-poor local solutions; and (iii) carrying out policy dialogues at sub-national, national and regional level, bringing key stakeholders together in a structured manner to discuss the barriers identified above and identify opportunities for action, an opportunity for local level actors including local governments, communities and civil society.

**Pathway 1:** Strengthening the enabling environment for promoting and implementing pro-poor local adaptation solutions at scale. The objective of Outcome 1 is to overcome the current barriers related to (i) negative perceptions and risk aversion around community-level actions; (ii) weak in-country capacity to design, implement and monitor adaptation solutions; and (iii) lack of suitable, timely relevant, and accessible climate information to inform resilience investments targeted at the poor. The pathway leads to the achievement of Outcome 1: the enabling environment for promoting and implementing pro-poor local adaptation solutions is strengthened.

**Output 1:** Knowledge and action research on climate risk informed pro-poor community-level solutions strengthened.
- Bringing together intergovernmental research organisations, national academic institutions, grassroots community development organisations, national and local governments, financial institutions, and ADB to develop a common understanding of the barriers identified above and the creation of a wide range of evidence and knowledge products on climate risk informed pro-poor local solutions. This will increase awareness and understanding among national and local decision-makers.
- Carrying out policy dialogues at sub-national, national and regional level, bringing key stakeholders together in a structured manner to discuss the barriers identified above and identify opportunities for action.

By ensuring that these objectives are met, the CRPP will contribute to transformational change through bringing about changes in culture and decision-making, reaching beneficiaries on a large scale, and strengthening adaptation of sustainability at the local level.
