### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0: PAGE 34

#### Output 2: Institutional and community capacity to develop and deliver climate adaptation investments at the community-level strengthened.

- **Developing a comprehensive climate risk assessment (covering hazard, exposure and vulnerability) and how these interact and evolve with changing socioeconomic conditions** and decision support tools for use in development planning and implementation.
- **Strengthening decision-making processes that are informed by climate information**, including building capacity of local adaptation decision-makers. Capacity building and increased access to climate information will also be supported through the GCF’s regional centers.
- **Building capacity of local governments and communities** to access international climate finance for adaptation projects. This will support the channeling of international climate finance to the local level so that it can be used effectively in adaptation.

It is expected that the proposed interventions under the outputs presented above will combine to support changing the culture and decision-making related to adaptation investments at the local level, including increasing the use of climate information in development planning. This change in decision-making is anticipated to result in new and improved climate risk informed policies, budgets and programming, and a more effective allocation of resources for adaptation. The pathway that this output will follow is one that directly and indirectly influence public and private investment in local level pro-adaptation. This represents a shift from the current situation where there are limited opportunities for large-scale investment and sustainable financing of local level adaptation measures, and thus contribute to transformational change.

Supporting assumptions are that (i) the research and knowledge products address critical gaps in understanding and awareness; and (ii) the pathways are inclusive: pro-local level adaptation. The objective is to overcome the barriers to scaling up adaptation solutions at scale by mobilising large-scale domestic and international resources for pro-adaptation at the local level of CRFP countries. A lack of suitable, locally relevant and accessible climate information to inform decision-making is a barrier to scaling up adaptation solutions at scale.

Investments in improving CIRS and integrating them into pro-poor climate financing decision-making processes will be supported through this output. This will contribute directly to the achievement of Outcome 2: Pro-poor adaptation finance mobilized at scale. The path towards achieving this outcome and overcoming the above stated barriers includes implementing the following activities under two outputs.

#### Pathway 2: Mobilising pro-poor adaptation finance at scale

The objective of this pathway is to overcome the barriers to scaling up adaptation solutions at scale by mobilizing large-scale domestic and international resources for pro-adaptation at the local level in CRFP countries. A lack of suitable, locally relevant and accessible climate information to inform decision-making is a barrier to scaling up adaptation solutions at scale.

#### Output 3: Investments supporting inclusive pro-poor, community-level adaptation prepared.

This output focuses on mobilizing international climate finance, and will:

- Provide technical assistance to SMDs to develop new large-scale investment projects that implement adaptation solutions. This support may include providing TA resources for climate risk assessments as part of investment project preparation and feasibility studies.
- Provide technical assistance to SMDs to identify potential co-financing partners, including ADB’s, resources for investing in adaptation solutions that benefit poor and vulnerable women and men.

# Output 4: Information and systems for delivering applied climate-risk informed investments at scale improved.

This output focuses on mobilizing domestic financing, and will:
- Improve national policies, plans and distribution mechanisms related to social protection programs and fiscal decentralization to ensure that they are responsive to climate change concerns. By strengthening the capacity of local governments to implement adaptation measures, this will lead to the channelling of public funds for adaptation measures that can be targeted locally and meet local adaptive needs.
- Support the enhancement of national climate information services (CIS) to meet the information needs of policy makers, planners, and practitioners at all levels. The CIS should provide information such as adaptive social protection and climate risk informed decentralized governance processes will need to be informed by climate risk information, and thus require improved climate information services. This will also support the development of a national climate information service that provides suitable, locally relevant, and accessible climate information to inform resilience investments targeted at all levels.

It is expected that the interventions described above will result in increased investment from international financial institutions in local level pro-poor climate change adaptation in CRPP countries, and increased investment in domestic financing for climate change adaptation. The pathways are designed to achieve transformative change through (i) creating sustainable and predictable financing through improved mechanisms for mobilizing resources at all levels of government and civil society; and (ii) create systemic change through improving the participation of women and men in adaptation related decision-making (climate risk informed decentralization). Outcome 1 and 2 are closely linked and interrelated as they both aim to increase investment in climate change adaptation. The pathways will support pro-poor local adaptation measures at scale in CRPP countries, the increasing political will and institutional capacity for implementing climate change adaptation.

Supporting assumptions are that (i) a sufficient number of appropriate large-scale investment projects for implementing adaptation measures at the local level are identified and; (ii) improving CIS systems and their translation into actionable information is supported. The pathways also assume that there are reforms such as social protection systems and decentralization processes. The key potential risk in this pathway is that COP21 will not result in a significant increase in public funding and spending capabilities that results in reduced availability of domestic and international financing for adaptation.

## Pathway 3: Implementing large-scale pro-poor adaptation solutions to build long-term capacity to deal with climate shocks and stress. The objective of this pathway is to implement local adaptation interventions at scale, which will reduce the vulnerability of poor people to climate risks by reducing their risk to adapt to multiple, long term and future climate risks and to learn and adjust in the extreme of adverse events. This pathway also aims to increase the capacity of governments and civil society for implementing local solutions at scale. The pathway leads to the achievement of two of the program's outcomes: (i) increased investment in pro-poor adaptation; and (ii) improved governance.

Exposure of poor and vulnerable people to climate shocks and stress is reduced. The path towards achieving this outcome involves overcoming the above stated barriers includes implementing the following activities under two outputs.


## Output 5: Climate resilient pro-poor livelihoods investments implemented.

- Implement large-scale investment projects that support community-based adaptation actions that are targeted at increasing resilience of poor people to climate change, agricultural systems and supply chains and information technology and risk management services, making their livelihoods more resilient.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 36

## Implement large-scale investments projects that support community-based adaptation actions that are targeted at the poor and vulnerable population to reduce the likelihood of the occurrence and/or the magnitude of climate-related hazards via the installation of natural or man-made barriers or ecosystem-based approaches, such as mangroves, coral reefs, and sea walls, and reducing crop yields and creaping hazards such as sea level rise.

It is expected that by implementing climate resident pre-poverty livelihoods investments, poor and vulnerable people will have access to new technologies and skills to apply and adopt new resilient livelihood techniques and skills that will improve their access to safe and nutritious food despite the impacts of climate change. This will be achieved through the implementation of a range of interventions including but not limited to infrastructure investments; governments and communities themselves will have access to financing for the investment in these projects, which will reduce poverty and vulnerability and increase resilience at scale thus reducing the exposure of poor and vulnerable people to climate shocks and stresses. By using new or existing national programs that are tailored to local circumstances and can be implemented at a national level, the CRPPB can contribute to transformational change because it (i) builds on existing national programs that meet the needs of poor and vulnerable population and are thus pertinent and have a high buy-in; in addition, it is designed to be flexible and adaptable to different contexts. The interventions are tailored to local circumstances; and (ii) they can facilitate deep systemic change by addressing underlying drivers of poverty and vulnerability at scale through the creation of new opportunities for employment that public and private institutions and creating policy incentives, and financing structures as part of a holistic implementation approach.

The two outcomes under this pathway are closely linked: the focus on nature-based solutions for reducing exposure to climate hazards can also contribute to improved food security outcomes through the protection and enhancement of natural resources. The CRPPB is designed to be implemented in parallel with achievement outcomes 3 and 4 and they provide the political will, institutional capacity and financing for the investments that are ultimately expected to be implemented under pathway 3. However, by capitalizing on existing national programs, the CRPPB can also contribute to achieving outcomes 1 and 2.

Supporting assumptions are that (i) the new techniques and skills introduced for improved climate resilient livelihoods will be adopted by poor and vulnerable people; (ii) there is sufficient institutional capacity and non-skilled labor force available to meet installation needs of green adaptive infrastructure, and new investments in these areas will not displace existing employment opportunities; (iii) there are sufficient low-end users willing and able to acquire the required skills and; (iv) low capacity and willingness of end users to maintain and operate new infrastructure.

The two primary potential risks associated with the pathway are (i) low capacity and willingness of end users to adopt new techniques, skills and; (ii) low capacity and willingness of end users to maintain and operate new infrastructure.


## Box 3: The CRPPB and Systems Transitions for Climate Resilient Development

### Climate Resident Development (CRD) has emerged as one of the guiding principles for climate policy and is identified by the IPCC in its 6th Assessment Report as a core requirement to pursue sustainable development. It is defined as an approach that seeks to address the root causes of poverty, including but not being, eliminate poverty and enable equitable, just and sustainable development.

#### System transitions have been highlighted as necessary processes for large-scale transformations, and are now recognized by the IPCC as one of the key elements in achieving climate-resilient social-ecological systems. The CRPPB has been designed to contribute to progress in core system transitions that catalyze broader transformational change that is consistent with the direction of the Paris Agreement.

#### Climate adaptation options (definition Section B3) that are targeted at the poor and vulnerable population, and have been identified by the IPCC as offering the potential for change within the key systems, as part of investments in new or existing large-scale government poverty reduction programs. The CRPPB is designed to be implemented in parallel with achievement outcomes 3 and 4 and they provide the political will, institutional capacity and financing for the investments that are ultimately expected to be implemented under pathway 3.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 37

## Co-benefits: The CRPP will have environmental, economic, social and gender co-benefits and will deliver against a number of the SDGs, including 1 no poverty, 3 good health and wellbeing, 11 sustainable cities and communities, and 5 gender equality. Some of the outcomes supported by the CRPP are also likely to have mitigation co-benefits (as highlighted in section b.3). However, it is important that gender equality is seen to be central to the philosophy of the program.

### 1. Women's participation in decision-making and leadership: Promoting gender equality is a core theme for CRPP. This will be achieved by improving national and local policies and planning frameworks, so that there are policy incentives and procedures in place for the inclusion of local people in decision making processes. In addition to this, as part of the CRPP's focus on strengthening the capacity of such governance structures, women will have the opportunity to participate in a meaningful way in decision-making at all levels. This is important because it will ensure that women are included in climate focused investments and resource flows will help to break generations of gender inequalities in economic and human capital endowments and voice and agency which shape their climate adaptive capacity.

### 2. Reduced inequities: This social co-benefit is highlighted because of the explicit focus on low income and disadvantaged groups, and the focus on poverty reduction and addressing existing inequalities. The CRPP will also seek to ensure that women are included in decision-making processes and institutional benefits by targeting these groups, increasing the flows of finance and responsibility to them.

The outputs and activities are described in the next section. Figure 2 provides a visual summary of the theory of change.

B.3 Project components description

Components, Outputs and Activities.

As outlined in section B.2, the CRPP comprises two separate but interlinked components: the CRPP Trust Fund (TF), which focuses on upstream support to strengthen the enabling environment required for financing and implementing adaptation solutions; and the CRPP Implementation Facility (IF), which focuses on the efficient roll-out of local adaptation measures through downstream investment program. CRPF is a key component of the IF.

CRPP TF. The TF, established in August 2021, is financed by development partners and administered by ADB. The trust fund provides technical assistance and grant resources for improving knowledge and understanding on climate change impacts and vulnerability; strengthening institutional capacity to support building local resilience. This would ultimately lead to a suite of investments which can be implemented by governments and other stakeholders in the CRPP countries, including through the IF.

CRPP IF. The TF will support implementation of outputs 1, 2 and 3 of the wider CRPP program (described below). In addition, infrastructure projects financed through CRPP TF are likely to primarily be implemented under the two key CRPP TF partners: the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and the Asian Development Bank (ADB), and the Huairou Commission (HC)). Under the CRPP TF, ADB will engage IED to implement some of the climate adaptation projects in the CRPP countries. The CRPP TF is expected to be fully funded by 2025.

The implementation guidelines for the CRPP TF have been developed with a focus on ensuring that the activities related to capacity building under output 2. Detailed implementation guidelines have been developed and will be finalized soon. In addition, the CRPP TF has been designed in such a way as to ensure that predictable funding is needed for supporting transformational adaptation at the local level; the TF will be implemented in phases (phase I: 2021-2025) with a total amount of USD76 million. To date, the Government of United Kingdom has committed USD34 million and the Government of Norway has committed USD8 million for support for the TF, to a total of about USD68 million. Discussions are ongoing with other bilateral donors and multilateral development partners (including ADB) on additional funding commitments that will receive support from the CRPP TF. However, due to limited availability of funding, the CRPP TF will assist selected countries. This will include all seven countries that are part of the CRPP IF plus selected additional countries. The CRPP TF will be managed by ADB and will report country progress to GCF as well as other donors.

**Window under CRPP TF:** In order to promote investments in women's economic empowerment, the building blocks for which include access to finance and education, approximately 15% of the TF window funding will provide technical assistance grants to (i) undertake policy dialogue and analytical studies which make the case for scaling up investment in women's economic empowerment; (ii) support development of feasibility studies for such investments; and (iii) build capacity of women machineries in country and civil society organizations to identify, design, implement and monitor gender-sensitive climate change adaptation solutions. The window will also provide grant resources to grassroots women's organizations to implement small-scale innovative adaptation solutions and pilot projects as part of the lesson learning and evidence generation process.

The CRPP TF will be financed by GCF and administered by ADB. It will be a program to provide critical grants for the implementation of climate change adaptation projects (projects) in seven countries that are targeted at the poorest populations. Without GCF financing, these projects would not have been possible. The CRPP TF is expected to be fully funded by 2025. Given the importance of providing longer-term financing, the IF will have a time horizon of 2023-2030. Projects seeking funding under this window should be in line with the CRPP program and should aim for implementation by 2030.

The IF will have at least USD120 million (USD100 grant and USD20 loan) from GCF for seven countries and will leverage at least USD550 million from ADB's resources and USD78 million from other development partners. The CRPP TF will be managed by ADB, while the IF will be managed by GCF.

Each of the seven countries will have least one project and will receive between USD10 million and USD40 million in grant funding (USD5-20 million from GCF and USD5-20 million from other development partners). The allocation and use of resources under the IF will be aligned with CRPP program described below).

This information is accessible to ADB Management and staff. It may be shared outside ADB with appropriate permission.

# Box 4: Lessons from Implementation of the Pilot Programme for Climate Resilience in African Countries

## High level coordination across multiple sectors

The PPCR implementation has found that high-level coordination was most effective approach for shaping a program of resilient investments and promising on implementation effectiveness and sustainability. The PPCR experience highlights that coordination at higher levels of government was more effective than under CRPP which has a large focus on strengthening institutional coordination.

## Linking and leveraging investments

Both linked investments and leveraged investments were implemented in the countries, providing countries' pathways. This lesson has been factored in the PPCR program design which aims to provide a flexible framework that considers country capacities, political structures, and overall development regimes. This lesson has led to the design of the CRPP, which is also independently robust, outputs which will provide flexibility for the countries to develop programs while ensuring alignment with the broader regional climate change resilience agenda.

## Facilitating private sector engagement

Upfront technical assistance and targeted advisory services have been provided to support the development of innovative solutions. This includes piloting new modalities of climate adaptation, validating their commercial viability, and creating a market for these solutions.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

## Outputs under the CRPP TF: The CRPP TF supports activities under outputs 1, 2 and 3. Activities under these outputs will not be financed by GCF, but they underpin and enable the achievement of the overall project objective.

### Output 1: Knowledge and action research on climate risk informed pro-poor community-level solutions strengthened.
Knowledge and support technical assistance will be provided to strengthen knowledge and understanding of climate risks at the local level. This will include:
- Strengthening the capacity of key stakeholders (both nationally and internationally) to identify solutions to overcome key barriers in access to finance for adaptation, including through the use of innovative financing options;
- Providing opportunities to enhance the effectiveness and equitability of adaptation finance delivery mechanisms; and
- Document and share lessons, including lessons emerging from COVID-19 response, which will inform future climate risk informed pro-poor community-level solutions. This will include exploring options for adaptation, including private sector financing.

### Activities: Activities will include supporting governments and communities to:
(i) conduct research and analysis for identifying a suite of transformational adaptation investments that meet local needs and are aligned with national priorities;
(ii) share experiences of using innovative financing options for adaptation, which can be scaled up at the national level; and
(iii) document lessons learned from COVID-19 response.

### Output 2: Institutional and community capacity to develop and deliver climate adaptation investments strengthened.
Institutional and community capacity will be strengthened through:
- Strengthening local and national capacity to develop and deliver community-level adaptation investments at scale, which meet the needs of vulnerable communities;
- Building capacity within national and local institutions for monitoring and evaluating adaptation investments; and
- Facilitating access to finance for adaptation projects, including linkage with Enhanced Direct Access Pilot, where appropriate. This can include support for developing a pipeline of projects that are aligned with national priorities.

### Activities: Activities will support:
(i) the development and institutionalisation of procedures and mechanisms for the devolution of responsibilities for adaptation planning and implementation, ensuring inclusiveness and participation in decision-making processes;
(ii) the conduct of capacity building workshops for government officials on applying climate risk information in the formulation of plans, policies, and strategies; and
(iii) the strengthening of national and local institutions for monitoring and evaluating adaptation investments.

### Output 3: Inclusive and pro-poor investment projects identified and prepared.
This output supports governments in developing large-scale bankable adaptation projects that are aligned with national adaptation priorities and address the nexus between climate and poverty, with special emphasis on women. At least one project will be developed under this output to demonstrate how the CRPP TF can support local level adaptation and contributes to overcoming the barrier of limited access to finance for adaptation at the local level.

### Selected projects proposed under this output will not be implemented through the CRPP TF outputs 4, 5 and 6.


---
**Page 46**
---

Activities under this output will: (i) support governments to prepare feasibility studies for adaptation projects, development proposals under Outputs 1 and 2, and engage potential sources of accessing concessional development and climate finance for implementation of such projects by engaging with potential financing partners (national development banks, multi-lateral development banks, climate finance mechanisms, etc.); (ii) provide technical assistance to governments in the identification and strengthening linkages with the GCF's Enhanced Access Direct access pilot; (iii) provide grant funding (on a very selective basis) for feasibility studies and project preparation activities. The overall process of project identification and preparation will be undertaken by the TF, which does not involve GCF finance, and thus there is no need to engage with the GCF in this regard. The technical assistance will be aligned with the National Adaptation Plans (NAP) and GCF readiness funding and country work programs.

Gender Window: The CRPP Gender Window will support the identification, development, and implementation of adaptation projects that are gender-sensitive and address women's vulnerability to climate change. As such, support will be provided through (i) technical assistance for creating an enabling environment for women-focused adaptation measures; identifying investment opportunities and providing grant funding for feasibility studies and project preparation activities; (ii) generating the necessary political will, commitment from governments and communities to invest in women's empowerment and gender equality as a key component of climate change adaptation; (iii) generating the necessary political will, and commitment from governments and communities to invest in women's empowerment and gender equality as a key component of climate change adaptation; and (iv) building capacities for the inclusion of women in adaptation decision-making at the local level and building their capacity to participate effectively in national and international climate change policy development and implementation which can empower local women to act on adaptation; and (v) place women at the centre of all CRPP activities, including project identification, design, implementation, and evaluation. Climate resilience of women, particularly poor women, and/or adaptation investments that are led by women.

When approached together, output 1,2 and 3 will strengthen the enabling environment in the countries for scaling up adaptation solutions that directly benefits the poor and vulnerable populations, including women.


Outputs under CRPP: The CRPP will support activities under outputs 4.5 and B. These outputs will be supported through a combination of GCF resources (as part of ADB financed investments) and/or bilateral donors through the CRPP TF and/or other partner financing institutions. Selected projects emerging from the feasibility studies and project preparation activities will also be supported by the CRPP TF through large-scale investments explicitly targeted at the poor and vulnerable population. The CRPP TF provides technical assistance to governments in the identification, development, and implementation of outputs 4.5 and B as part of ADB financed investments (projects) in particular areas where there is a high level of vulnerability to climate change. This will be particularly relevant for COVID-19 recovery, thereby ensuring CRPP's alignment with COVID-19 recovery. Annex 2.3 describes the importance of supporting these outputs in the context of increasing climate risk and barriers in scaling up adaptation solutions that directly benefits the poor and vulnerable populations, including women.

Output 4: Information and systems for delivering low-carbon-risk informed investments at scale

Enhanced climate risk information services that can provide reliable, tailored information based on the past, present and potential future climate will be critical for better understanding of potential risks over very different time horizons. The CRPP TF has been working with ADB to develop a suite of climate risk information services have also been identified by the IPCC as a key adaptation action for enabling systems transitions. It is important that these services are developed in close collaboration with the relevant national focal area, through engagement with local stakeholders, including women. While there has been improvement in weather and climate forecasting and provision of downscaled climate change projections, existing climate information services have not yet reached their full potential. The CRPP TF will work to ensure that these services do not reach "the last mile". Often the poor and vulnerable communities whose livelihoods are climate sensitive do not have access to this information or the capacity to use it effectively. Accessing and using climate risk information is fundamental to planning and financing adaptation solutions in their constituencies. Global analysis on

climate information services reveals that capacities to deliver climate-risk and reliable early warning information services varies across countries and the functional capacities on monitoring and evaluation of resilience and adaptation are also uneven. While some progress in improvements and weak capacities to adapt such practices can be a major barrier to deal with climate risks. The World Bank, Green Climate Fund, 2019 report notes that “while investments have substantially increased over the past decade, there is still much more work to do to ensure the provision of high quality climate information and services for climate-resilient action.”


In addition, the World Meteorological Organization has identified that there is a need to invest specifically in early warning systems and climate risk information services. This will help countries better understand and produce accurate forecasts and timely warnings, to better understand and anticipate the likely human and economic impacts of extreme weather events. The WMO also notes that there are significant gaps, specifically the types of impacts associated with the forecast to more effectively trigger early action based on the forecast. These gaps can be addressed by developing a greater understanding of how climate risks reach the end users such as farmers. This output will support countries in developing, maintaining and using climate risk information services for pre-disaster decision-making purposes for resilience, thereby providing an opportunity to better understand the types of impacts associated with extreme weather events and understanding of impacts. It will also help the large-scale mobilisation of domestic resources for pre-peak disaster response and recovery planning. Activity Area 4.1: Climate information services for key sectors under this activity area, it will be important to support countries in strengthening their national frameworks for climate services guided by the five pillars of the Global Framework for Climate Services which includes use of data, information and knowledge; capacity building; access to information and knowledge; modeling and prediction; and capacity building. (i) improvements in multi-hazard early warning systems to support disaster risk reduction and adaptation; (ii) development of a climate services platform that will be structured around the key elements identified by the WMO on Multi-hazard-based Forecasting and Warning Systems (MHWFS); (iii) strengthening national frameworks for climate services, including requirements, capacity development and validation; and (iv) adapting and scaling up of gender-sensitive climate information services and demonstrate its application to manage farm level climate risks.


Activity Area 4.2: Climate risk informed social protection systems. Under this activity area, it will be important to support countries in strengthening their national frameworks for climate services guided by the five pillars of the Global Framework for Climate Services which includes use of data, information and knowledge; capacity building; access to information and knowledge; modeling, forecasting and early warning capability, which can support forecast-based financing through social protection programs based on the lessons learned from the successful pilots being undertaken by humanitarian organizations such as World Food Programme and Red Crescent Societies. (v) development of tools and resources that can be deployed in social protection programs to facilitate transfer of assistance in the aftermath of extreme weather events; (vi) strengthening national frameworks for climate services, including requirements, capacity development and validation.

## Activity Area 4.3 Climate risk information systems for decentralization policies. This will support the implementation of climate-resilient decentralized policies and frameworks from climate risk perspective based on experiences of the United Nations Capital Development Fund (UNCDF) in introducing participatory local climate risk assessment, vulnerability analysis, and adaptation planning at the Local level (LoCAL): specifically, (i) national and local governments in the preparation of climate risk and vulnerability assessments; (ii) development partners in supporting LoCAL to prepare a climate-resilient decentralization and build capacity of local governments through trainings, workshops and consultations in use of participatory tools for climate risk assessment, vulnerability analysis, and adaptation planning; (iii) adaptation investments as part of local development plans, mobilize financing for adaptation, formulate incentives to scale up adoption, and increase awareness among local stakeholders on adaptation; (iv) local government assistance in the preparation of a LoCAL action plan for climate-resilient decentralization, including undertaking capacity building, improvement of methodologies, and strengthening public participation mechanisms at the local level. This will be done through the following components: (i) implementation of adaptation measures that have been prioritized in local development plans through participatory tools; (ii) establishment of a LoCAL action plan for climate-resilient decentralization including women's organizations using existing budgeting and fiscal transfer mechanisms and building capacity for local governments to implement climate-resilient decentralization at the local level, with sufficient funds and capacities to respond to climate change at the local level, mainstreaming climate change adaptation into all development plans and budgets; (iii) strengthening public participation in decision-making processes available for local adaptation to climate change adaptation. The CRPP provides a significant opportunity to replicate and scale-up some of the key activities implemented under LoCAL.

## Output 5. Climate resilient pre-poor livelihoods investments implemented. Many low-income countries are highly vulnerable to climate change, with large populations living in poverty. In Papua New Guinea for example, 87% of the total population live in rural areas, and Cambodia (76%), Vanuatu (74%), Timor-Leste (69%), Lao PDR (53%) and Solomon Islands (52%) are also highly vulnerable to climate change. The majority proportion of GDP in many of these countries (Vanuatu 27%, Pakistan 25% and Cambodia 23%) and many rural households derive their income from agriculture, which is the most important sector for food security[1]. In Lao PDR for example, 55% of rural households are subsistence farmers with incomes below US$400 per year. The majority of these people live in remote areas where access to markets and services in agriculture is more than 50% of total employment. These people's livelihoods are heavily dependent on natural resources such as forests, rivers, lakes and coastal zones. Many of the most vulnerable communities often inhabit marginal land on hillsides, in deserts and floodplains, and on coastal zones that are highly exposed to floods, droughts and storm surges, which can erode their natural asset base. Sub-optimal agricultural practices and lack of access to information and services exacerbate these climate change impacts on their income and food security. This output will therefore support poor and vulnerable communities in the most affected countries to increase their resilience to climate change by investing to create climate resilient sustainable livelihoods. New adaptation interventions will be needed at the farm level to improve agricultural productivity (e.g., improved seeds, fertilizers, irrigation systems, soil conservation and water management), provide farmers in poor communities with access to climate information, early warning services and weather forecasts, increase their access to markets for their produce through production practices (e.g. index-based weather insurance), and supply chains from local to national level that will help them to better manage the risks of climate change and ensure sufficient food despite the impacts of climate change (e.g., improving transport and logistics infrastructure etc.).

Activities under this output will be guided by the Sustainable Livelihoods Framework (SLF), first proposed by the then UK Department for International Development, which identifies five types of assets or capitals upon which livelihoods are based: natural capital (e.g. land, water, forests, wildlife); financial capital; human health; social capital (quantity and quality of social resources as networks and access to wider institutions


---
**Page 49**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 45

In society), including fresh water, soil quality, forests, healthy ecosystems), physical (infrastructure, services, and productive assets), and financial (cash and other liquid resources, such as savings, credit, remittances, pensions, etc.). The SLP will apply an approach that is consistent with the concept of building household food security and climate resilience and uses a common set-based concept for the identification of the activities under this output. With its focus on capacities, vulnerabilities and livelihood stock assessments, the SLP will also provide a basis to identify the most vulnerable households and their capacity to cope with and adapt to climate-related impacts. This livelihood resilience perspective is particularly relevant in the context of the GCF, which aims at addressing poverty and inequality and differences in perceiving risk and taking anticipatory actions, either individually or collectively, and places greater emphasis on human capital, rather than dominated by ecosystems, technologies, political contexts, markets and resource networks.

This output will finance the following types of activities. The activities will be selected based on assessment of their potential to contribute to climate resilience and poverty reduction, while avoiding duplication. The activities are closely aligned to the GCF paradigm shifting investment pathways outlined in the Agriculture and Food Security Sectoral Guide, as well as the adaptation options identified by the IPCC as being most relevant for the region. These include: (i) promoting resilient crop production systems; some activities, such as promoting the uptake of climate smart agricultural practices are also anticipated to have co-benefits in terms of poverty reduction.

### Activity Area 5.1: Climate-resilient agroecosystems
The main focus will be preserving and increasing natural capital, such as water and soil for productive purposes, and building human capital, as described above. The activities will include (i) promoting the adoption of climate resilient crop production practices. This output is closely aligned with the GCF Agriculture and Food Security Paradigm Shift Investment Pathway, which aims to promote a shift from conventional to more sustainable agricultural production systems. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 46

## Activity Area 4.3 Climate resilient agriculture supply chains.

The focus will be to build human capital and physical capital, as identified in the SLP, to deal with climate impacts on the agricultural supply chain. This will support activities under the Green Climate Fund’s Strategic Objective 2: Promoting Food Security and Nutrition through Pathway 3: Reconfiguring food systems. This will support activities such as (i) improving the transport and logistics infrastructure, especially through the use of ICT solutions, to successfully bring products to market despite climate impacts; (ii) strengthening the resilience of agricultural supply chains by investing in climate-resilient infrastructure for the siling and storage of seeds, tools, vehicles and energy infrastructure as well as installation of new or improved irrigation systems; (iii) improving access to markets through the development of cold chain facilities on productive inputs and market produce; (iv) providing technological solutions and capacity building for increased productivity in smallholder agriculture, especially women’s groups, particularly in areas such as fruits and vegetables. The COVID-19 pandemic has highlighted the importance of resilient agriculture supply chains and will continue to be a priority during recovery and will contribute to outcomes related to increasing food security of poor and vulnerable populations.

## Output 6: Climate adaptation pro-poor infrastructure implemented.

There are urgent needs to scale up investments in adaptation infrastructure, i.e., infrastructure that has a primary purpose of building resilience to climate change impacts. These investments can help reduce the vulnerability of people and physical vulnerabilities of the poor and vulnerable communities to the impacts of climate change and protect them from adverse effects. Adaptation infrastructure can also provide co-benefits such as environmental benefits, that help build community resilience, as well as providing economic benefits. This output will support local governments in developing adaptation strategies and plans for scaling up investments in green infrastructure in respective countries in order to ensure complementarity and to avoid duplication. They are also closely aligned with the SLP’s Strategic Objective 2: Promoting Food Security and Nutrition through Pathway 1 and urban and infrastructure system transitions. Some activities will also have important mitigation co-benefits, such as the conservation of mangrove forests which could potentially mitigate upwards of -37 to -57 t CO₂ eq per year.

### Activity Area 6.1 Ecosystem-based infrastructure.

This will support activities such as (i) national and local governments in collaboration with national scientific organizations in identifying locally appropriate green infrastructure solutions; (ii) undertaking awareness raising campaigns for the poor and vulnerable communities; (iii) in undertaking trainings for local governments, communities and civil society on ecosystem-based adaptation strategies and solutions; (iv) providing technical assistance to local governments/communities (especially women’s groups) by providing small grants for planting and restoration of mangroves and other coastal ecosystems; (v) developing a national or sub-national mechanism for developing a consistent approach to designing and implementing nature-based solutions, including ecosystem-based adaptation measures; and will thereby guide the GRPP to deploy such investments in green infrastructure.

### Activity Area 6.2 Flood and landslide protection infrastructure.

This will support activities such (i) undertaking local planning processes to identify and prioritize small scale protective infrastructure and restoration of existing infrastructure for flood and landslide protection; (ii) providing technical assistance on flood and landslide protection measures, especially measures that use a combination of grey and green infrastructure solutions; (iii) in undertaking trainings for local governments and communities in maintenance of protective infrastructure.

### Activity Area 6.3 Emergency sheltering shelters.

This will support activities such as (i) undertaking local planning, with active participation of women, to identify the need of multipurpose emergency shelter facilities that can accommodate poor and vulnerable populations; (ii) providing technical assistance for local governments in developing emergency shelters (with gender inclusive and socially appropriate design features); (iii) in undertaking trainings for local governments and communities in planning and maintenance of multipurpose emergency shelters.

## Ideas on Project Concepts

The following paragraphs provide a summary of ADB investments (projects) that while currently in their very early stages of conceptualization, have the potential to align with the objectives of CRPP (subject to findings of feasibility studies). The project concepts are described in more detail in Annex 2.3 and demonstrate examples of large-scale interventions that could be considered for inclusion under the projects listed above using the CRPP IF resources provided by GCF. Without financing from the IF, activities proposed under these projects that explicitly promote adaptation to climate change at the local level will not be implemented.

### Cambodia: The Proposed Climate Resilient Smallholder Development Project

The project is designed to build on the successful implementation of past and ongoing ADB financed Tone Sap Poverty Reduction and Social Development Project (TSPS) in Kampong Speu province. The TSPS has supported Local Government Improvement groups (LGIs) and provided them with group revolving funds managed by a committee of members. These LGIs have been instrumental in mobilizing savings from the poor to invest in productive assets and subsequently to access funds from rural credit organizations. The LGI model enhanced the income-generating opportunities of its members supported by credit, skills development, and market access.

The project will build on this experience by supporting the implementation of climate change adaptation actions that: (i) enhance resilience and climate risk reduction mainstreaming in agriculture development activities and project design; (ii) integrate a suite of climate change adaptation actions related to rural infrastructure rehabilitation and expansion; (iii) promote the adoption of climate-resilient varieties of crops, livestock and bio-engineering to strengthen infrastructure; (iv) support the establishment of an early warning system for floods and droughts; (v) enhance disaster risk management (adaptive social protection); (vi) institutional resilience through strengthening capacity of local institutions to manage climate change adaptation actions and promote community-based disaster risk reduction by mobilizing green finance and introducing insurance solutions in rural areas to support livelihoods and reduce vulnerability to climate-related shocks. The proposed project directly contributes to the activities under output 4 and output 5 of the CRPP.

### Laos: The Proposed Building Resilience and Reducing Rural Poverty Project

The project will build resilience for rural poor in Lao PDR to climate change by expanding the coverage of the non-contributory social insurance scheme (NCSIS) and providing access to microfinance services. It will also provide livelihood support in response to the impacts of climate and weather-related hazards, such as floods. The inclusion of shock- absorption mechanisms for rural poor households is critical given their limited ability to cope with shocks like floods to smooth consumption and minimise negative coping strategies. The project will expand coverage of the NCSIS scheme from 10% to 35% of the rural population in Lao PDR, supported by the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MFA) and the World Bank into new geographic locations that have high levels of vulnerability to climate change impacts. The project will also provide livelihood support for vulnerable households through microfinance services provided by the MFA. The project will strengthen the capacity of MFA and the Ministry of Planning and Investment (MPI) to implement priority social protection programs. Specific activities will include: (i) integrating vulnerability to climate shocks and risks into the social insurance scheme; (ii) providing livelihood support for vulnerable households through microfinance services on climate risk; (iii) link the social registry with early warning systems; and (iv) provide operational and technical assistance to MFA and MPI in implementing priority social protection programs, including their environmental and social safeguards management. Thus, support from the CRPP if will support Laos

## B

### Proposed Flood and Drought Mitigation and Management Project

The proposed Flood and Drought Mitigation and Management Project aims to increase the resilience of rural livelihoods and reduce the economic losses from floods and droughts as a result of increasing extreme weather events caused by climate change in Lae PDP. Flood and drought events already occur frequently, with an average of 10–20% of the population affected each year. The project will focus on the population that relies on agricultural activities as the primary source of livelihood, and live on or below the poverty line.

The project will address the specific adaptation needs of rural agrarian populations who live in the coastal zone of Lae Province, Papua New Guinea (PNG). It will contribute to reducing flood risk, drought-related hazards and fill an identifiable funding gap in this regard. The project will support women to play a role in decision-making processes related to climate change.

### Project Activities

The project activities are aligned with output 5 and output 6 of the GRPP:

1. **Improved Water Resources Management**: This includes integrated water resources, drainage, and flood risk management schemes, and restored natural protection buffers.
2. **Restoration of Ecosystems**: This will contribute to reducing coastal erosion rate, provide protection from storm surge, and reduce saline intrusion in coastal areas.

### Project Outputs

The project directly contributes to the activities under output 5 and output 6 of the GRPP:

1. **Improved Water Resources Management**.
2. **Restoration of Ecosystems**.

### Proposed Improved Technical and Vocational Education and Training for Climate Resilient Employment Project

The proposed Improved Technical and Vocational Education and Training for Climate Resilient Employment Project aims to strengthen climate resilience through technical and vocational education and training (TVET) in Lae Province. The project will focus on the following areas:

1. **Climate Change Adaptation**: This includes skills training for climate resilient agriculture and non-agriculture income generation activities, as well as disaster preparedness and response.
2. **Banking Mechanisms**: This involves providing access to financial services that are tailored to long-term resilience to climate change.

The project directly contributes to the activities under output 6 of the GRPP:

1. **Improved Technical and Vocational Education and Training**.

## Project Eligibility Criteria

Projects that seek GCF co-financing under the GCF IFP are subject to a strict assessment and selection process, which is based on a framework of criteria that is closely aligned with GCF investment criteria, while also reflecting the core values and priorities of the CRPP. These criteria are also aligned with the selection criteria for GCF IFP. In order to be considered for GCF co-financing, project proposals must also demonstrate alignment with the CRPP’s objectives and principles. The eligibility criteria will apply in addition to due diligence conducted by all projects (such as safeguards) and conduct of economic analysis under ADB’s ongoing practice.

The eligibility criteria would include the following: See Annex 2.4 for more details

## Timor-Leste: The proposed Agriculture Value Chains and Water Harvesting Project aims to enhance the sustainability of rural livelihoods in Timor-Leste by increasing the resilience of poor subsistence farming families, smallholder producers, women and informal workers against climate change impacts while improving the productivity of small-scale agriculture by using climate-smart approaches, including investments in water harvesting. By providing access to markets for agricultural products through investments, the project also aims to enable smaller producers, especially women, to expand their production and income-generating activities. The project will be implemented in four municipalities (Lautém, Manatuto, Viqueque and Bobonaro) as well as in the municipalities of Dili (non-urban), Manatuto, Manufahi, and Oecusse which together represent the core agricultural areas of Timor-Leste. These municipalities are characterized by high levels of poverty, being at high risk from the impacts of climate change and include communities with high levels of poverty.

The project is in line with the priorities of the NDC, the National Adaptation Programme of Action to Climate Change (NAPA), and the CRPP. The project will contribute to the activities under output 5 of the CRPP.

## Impact potential:
Following criteria will be used to determine the project's impact potential:

a. The project must clearly set out how it will reduce risk (hazards, exposure and vulnerabilities), under current and future conditions of climate change and socio-economic development, that their objectives aim to address, articulating a clear causal narrative between specific activities and their objective outcomes. This narrative should be based on the best available evidence and data, and quantified using reliable data sources and information will be prioritized. [Required]

b. Population at risk: The project must clearly define the population at risk (e.g., total population (TI); householded; TI-Geographic; or TI-SQG) [Strongly Desired]

c. The project must describe how the project activities are expected to contribute to be generated by the implementation of the project activities. Only projects that identify measurable adaptation benefits will be funded.

d. Projects must align to GCF adaptation result areas and prioritization will be given to those projects that meet the following criteria:

e. Prioritization will be given for projects with a higher expected total number of beneficiaries relative to total population (disaggregated by gender)

f. Projects should demonstrate high potential cost per beneficiary

## Paradigm shift potential:
The potential of the sub-project to catalyze impact beyond a one-off intervention.

a. The project must clearly describe how the project outcome will be sustainable in the long run:

i. As a result of the project, the country or region will have secured funding through securing additional public and/or private financials, and capacity of national/local institutions to implement similar activities in other regions/countries (e.g., through development of replicable models for climate change adaptation programs) [Required]

b. Projects should demonstrate that projects which demonstrate the potential of being replicated elsewhere in the same country or other countries in region (e.g., successful agroforestry initiatives replicated in different ecological zones of a country) [Strongly Desired]

c. The project must describe how it will contribute to the adoption and/or adaptation of new technology or approaches which can help address adaptation barriers in the region (e.g., use of eco-friendly construction materials, improved water harvesting techniques in urban areas) [Desired]

d. The project promotes sharing of knowledge and lessons within and with other CRRP countries [Required]

## Sustainable development potential:
All projects must demonstrate sustainable development potential by delivering at least one positive co-benefit from the following and have associated indicators. Prioritization will be given to projects that demonstrate more than one co-benefit (Required):

a. Economic co-benefits – explicitly aim at poverty reduction

b. Social co-benefits - human health and social development through improved food and nutrition security, reduced vulnerability to impacts of extreme weather events etc.

c. Environmental co-benefits - improved ecosystem services and biodiversity

## Gender:
Gender is a standard criterion that projects must meet independently of the project's focus area. The following gender criteria will be used for all projects:

a) Projects must demonstrate compliance with the Gender Equality Theme (GET) or Effective Gender Mainstreaming (EGM) as per Guidelines for Gender Mainstreaming in Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation [GEN] if the project outcome directly addresses gender equality and/or women's empowerment by narrowing the gap between men and women on at least one of the following: (i) women's access to resources increased, (ii) gender equity in human development enhanced, (iii) gender equality in decision-making and leadership enhanced, (iv) women's time poverty and drudgery reduced, and/or (v) women's rights strengthened.

The four areas of the Adaptation Design Bank have four gender mainstreaming categories (i) category a: gender parity as a focus (GEN); (ii) category b: effective gender mainstreaming (EGM); (iii) category c: same gender elements (SGE), and (iv) category d: no gender elements (NGE).

B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

## Executive Entity of GRPP

As Executing Entity for the CRPPF, ADB will be responsible for the overall Program, particularly the provision of technical assistance support under CRPPF (Component 2), and the provision of grants and loans for projects under CRPPF IF (Component 2).

Subject to GCF Board approval for this funding proposal, ADB and GCF will enter into a program-specific agreement (IF Implementation Agreement) that will set out the terms and conditions of the Program. This agreement will set out the specific details concerning GCF's provision of and ADB's administration of the GCF funds.

## GCF Funds

GCF funds for the CRPPF will be ring-fenced within an ADB GCF Trust Fund which was created in support of GCF. The IF Implementation Agreement will specify that only a portion of the total amount of GCF funds will be used to fund the Program, and the specific amount of GCF funds available under this agreement for the CRPPF will be based on criteria outlined in this proposal which will be included in dedicated IF Implementation Arrangements.

The IF Implementation Agreement will include guidance on the use of GCF funds, including the terms and conditions set out by GCF and ADB, and include further guidance on implementation arrangements, project selection and prioritization, monitoring and evaluation, and reporting. For Projects implemented by an executing agency and/or implementing agency (as each defined by ADB), where such agency is expected to change GCF Proceeds for the purposes of the Funded Activity and/or such agency will execute, carry out or implement Funded Activities, the executing agency and/or implementing agency will enter into a separate agreement with ADB. In addition to the implementation arrangements described in this Funding Proposal, ADB will enter into a subsidiary agreement with the relevant executing/implementing agency.

## 2. CRPPF IF Grant Arrangements

Under CRPPF IF, ADB will offer concessional loan and grants to sovereign borrowers to be funded by GCF. For each CRPPF IF project, ADB will sign separate loan and/or grant agreements with the sovereign borrower/beneficiary for each source of financing (i.e., ADB's own resources and GCF funds), and through such agreements, ADB will provide concessional loans and grants to the sovereign borrower/beneficiary. Depending on the structure of the project, ADB will also enter into project-level agreements with relevant borrowers' agents or counterparties, including but not limited to national governments, local authorities, public sector entities, National Development Banks and potentially, GCF Direct Access Entities, where feasible. If additional co-financing is required for a CRPPF IF project, ADB may seek such co-financing from other sources (e.g., the ADB and GCF funded loans and grants). Loans funded by GCF funds are expected to be pari passu with the ADB loan,享受相同的利息率和费率适用于所有贷款。

For each CRPPF IF, ADB enters into separate legal agreements with borrower/counterparties for each financing source. A loan/grant from ADB's own resources will be covered by one legal agreement, while the loan/grant from GCF funds will be covered by another legal agreement, with cross-references between the two as appropriate (Figure 3).

### CRPF Flow of Funds

The CRPF will be implemented from 2023 to 2030. Projects seeking financing from GCF will need to be listed in the country programming document (CPD) for consideration by ADB for implementation in 2025. Projects which will be supported by the program will be identified in accordance with ADB's country programming process and approved by the ADB Board on a project-by-project basis.

Payments of interest and principal for GCF funded projects loans will be repaid back to ADB, into the ADB GCF Trust Fund. Principal, interest payments, and any unused funds or income will be subsequently returned to GCF.

### 4. CRPP TF Legal arrangement and Flow of Funds

The CRPPF was established in 2001 as a multi-donor trust fund under CRPPF to support TA projects, and any other activities that may be agreed upon between financing partners and ADB. Contributions from bilateral donors will be held and administered by ADB separately from ADB's other resources. Contributions to the CRPPF Trust Fund can also be effected through an Instrument of Contribution or through the signing of a trust fund contribution agreement.

### 5. Role of ADB

As a GCF AE, ADB, and GCF have entered into several project-specific legal agreements which are under implementation, delivering climate impacts throughout ADB's developing member countries.

In its capacity as AE and Executing Entity, ADB will administer grants and loans from GCF under the CRPPF Trust Fund:

- Provide grants and loans as per the CRPPF IF terms and requirements, alongside loans using ADB resources.
- Ensure that all lending activities are conducted in accordance with the same duty of care and due diligence for all sources.
- Carry out a full environmental assessment of the GCF funds for grants and loans in accordance with its policies, procedures, and practices, and with at least the same degree of care as it is required to be carried out under ADB's own lending policy.
- Establish a governance structure of the CRPPF which will comprise of (i) CRPPF Partnership Group; (ii) CRPPF Steering Committee; (iii) CRPPF Working Group; and (iv) CRPPF Secretariat.

Overall, ADB will be responsible for managing the CRPPF Trust Fund, including hiring part-time and intermittent experts, including an additional expert specifically engaged for the CRPPF, with the following responsibilities:

- Originate projects and develop a pipeline for CRPP funding.
- Provide support to structure projects and identify financing partners.

## Governance Structure of CRFFP

The CRFFP will be operationalised through the Community Resilience Financing Partnership Facility (CRFFP), a multi-donor trust fund managed by a Specialized Entity. The Facility will aim to mobilize financial and knowledge resources from various development partners to support scaling up of climate adaptation investments at local level.

### Key Objectives

1. **Strengthening Partnerships:** To strengthen partnerships among governments, civil society organizations (CSOs), donors, civil society, and the private sector.
2. **Influence Global Directions:** To influence global directions on community-led adaptation through advocacy and knowledge sharing with key stakeholders such as the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) Secretariat, donor agencies, and other relevant actors.

### Governance Structure

The governance structure of the CRFFP will comprise:

- **CRFFP Partnership Group:** The CRFFP Partnership Group will be the primary advisory body to ensure the operationalization of the CRFFP. It will consist of representatives from development partners, with one representative serving as the Chair.
  - A representative from ADB will serve as the Chair.
  - A member from one of the CRFFP financing partners will also serve on the Chair.
- **CRFFP Steering Committee:** The CRFFP Steering Committee will be responsible for:
  - Approving CRFFP annual work plan based on endorsement received from financing partners.
  - Approving CRFFP annual report based on endorsement from financing partners and the Board of Directors of the Climate Change Support Services (CCSS) of ADB, and members of the Steering Committee will review and approve the CRFFP annual report for submission to the CCSS Board.
- **CRFFP TFP and IF:** The Chair of the CRFFP Steering Committee will designate a person for approving all activities of CRFFP resources for specific projects. After approval by the CRFFP Technical Committee, the TFP and IF will allocate resources and provide technical assistance and grant approval.

### Members of the Board

The members of the Board will include:

- (i) Asian Development Bank (ADB), represented by the Chair of ADB’s Climate Change Support Services Council.
- (ii) International Finance Corporation (IFC).
- (iii) Countries that receive CRFFP support: Non-financing partners - International Institute for Environment and Development (IIED).

### CRFFP Secretariat

The CRFFP Secretariat will be responsible for:

- Approving CRFFP annual work plan based on endorsement received from financing partners.
- Approving CRFFP annual report based on endorsement from financing partners, the Board of Directors of CCSS, ADB, and members of the Steering Committee.

### Additional Roles

The CRFFP Secretariat will also be responsible for:

- Ensuring compliance with CRFFP implementation guidelines.
- Approving CRFFP annual report based on endorsement from financing partners, the Board of Directors of CCSS, ADB, and members of the Steering Committee.

CRFF Working Group: The CRFF Working Group will be responsible for reviewing project proposals and ensure they meet the eligibility criteria outlined in Annex 2.4 and make recommendations to CRFF Secretariat on projects to be funded by the CRFF. The Working Group is co-chaired by the World Bank and UNDP, and includes the Change CCSD, and comprise of ADB staff from relevant departments with technical expertise on social development, gender, climate and disaster resilience, urban development, and agriculture and food security.

The CRFF Secretariat will be responsible for managing the CRFF and its day-to-day operations. The secretariat will be housed at the ADB and operate under ADB’s policies and procedures. The CRFF Secretariat will consist of (i) a Secretariat Director; (ii) Knowledge Expert; and (iii) Gender Expert. The roles and responsibilities of the CRFF Secretariat will include: (i) managing the CRFF, including preparation of work plan and annual report; (ii) convening CRFF Committee meetings; (iii) preparing the annual work plan and annual report; (iv) supervising day-to-day implementation of annual work plan; (v) preparing minutes of working group meetings on evaluation of CR projects; (vi) coordinating with ADB’s relevant departments and other stakeholders in developing partnerships with relevant institutions and programs at global and regional level; and (vii) disseminate the CRFF results and achievements. The role of gender expert will be to ensure gender requirements are being met.

7. Allocation of Resources

The administration of different funds under the CRFPF is governed by respective Implementation Guidelines that would be endorsed by the respective CRFPF financing partners and approved by the CRFPF Steering Committee on or before the first fund allocation. The CRFP IF Implementation Guidelines will be endorsed by the ADB Management Board and approved by the ADB Board of Directors.

Procedure for Allocation of Resources from CRFP TF: The procedure for allocation of resources from TF (not for individual projects) is as follows:

* Project proposals for TF can be initiated by countries, development partners, and AID. In Q1 of each year, the CRFP Secretariat in close consultation with the CRFP Partnership Group will prepare a work plan which would include the list of prioritized project proposal. The work plan for the TF will be approved by the financing partners of the CRFP TF.

* ADB user departments will prepare project proposals using the application form (to be developed as part of Implementation Guidelines and draft concept paper in standard ADB concept paper format) and will submit them to the CRFP Secretariat, depending on the nature of application. Applications will be reviewed in batches.


The applications will be reviewed by the CRPP Secretariat to ensure that they comply with the eligibility criteria described in the Implementation Guidelines and priorities in the annual work plan. If the application does not meet the criteria, the CRPP Fund Manager will inform the ADB user department for revision or withdrawal. If the application complies, the application will be included in the batch for circulation to the CRPP Working Group.

The CRPP Secretariat will circulate the applications to the CRPP Working Group and the CRPP Fund Manager will make a recommendation to the CRPP Working Group on each proposal based on three criteria: (i) alignment with the CRFP priorities, (ii) impact of the project on the fund(s), (iii) amount of cost financing, and (iv) overall responsiveness to the eligibility criteria. The CRPP Fund Manager will also advise the respective working groups on the availability of CRF resources.

The working groups will have seven working days for review, comment, and endorsement. The user departments will be informed about the comments received from the working group(s). If necessary, the application. Once completed, the CRPP Fund Manager will forward the applications to the CRFP Steering Committee for committee approval.


After approval by the CRFP Steering Committee for fund allocation, the proposals will be processed in accordance with the ADB procedures and guidelines on procurement, financial management, limitation, consulting services and procurement, social and environmental safeguards, financial management and reporting, and anticorruption and good governance.

Procedure for Allocation of Resources from CRFP IF: The GCF funds for the CRFP IF will be fungible within the CRFP TF. The allocation of resources pursuant to the AUMA. GCF use of funds for individual projects will be based on criteria outlined in this proposal which will be included in a dedicated document prepared by the CRFP Secretariat and approved by the CRFP Steering Committee. Allocation of resources from CRFP IF (which will blend ADB and GCF finance) will include the following steps:

* In Q1 of each year, the CRFP Secretariat in close consultation with the CRFP Partnership Group will prepare a work plan which would include the list of prioritized project proposals.

* ADB user departments will prepare project proposal using the application form (to be developed as part of Implementation Guidelines and draft concept paper in standard ADB concept paper format) and will submit them to the CRFP Secretariat, depending on the nature of application. Applications will be reviewed in batches.


The applications will be reviewed by the CRPP Secretariat to ensure that they comply with the eligibility criteria (Annex 2.4). If the application does not meet the criteria, the CRPP Fund Manager will inform the ADB user department for revision or withdrawal. If the application complies, the application will be included in the batch for circulation to the CRPP Working Group, as the case

The CRPP Fund Manager will make a recommendation to the CRPP Working Group on each proposal based on compliance with the eligibility criteria for the use of funds from CRPP IF resources to support the application.

The working groups will have seven working days for review, comment, and endorsement. Where needed, the CRPP Fund Manager may also advise the respective working groups on the availability of CRPP IF resources to support the application.

The user department will then have five working days to take the conversion action required by the CRPP Working Group recommendation. The CRPP Fund Manager will prepare the minutes of the decisions of the working group and forward the application for approval to the CRPP Steering Committee for funding allocation.

After approval by the CRPP Steering Committee for fund allocation, the proposals will be processed in accordance with all relevant policies and procedures for grant and loan approval, including without limitation, environmental and social safeguards, financial management and reporting, and anticorruption and governance requirements.

The CRPP Secretariat will provide semi-annual reporting to the GCF Secretariat so that the GCF Secretary can report on progress towards achieving the GCF Strategic Priorities 2020–2023, including climate resilience, and where appropriate recommended remedial measures. Such reporting will be in addition to annual reporting.

## Track Record of ADB

ADB will administer the Community Resilience Financing Partnership Facility, which will include the CRPP TF and the IF. ADB has vast experience in developing and financing climate adaptation projects and in administering Trust Funds for GCF.

### ADB’s Experience in scaling up in climate change

ADB has an extensive experience in developing financing and implementing climate adaptation projects in Asia and the Pacific region. ADB has approved its Strategy 2030, which recognizes “resilience” as a key objective for achieving sustainable and inclusive development. The strategy also sets out three strategic goals: (i) to increase resilience of communities by focusing on “Tackling climate change, building climate and disaster resilience, and enhancing environmental sustainability”; (ii) to support countries in their efforts to achieve the Paris Agreement targets related to climate financing through to ensure that (i) 75% of the number of its committed operations (on a three-year rolling basis) will be focused on adaptation and mitigation; (ii) 20% of ADB’s total portfolio will focus on climate change mitigation and adaptation by 2030; and (iii) “climate finance from ADB’s own resources will reach USD80 billion by 2030, with at least half of this amount to be spent in developing countries.” The strategy also sets out a target for scaling up climate finance from USD80 billion to USD100 billion by 2030, out of which around USD54 billion will support climate change adaptation. ADB’s Strategy 2030 is aligned with the GCF Strategic Priorities 2020–2023 and the goals of the Paris Agreement starting July 2023 for sovereign meetings. Meeting these climate targets will require expanding investments in climate adaptation and ensuring that the benefits of such investments reach vulnerable communities, especially women and girls.

ADB’s priorities on climate adaptation

ADB has been one of the leading MDBs on climate change. It has been implementing a climate risk management framework since 2014. Application of the framework is mandatory for all ADB projects and provides guidance to project teams in assessing risks at each stage of implementation. The framework allows integrating climate risk considerations systematically in ADB-financed projects, however, recognizing the importance of increasing climate risk and its huge impact on vulnerable communities, ADB has been working with partners to develop a more comprehensive approach for adaptation and addressing underlying drivers of vulnerability. It is expected that such projects can help steer development towards greater resilience and sustainability.

ADB’s strategy on climate change is aligned with GCF’s strategic priorities 2020–2023. It is important that such projects advance resilience in four areas in line with GCF’s strategic priorities 2020-2023.
