## B

### Key aspects of climate resilience (such as community-level infrastructure on cyclone preparedness; social and institutional resilience (such as adaptive social protection to support communities deal with droughts), each of which is a key aspect of adaptation at the local level, thus addressing the four key areas of risk (such as disaster microfractures). The four key aspects allow advancing all four adaptation targets of GCF's strategic priorities 2020-2023, namely, livelihoods and people and communities, health, food and water security, and climate change finance. However, while the GCF has demonstrated its ability to build countries' capacity in designing and implementing such holistic solutions, but there is a need to do more and scale up.

### ADB's approach to delivering adaptation at scale through partnerships

Partnerships are an important aspect of ADG Strategy 2030. In the context of adaptation, this becomes even more critical because of the new risks that climate change poses. These risks are not only environmental but also social, political, economic, cultural and public drivers that can contribute to vulnerabilities and thereby increasing climate risk. The GCF is working with a wide range of partners including governments, civil society organizations, global policy and action research organizations (such as the International Institute for Environment and Development), and coalitions of grassroots organizations and bilateral agencies (such as the Huonville Community Action Group in Australia).

### ADB experience in administering trust funds

ADB administered seven trust funds to support many of its programs, projects, technical assistance, and other activities. ADB administers contributions of financing partners for 10 trust funds. Of these, four are single-partner trust funds; two are multi-partner trust funds, and 12 are multi-trust fund partners, and 14 trust funds are under financing partnership facilities. ADB has also administered trust funds for the following: climate change adaptation and integration, urban development, and water. In 2019, ADB's trust funds received replenishments totaling USD$27.1 million and contributions for new trust funds amounting to USD$8.7 million.

### B.5 Justification for GCF funding request

#### Why GCF financing?

The CRFP IF will use financing from CCF in order to support:

- The poorest countries with high exposure to climate risks in implementing transformational adaptation measures. The CRFP aims to support adaptation projects in low-income developing countries that confront severe structural impediments to sustainable development and climate change adaptation. These countries have limited capacity (technical, financial and human) to undertake adaptation measures at scale: (i) small islands develop countries are particularly vulnerable because of their exposure to extreme weather events risks, and have a narrow resource base, thereby with high needs for adaptation; and (ii) in targeted poor countries, the poverty rate is very high, and there is limited capacity to implement adaptation as a climate priority, actions at scale remain limited due to lack of financing to support measures that aim for transformational change. Targeting the poorest and most vulnerable countries is in line with the use of GCF funds to address the needs of the most vulnerable communities and groups.

The focus on the poorest countries and communities becomes even more important in the post COVID-19 context, where the number of people living in extreme poverty is projected to increase. Domestic budgets are not available to meet this shortfall. Part of this shortfall will be addressed by concessional ADB funding with a combination of loan and grant depending on

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 60

B

**Projects promoting adaptation measures as public goods:** Investments in adaptation are often stymied by their public good nature. Such investments may be underfunded because investors do not take account of the benefits that accrue to others, and because they are difficult to privatize or commodify.

Moreover, as discussed earlier, a key barrier for scaling up adaptation is the fact that adaptation finance is not necessarily fungible with other types of climate finance. For example, it would be counterproductive to use systems through climate risk information, improved poverty and vulnerability databases for targeting, digital financial services, etc., to support mitigation projects. In this context, concessional financing from GCF will be critical to help implement adaptation measures that act as public goods, especially in the post-COVID-19 context where the financial capacity of countries are already stretched and there is a need to scale up adaptation.

**Projects implementing transformational adaptation measures:** The CRHPA has a focus on promoting transformational adaptation that addresses the nexus of climate change, gender and poverty i.e., measures that address the underlying drivers of vulnerability. Transformational adaptation requires fundamental processes and structures that construct vulnerability and allow it to accumulate and persist. Transformative adaptation is particularly important in contexts where adaptation is not limited to communities, since the barriers faced by these communities are not just restricted to technical and financial, but also social and political. This means that transformational adaptation will require a focus on allowing adoptive inclusive approaches, so that poor and vulnerable communities, especially women, have an equal voice at the table and are in a position of power to influence decisions, including processes where different interests may be in conflict.

Without financing, excepting grant financing from GCF, it will not be possible to promote such transformational measures. The proposed concessional finance from GCF through COP26 will be critical for this purpose. This is because the CRHPA has a focus on promoting adaptation that addresses the nexus of climate change, gender and poverty.

In turn, this will allow greater leveraging of ADB investment, thus increasing the total level of finance flowing to vulnerable groups. While incremental adoption is equity critical, it will be financed as part of ADB's regular financing.

**Project outputs with non-revenue elements:** Under-investment in adaptation is restricted by a typical problem that adaptation measures are often seen as having low returns on investment (ROI), yield assured near-term benefits, and generate positive streams of direct or indirect income, particularly in the case of market-based solutions. However, the benefits of adaptation projects are realized in the form of reduced losses (which may not be reaped for many years), rather than streams of positive income. Thus, grant resources from GCF will be critical to support such projects and ensure that they are financially viable. This is particularly true with regard to investment, especially where this is funding non-market elements (e.g., nature-based solutions, and thus green infrastructure) which have a longer time horizon for benefits generation.

Moreover, such measures often generate co-benefits, such as alleviating poverty, enhancing development outcomes or other welfare benefits, which need to be better identified and captured to encourage greater investments.

**Overcoming risk perceptions of community level adaptation interventions:** Implementing adaptation measures at the local level is challenging because many remain underfunded. Furthermore, while local measures have high transaction cost, are subject to local governance-related challenges and favour of supporting national-level projects that align to existing political economy. However, the benefits of such investments are often realized in terms of reduced losses (which may not be reaped for many years), rather than streams of positive income.

Thus, grant resources from GCF will be critical if long-term sustainability is to be ensured. The CRHPA IFP can demonstrate the benefits of decentralized and inclusive solutions that are designed based on principles of subsidiarity. Thus...

## 6.6 Exit strategy

The CRPP is designed to promote sustainability through the adoption of the following approaches:

- The CRPP will support outputs that are closely aligned with national climate priorities and to address climate impacts that hinder the achievement of poverty reduction.
- With a key focus on mainstreaming, the CRPP TF will work with countries to ensure that climate change adaptation becomes an integral part of development planning and implementation. This will be done by strengthening policies and systems for the use of climate risk information for decision-making, with policy makers, planners, and local government and civil society organizations.
- The CRPP TF will also support the design and implementation of climate-resilient projects and programs through the use of climate risk information for decision making. With a focus on mainstreaming policies and systems for the use of climate risk information for decision-making, with policy makers, planners, and local government and civil society organizations.
- The CRPP TF will ensure that all interventions contribute to national development priorities and climate resilience, and is taken up nationally by relevant agencies. Country ownership will be achieved through a wide range of stakeholders including governments, international financial institutions, civil society organizations, communities, financial intermediaries, and the private sector. With such a wide-ranging approach, it is expected that the CRPP TF will have a significant impact on national development priorities.
- Scaling up adaptation in the context of pro-poor development: The CRPP is based on the understanding that poverty reduction strategies/programs financed by national or local budgets will not be achieved due to climate change impacts. Therefore, the CRPP TF will work with countries to ensure that climate change adaptation becomes an integral part of development planning and implementation.
- Accordingly, the CRPP TF will work with countries to integrate the CRPP activities fully with such ongoing programs and projects as well as with national strategies and plans for poverty reduction.
- If will have to demonstrate that proposed adaptation outputs are embedded within large programs that are being partly financed by government, and therefore will be continued beyond CRPP in time. For example, it may support the integration of climate change adaptation into national strategies and plans and designs and will extend beyond CRPP life span.

Promoting adaptation through national curriculum system: The CRPP recognizes that increasing climate resilience requires building skills among poor and vulnerable communities on addressing climate risk and exploring opportunities. The CRPP TF prioritizes development of new life skills and capacity-building activities to ensure that the poor and vulnerable are better prepared for climate change. The integration of these elements into national curriculum being largely financed by government budget, ensures that such activities will be continued beyond CRPP in time. For example, it may support integration of climate smart agricultural value chain practices in the curriculum of government agriculture training colleges.

## Strengthening National and Local Institutions

A key focus of CRFP is to strengthen the capacity of national and local institutions in order for them to develop and implement climate solutions and ensure that adaptation finance reaches the poor. While the CRFP IF aims to do so by building capacity of NADs, all projects seeking financing from CRFP IF will have a key focus on strengthening capacity of national and local institutions. This is done through a combination of technical assistance, capacity development, and good governance so that benefits of adaptation reach all in a just and equitable manner and continues beyond COP21. The CRFP IF also aims to ensure that the projects are designed with an explicit consideration for taking information in planning and budgeting processes in order to inform capital and recurrent expenditure.

## Engagement of Communities, especially Women

Meaningful participation of communities, especially women, is a key focus of CRFP IF. This is particularly true for CRFP which has a large focus on demonstrating social, economic and environmental co-benefits of adaptation projects. The CRFP IF aims to ensure that the projects are designed with an explicit consideration for taking information in planning processes in order to inform capital and recurrent expenditure.

The CRFP TF has a dedicated output on strengthening engagement of communities and building their capacity, especially capacity of grassroots organizations through local policy and planning reforms. The CRFP IF will support this by providing technical assistance to governments and civil society organizations in developing the project and has an explicit role for communities in implementing projects. This is done with the aim that the projects are designed in a way which can be easily contribute to its sustainability. For example, co-design of neighborhood scale ecosystem-based water management systems with local communities will ensure that they have ownership over these measures and suitability of the measures and that local communities are involved in their maintenance beyond the project period.

## Creating Opportunities for Scaling Up

A key focus of the CRFP TF is to support governments and communities in accessing adaptation finance, including from the private sector. The financing support from CRFP TF will be provided through two main channels: (i) by supporting projects that have already been implemented or are being implemented; and (ii) by providing technical assistance for developing new projects. In both cases, it brings an explicit focus to the need for more such projects nationally, which could better leverage and use government funds.

The leveraging of CRF resources alongside ADB will create a critical mass in terms of scale and impact that can be achieved through this type of support. For example, projects on ecosystem-based adaptation will explore the potential of strengthening forest governance and land tenure systems to ensure long-term sustainability and explores innovative options for revenue generation such as leveraging ecotourism initiatives.

# EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

## D.1. Investment Criteria

The impact of the CRPP will be contributing to a paradigm shift towards climate resilient sustainable development in Asia and the Pacific region. The program will achieve this impact by strengthening the resilience of communities, households, and institutions at all levels (national, sub-national, local) across Asia and the Pacific region through implementing outputs related to: (i) strengthening knowledge and action research on climate change adaptation; (ii) building institutional capacity for delivering climate-resilient investments in the public sector; (iii) building community-level capacity to develop and deliver climate adaptive investments at community-level; (iv) identification and preparation of projects that will be delivered through the CRPP, including those that are expected to contribute to delivering applied climate-risk informed investments at scale; (v) implementing pro-poor climate resilient livelihood strategies and practices; and (vi) implementing climate adaptation pro-poor infrastructure. In doing so, it is expected that the CRPP will contribute to a reduction in poverty and vulnerability of communities and people and their livelihoods posed by increased weather extremes and slow-onset climate related hazards thereby contributing to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The CRPP will also support large-scale transformations and a core enabler of climate-resilient development outcomes and sustainable poverty reduction, as outlined in the ADB’s 2015-2023 Strategy Agreement and the key recommendations from the WBG contributions to the IPCC 6th Assessment Report.

The pathway towards the achievement of improved well-being includes the implementation of local site specific projects that will directly benefit at least 10 million people, collectively supported by the ADB. On completion of these projects, it is expected that a minimum of 3,219,400 of the poorest and most vulnerable people in the CRPP countries will be directly benefited from the project activities. Direct beneficiaries are considered to be those people that are specifically targeted by the intervention and participate in or are recipients of, goods (e.g. agricultural extension), services (e.g. early warning climate information services) or other outputs provided as part of the project implementation. The CRPP will cover three IFU output areas, during the lifetime of the projects and are expected to have a measurable impact beyond the project period. The following table provides a breakdown of minimum expected direct beneficiaries per CRPP country.

| Table 3: Direct beneficiaries disaggregated by country |
| --- |
| Country | No. of Direct Beneficiaries | % of direct beneficiaries of total population |
| Cambodia | 1,300,000 | 45% |
| Lao PDR | 1,200,000 | 0.45% |
| Indonesia | 141,000 | 0.29% |
| Pakistan | 486,000 | 0.23% |

In addition to those directly benefiting from the activity under each project, there will be a number of people that benefit indirectly. It is expected that a minimum of 4,460,000 people will indirectly benefit from the program. Indirect beneficiaries are those people that are not specifically engaged by the project activities and services but who live in close proximity to the project area and are likely to be exposed to its benefits or climate change impacts. These indirect beneficiaries may include: (i) households living within the coverage area of a project; (ii) households living outside the coverage area, but whose livelihoods will be affected by the project activities; or (iii) vulnerable people eligible for potential social protection payments based on upgraded targeting criteria. The number of indirect beneficiaries is estimated to be 10% of direct beneficiaries per country and are estimated for each project during the detailed design phase and reported on in the annual reporting to GCF. ## D.2. Paradigm shift potential

The CRPPF will catalyze following impact that will help achieve the paradigm shift of Increased Climate Resilient Sustainable Development in Asia and the Pacific:

### Scaling-up and replication
As described earlier, finance for local climate adaptation remains limited, especially finances that targets the poorest communities. The CRPPF aims to demonstrate replicable solutions at scale by scaling up what works best within the region. The CRPPF only support projects that demonstrate the potential of being replicated elsewhere in the same country or other countries with similar contexts. If a project is not scalable and replicable, it will not be supported.

### Adaptation finance
The CRPPF aims to catalyze a paradigm shift towards climate resilient sustainable development. GCF funding will be used together with the existing resources of the CRPPF to overcome the barrier by mobilizing resources close around USD750 million for adaptation solutions that can be scaled up and replicated in other countries. This is an order of magnitude increase compared to what is times many the original investment. Further, with a strong focus on partnering the CRPPF aims at leveraging and influencing further resources through national budgets, private sector, bi-lateral development partners, etc.

### Creation of enabling environment (sustainability of outcomes)
The CRPPF will support outputs and outcomes that are closely aligned with national climate priorities. The CRPP TF will therefore catalyze


---
**Page 73**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 69

impacts become a one-off project investment and deliver a paradigm shift by ensuring that climate risk informed development becomes the norm. It will do so by strengthening policies and regulatory frameworks that promote resilience, while also improving access to climate information and decision-making with policy makers, planners, and local governments and communities.

This will ensure the sustainability of outcomes beyond completion of the project. The GCF financing will be used for a 10 year period per country, which is longer than most other funding mechanisms. This allows for the context of localization and decentralization. By strengthening national and local institutions, the CRPP IF will enable countries to take up climate change adaptation as an integral part of their development agenda.

The CRPP IF will be taken up nationwide by relevant agencies. Countries' ownership will be achieved through stakeholder engagement at all levels, including government, civil society organizations, communities, financial intermediaries, and the private sector. With such a wide ranging target, the CRPP IF is expected to have significant impact on climate change adaptation.

## Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

With a key focus on mainstreaming, the CRPP will promote the integration of climate change considerations into policy and decision-making processes. It has an explicit objective to shift to adaptive programming under output 4, increasing the use of climate risk information in development planning at all levels, including national, sub-national, local government and civil society organizations. It will also improve climate risk information systems and integrate them with other relevant systems. The CRPP IF will support countries in building capacity for decentralized decision-making processes to support governments build robust, science-based local climate assessment frameworks. This will include the development of tools and methodologies that can be used by communities, and information needed to inform local planning and investment.

The focus on such actions is expected to lead to a more resilient society, with greater ability to adapt to climate change impacts. The CRPP IF will be supported by the IPCC AR6 as highly likely to support system transitions and contribute to transformational change[18].

## Innovative solutions and approaches

The CRPP builds on the lessons learned from the implementation of large-scale resilience programs, which highlight the need for flexible measurement mechanisms.

These lessons include: (i) that adaptation is not just about climate but how it is supported; including longer-term commitment; (ii) the need for adapting flexible programming approaches, including long-term commitments and a focus on building capacity to adapt; (iii) the importance of testing ideas, learning, and evaluations. Accordingly, the CRPP is planned for a 10 year implementation period per country, which will allow for the development of innovative solutions and approaches that are tailored to development of investment ideas; and making resources available through a combination of technical assistance and financing.

The CRPP IF will be designed as an integrated approach with three components: (i) knowledge sharing and learning component; (ii) climate risk information component; and (iii) the adaptive management principles at a program level. The CRPP IF will ensure that knowledge emerging from the implementation of the CRPP IF is shared, learned from, and applied to other investments in the country.

As a result, it is anticipated that the GCF financed CRPP IF, through the size and the type of investments, will deliver transformational change.

## Sharing of knowledge and learning

The CRPP IF has dedicated activities on knowledge and learning which are critical for capacity generation and sharing of knowledge on climate risk and resilience. It will include a monitoring, evaluation and learning component, which will build collective learning processes, and ensure that lessons learned from the implementation of the CRPP IF can be used to inform and support adaptive management principles at a program level. The CRPP IF will enable that knowledge emerging from the implementation of the CRPP IF is shared, learned from, and applied to other investments in the country.

The design of the CRPP IF means there will be very large potential for learning and replication, with the transfer of experience across countries.

## Table 5

Table 5 shows how proposed GCF CRPP financing outputs (described in section B.3) will complement past and ongoing similar initiatives in the CRPP IF countries that are financed by UNFCCC-related funds and contribute to the paradigm shift. It should be noted that because of the nature of current and past projects it just is not possible to provide a comprehensive list under CRPP IF. The proposed GCF financing for each country will result from comprehensive consultation with government counterparts, civil groups, and other stakeholders in order to ensure replicability and scaling-up of other interventions based on lessons learned and national priorities.

## D3. Sustainable development

Through investments in local infrastructure, the CRPP directly targets the pillars of sustainable development: social and economic.

### Environmental co-benefits.
The CRPP TF will support countries to increase awareness and build capacity to develop and implement large-scale adaptation projects, including projects that promote ecosystem-based approaches (EBA) and integrated coastal zone management (ICZM), as well as climate-smart agriculture projects. The CRPP IF will support implementation of such measures in the context of poverty reduction programs in rural and urban areas, thereby promoting environmental co-benefits. Such measures become critical for ensuring that vulnerable groups, particularly women, youth, indigenous peoples, and other marginalized populations are dependent on natural assets. Since such livelihoods are often impacted by climate change, it is important to ensure that they are resilient to the impacts of climate change, where appropriate (judged by decision on scale and subsidiarity) to strengthen resilience of poor population.

In this regard, CRPP TF will support countries in implementing a range of adaptation measures, such as: (i) community infrastructure - adapting natural assets in place of/in mix with grey infrastructure to reduce vulnerability; (ii) climate-smart agriculture practices - promoting the adoption of climate-smart agricultural practices; (iii) well-being - promoting protection of natural assets as part of social protection programs; and (iv) governance and decentralization - increasing role of women's group in protecting natural resources etc.

### Social co-benefits.
All interventions supported by CRPP TF and IF will have strong social benefits, because of the explicit focus on low income and disadvantaged groups, and the focus on poverty reduction and achieving gender equality. The CRPP TF and IF will also support countries to achieve greater distributional benefits by targeting these groups, increasing the flows of finance and responsibility to them, and ensuring that they are not left behind in the process of climate change adaptation. This will result in social capital. The explicit targeting of women through a gender window will also lead to additional social benefits, including: (i) promoting gender equality; (ii) promoting resilience of vulnerable groups; (iii) co-benefits, including (i) adapting and shock-response social protection; (ii) community-driven development in rural and urban areas; and (iii) creating resilient livelihoods support.

### Climate-resilient investments.
The CRPP TF will make a strong case for climate-resilient investments to be promoted by CRPP TF and supported by CRPP IF, which is captured through the triple dividend of investing in resilience. This includes: (i) the economic activity stimulated by reduced impacts; forward-looking planning, long-term capital investment, and improved risk management; (ii) the social benefits from increased access to services and resilience investments, including positive spillovers as well as net non-market benefits. Moreover, with a focus on gender equality, the CRPP will promote bottom-up processes, which are critical for inclusive sustainable development.

### Gender-responsive development impact.
The CRPP TF seeks to address the gender-climate-sustainability perspective by promoting the inclusion of women in all aspects across the project cycle. The Investment Fund that combines to address structural drivers of women's inequality, women's disproportionate vulnerability and exposure to climate change, and women's under-representation in decision-making. The Investment Fund must either be categorized as Gender Equality Theme (GET) or Effective Gender Mainstreaming (EGM), meaning that specific targeted activities that address issues of women's equality must be identified in the project cycle.

## Project Green Climate Action Plans (GPAs), with associated budget allocation and monitoring and reporting plans.

For GPA projects, the project's results framework must include outcome indicators that are specific to poverty reduction, gender equality, and climate change adaptation.

The CRPP Trust Fund has dedicated resources for undertaking upstream analytical and capacity building work in assessing and identifying appropriate strategies and interventions. The types of appropriate strategies and interventions that will be supported by the CRPP Trust Fund are articulated at a higher level in the CRPP's Gender Action Plan (GAP) presented in annex 8.2.

### Promoting gender equality is a core thrust of the CRPP and which will be promoted through all projects supported under this funding window.

First, women’s groups should be involved as active participants in the identification and implementation of adaptation measures. Second, women’s groups should be included in decision-making processes for climate change adaptation, thereby shifting a view from women's status as victims and passive beneficiaries to stakeholders and drivers of resilient development. Such examples are also visible in the current pandemic response where women have been at the forefront of providing care and support services.

It is thus important that such women’s leadership roles are promoted in different investments strategies and projects supported under this funding window, including through gender-sensitive budgeting. Women-focused investment and resource flows are also necessary to break generations of gender inequality and ensure that women can participate fully as equal partners in the development process and its adaptive capacity. An annex on Gender assessment and program-level action plan is included as an Annex.

### Thus, the CRPP will deliver against several of the SDGs, including 1 (no poverty), 3 (good health and wellbeing), 5 (gender equality), 10 (reduced inequalities), 11 (sustainable cities and communities) and 13 (climate action).

#### D.4. Data on recipients

Addressing resilience needs of the poor and vulnerable population.

The recipients of the program are the most vulnerable groups in society, those groups that are affected by climate change, do not have the finance or capacity to absorb, adapt and recover from climate shocks and stresses, and need support to strengthen their assets, livelihoods and coping mechanisms. The CRPP will provide grants for investments in adaptation projects, as well as strengthening institutions and systems, to allow climate finance to reach the poor and vulnerable groups. This is a more transformative overall effect, because it allows the targeting of multiple barriers. Thus, there is a high potential for reaching the least developed countries, the small island developing states, and the poorest in middle income countries.

**Cambodia:**

Cambodia is an LDC in Southeast Asia with a population of 16.3 million in 2018, 76 percent of its population lives in rural areas and the urban concentration is located in the most fertile river valleys and flood plains. About 45% of Cambodia’s population live below poverty line (World Bank, 2019). The country has been falling back into poverty when exposed to economic and other external shocks. Cambodia's population relies heavily on agriculture for its livelihoods, with about 78 percent of the rural population working in this sector, representing nearly 49% of the country’s labor force. Climate change shocks are projected to victimize 61% of the rural Cambodian population by 2050 (World Bank, 2019). The agricultural sector is also vulnerable to climate change and industry. Women are similarly more disadvantaged, especially in decision-making and income disparity.

The CRPP Trust Fund has provided support for a number of projects that have been impacted by climate shocks and the COVID-19 pandemic; such the constraints on the public budget management requires outsourced assistance especially in dealing with uplifting conditions of the rural poor.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 79

## Indonesia has the largest economy in Southeast Asia. With high economic growth, the poverty rates in the country have been reduced from 24% in 1998 to 15% in 2016. Nevertheless, poverty remains a serious issue, especially in absolute numbers, because of the large population size, and thus numbers tens of millions of people. Poverty rates are higher particularly in remote areas and among certain groups. While Indonesia has undertaken several poverty reduction programs including social protection programs and urban informal settlements upgrading under the poverty line and roughly equal percentage being near poor. Prior to COVID-19, it estimated 61.8 million people were living below the national poverty line (40% of the population) accounting for about 25% of total consumption, whereas the top 20% consumes 45.6%. Unemployment is also a major concern in Indonesia, with an unemployment rate of around 7%, and this is expected to increase as the country urbanizes and thus an increasingly important target group in Indonesia’s poverty reduction programs, including social protection programs and urban informal settlements upgrading. These programs do not consider long-term changes in climate and thus the impacts generated by these programs will be affected by climate change. The government has recognized that such poverty reduction programs integrate climate resilience considerations to build adaptive capacity of local communities.

## Lao PDR is a landlocked country in Southeast Asia and is one of the LDCs. It is a highly vulnerable country with low readiness. While the country’s economy has been growing, delicately balanced macroeconomic conditions have made it difficult for the government to invest in climate change adaptation programs. The challenges to the Lao PDR's development? A large percentage of population remain close to the poverty line and are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts. Agriculture accounts for 16% of GDP and engages, to various degrees, over 65% of the population (of whom more than half are women). The large section of the rural population engaged in agriculture are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts. Climate change is expected to have a negative impact on agricultural production and inadequate social protection system. With projected increase in temperature and intensified precipitation patterns, there will be an increased risk of flooding and droughts. These changes can lead to food shortages by floods, increased crop evapotranspiration in the dry season with associated higher demand for water, more frequent and severe heat waves, and increased frequency of extreme weather events such as flash flood or destructive floods. Investments will be needed to support the rural poor in climate resilient livelihoods and to improve their access to social protection systems. The government has recognized that investments are needed in strengthening climate services for improved decision-making. As an LDC, Lao PDR lacks the financial resources required to implement adaptation measures on its own. In 2019, the country’s budget mean Government cannot afford the adaptation funding gap. Moreover, the COVID-19 crisis is expected to have a significant impact on the economy and public finances, with poverty rates expected to rise deeper, or back into poverty. Such future funding exacerbates pre-existing vulnerabilities, for example, the food insecurity in the northern rural provinces, being already a critical issue as several months rice harvest has been delayed due to the COVID-19 pandemic, and the lack of school meals for children having learning setback.

## Pakistan is characterized by diverse topography, ecosystems, and climatic zones. Both floods and droughts impact heavily on agriculture and are expected to increase in climate shocks in the world today. As of 2015 approximately 24% of the population still lived below the national poverty line and 20% of the population resided in areas with high vulnerability to climate change impacts. The country’s economy is dominated by agriculture, which accounts for about 40 percent of GDP and employs over 40 percent of the population will be living below the poverty line. Agriculture is still the largest employer, and it is also a major source of income for many households in Pakistan. However, it is expected that agricultural growth will lag if foodconvores continue and disrupt needed transition towards more climate-resilient livelihoods. The country’s vulnerability to climate change impacts is ranked as one of the most stressed country, ranked 14th globally in terms of water stress. The target area for the project is the coastal areas where poverty rates are among the highest in the country. These internal information is accessible to ADB Management and staff. ## Papua New Guinea
Located in the South West Pacific, PNG comprises the eastern half of New Guinea island, including the provinces of Milne Bay, Morobe, New Britain, and Bougainville, and around 900 smaller islands and atolls. PNG is a highly climate vulnerable country[1] and is also considered to be a Small Island Developing State (SIDS). The country has a low Human Development Index, and high poverty rates between 30-40% and an adult literacy rate of around 50%. A large proportion of the population lives in rural areas where subsistence agriculture and small-scale mining are primary livelihood activities in rural areas. Estimate suggest that 70% of households in PNG live below the poverty line, with a significant portion living on less than $1 per day. The lack of infrastructure and dependence on climate sensitive livelihoods all heighten the vulnerability of the local population to climate change impacts such as droughts, floods, cyclones, landslides (both natural or climate or other shocks) has an immediate and significant impact on food security.[2] Climate change is likely to increase hazards, and without adaptation measures, the exposure of the rural community will further increase. The country’s vulnerability to climate change is also reflected in its high poverty rate for the most vulnerable, and the constraints on the public budget mean Government cannot lift the adaptation burden from the most vulnerable. In 2019, the government estimated that 58% of the population (those who lost their jobs) and poverty (increase by 4.5 percentage points). The 2020 fiscal balance is estimated to women – from 37.6% in 2019 to 42.1%. In 2020, the public sector debt is expected to reach 49% of GDP in 2020, compared with 38.8% in 2019.

## Timor-Leste
occupies the eastern half of the island of Timor, which it shares with the Indonesian province of East Nusa Tenggara. The country is landlocked and has a mountainous terrain. The country is mountainous. The country is one of the poorest countries in Asia and is a LDC. Data for 2016 estimate that 35% of Timor-Leste’s population lives below the poverty line, with an estimated USD$190/day, and 84.8% were classified as living in multi-dimensional poverty.[3] Such conditions make the population more vulnerable to shocks and stresses. Agriculture accounts for 9% of GDP, and 41% of employment is in agriculture. The country’s agricultural sector is dominated by small-scale subsistence agriculture is subsistence farming, with coffee being the only major crop grown for export. Rural areas suffer from food insecurity due to low productivity and poor access to markets. The majority of the population are coupled with extreme climate events.[4] Gender analysis shows that for women, these circumstances are far worse than men as they have limited access to resources such as land, water, and credit. Women can be characterized as low-input, low-output producers. Although the Timor-Leste people have some capacity to adapt to climate change impacts, their ability is constrained by a lack of access to natural resources, aggravated by their limited access to alternative livelihoods. Traditional gender roles gender roles in agriculture are also a barrier to adaptation and women’s participation in agricultural forecasting hinder adaptive actions. With agricultural systems, including the livelihood conditions and food insecurity issues, expected to be severely affected by both by extremes in extreme events, as well as by slow onset hazards such as droughts and floods. The country is highly vulnerable to climate change; it faces both slow and rapid onset meteorological hazards. Disasters resulting from these climate hazards are a very significant cost for the country. In 2019, disaster-related expenses cost on average an estimated $6 of GDP every year. As an island-nation, the country is particularly at risk from sea-level rise. Vanuatu lacks the financial resources to address climate threats, especially for the most vulnerable and the country’s capacity to adapt is severely constrained by a lack of resources to close its adaptation funding gap. The country’s GDP is likely to contract by 9% in 2020 due to reduced tourism.

## Vanuatu
is a small island developing state in the Pacific that is characterized by low-lying areas that have high levels of climate risk, especially to tropical cyclones. Vanuatu was ranked as the country most at risk from climate change impacts and is one of the most vulnerable countries in the world.[5] The country faces both slow and rapid onset meteorological hazards. Disasters resulting from these climate hazards are a very significant cost for the country. In 2019, disaster-related expenses cost on average an estimated $6 of GDP every year. As an island-nation, the country is particularly at risk from sea-level rise. Vanuatu lacks the financial resources to address climate threats, especially for the most vulnerable and the country’s capacity to adapt is severely constrained by a lack of resources to close its adaptation funding gap. The country’s GDP is likely to contract by 9% in 2020 due to reduced tourism.

## D.S. country ownership

This document has been reviewed and approved by seven countries. Consultations have been undertaken with stakeholders from Cambodia, Lao PDR, Indonesia, Pakistan, PNG, Timor Leste, and Vanuatu. For these countries, ADB has investments in the pipeline that have the potential to meet the objectives of the CRPP-in-depth consultations were held at national level with government officials, development partners, civil society organizations, and grassroots women's organizations, in order to understand local needs and most importantly the type of transactions that would be most beneficial.

In order to ensure scale, replicability and sustainability, implementation of the CRPP will be country-led and tailored to fit country and local context, recognizing the broader political economy context. Thus the outputs should be aligned with national priorities (as outlined in National Adaptation Programmes of Action [NAPAs] and national poverty reduction priorities (as outlined in national development plans). For more information, please refer to Annex 2.

### Cambodia

Cambodia has prioritized climate adaptation as a priority and to reduce its vulnerability. It has identified several key areas for action under the National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) and Climate Change Strategic Plan (CCSP), 2014-2023, and has embedded adaptation in the National Sustainable Development Plan (NSDP). The government has also developed a climate change strategy and action plan. Cambodia has identified entry points for a coherent approach to integrate with the national development processes. Within the framework of the NSDP, the government has prioritized 15 key areas for climate change adaptation. The implementation of the adaptation actions under the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) has been formulated and to be integrated into the NSDP. Cambodia has also identified entry points for a coherent approach to integrate with the national development processes.

The government has identified priority areas for climate change adaptation, including promoting and scaling up smart farming systems and promotion of post-harvest technology. In 2020, Cambodia has developed an Updated National Adaptation Programme on increased priority actions and sectors: water resources, forestry, coastal and agriculture, health, biodiversity, infrastructure, livelihoods and tourism. The government has identified the need to improve the climate risk management capacity in order to integrate the NAP into the NSDP. The government is also working towards integrating as part of the National Programme for Sub National Democratic Development (NPNSDD) process.

### Indonesia

Indonesia has put in place ambitious climate adaptation actions outlined in National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC). It has identified key sectors: water resources, forestry and land use, agriculture, coastal and marine, infrastructure, energy, health, education, transport, and disaster risk management. The NAPCC also identifies critical areas for strengthening resilience of the poor and vulnerable population. In governance, the NAP-PR promotes strengthening of institutional capacity to manage climate change risks and enhance the adaptive capacity of the poor and vulnerable population. Further, the recently approved Rencana Pembangunan Jangka Menengah Nasional (RPJMN) 2019–2024 has identified key sectors for adaptation: water resources, forestry and land use, infrastructure development as a development of living environment to increase resilience to disaster and climate change. The government has also developed the National Climate Change Action Plan on protection in resilience building and the government has developed the Adaptive Social Protection Roadmap.

### Lao PDR

Lao PDR has prioritized increasing climate resilience in agriculture, forest land-use and strengthening livelihoods of vulnerable groups. It has identified key sectors: water resources, forestry and land use, small-scale farming systems, strengthening resilience of infrastructure, and enhancing water resources information system. Additionally, the NDC recognizes building capacity will be crucial for effective implementation of


---
**Page 86**
---

adaptation strategies, including capacity for risk-informed decision-making at all levels from national to local. Further, the Agricultural Development Strategy to 2025 and Vision 2030 have prioritized water resources management to support the sustainable use of land and dry-season crops. The government priorities include: (i) reducing rural development by: (ii) increasing forest and vegetation cover; (iii) developing water management infrastructure, including irrigation systems.

Timor Leste in the Dili Declaration on Climate Change Adaptation recognizes that strong interdependence of climate adaptation and development target, and that the national development priorities can only be sustained if they are adapted to the impacts of climate change. The government has therefore given priority in order to reduce vulnerability of farmers to drought and flood by improving their capacity to plan for and manage these risks. This includes: (i) increasing forest and vegetation cover; (ii) developing water resources management infrastructure, including irrigation systems.

Pakistan in its updated NDC (2021) adopts a long-term vision of mainstreaming climate resilience considerations in the economically and socially vulnerable sectors of the economy. In particular, it identifies adaptation as one of the key strategies for achieving this vision. The National Climate Change Policy (2012) spells out the policy measures for adaptation in vulnerable ecosystems and livelihoods. The National Adaptation Plan (NAP) 2015-2030 (UNFCCC, 2016) provides a detailed list of actions with timeframe for implementation, including priorities related to water sector, agriculture and livestock, marine and coastal ecosystems, and disaster preparedness. The transition plan for the NAP 2015-2030 is being developed in 2021 which will prioritize four key sectors that include agriculture, infrastructure, transport and health.

Vanuatu: The objectives of the GRRP are aligned with NAPA (2007), the Vanuatu Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction Policy (2016-2030) and the Vanuatu Recovery Strategy 2020 – 2025 (VRS, 2020). The GRRP is aligned with the NAPA (2007), the VCRDPR (2016–2030) and the VRS (2020). Disaster Risk Reduction Policy (2016-2030) has identified community focus is one of its principles of implementation. The GRRP will be implemented through a multi-sectoral approach, with emphasis on building capacity at all levels to address climate change impacts through bottom up approaches, drawing on local skills, values and traditional knowledge, and enhancing decision-making processes.

Consultation with NDAs: In the course of development of this proposal ADB has officially coordinated with the NDAs, including having consultations with NDAs and relevant sector ministry to explain the program priorities and ensure their priorities reflect such needs in the proposal. The NDAs were provided with copies of the draft project document for review and comments.


---
**Page 87**
---

### D.8. Effectiveness

The projects funded under the CRPP will be subject to ADB’s economic appraisal criteria and will have to meet an economic internal rate of return (EIRR) threshold. This means that in present value terms, the total economic benefits must exceed the total economic costs (when analysed in accordance with ADB’s 2017 Guidelines for the Economic Analysis of Projects).

The projects will also be subject to ADB's requirements for financial management and analysis. For each project, financial analyses will be prepared in accordance with ADB’s Financial Management and Analysis of Projects (FMAP) guidelines. The results of these analyses, including the EIRR calculations, will be clearly presented and will be reasonable; (i) the internal financial rate of return (FIRR) calculated on a discounted cash flow basis must be greater than 10% in present value terms, or if the FIRR is less than this threshold, it should be greater than the weighted average cost of capital; and (ii) the FIRR will be robust under various sensitivity tests.

The CRPP programmatic approach proposed will deliver very high efficiency and effectiveness, because the GCF funds will be leveraged against large volumes of ADB financing. At the CRPP IF level, the program will use a co-financing ratio of 5:1 (ADB:GCF). This means that for every $1 in GCF funding, there is an additional $5 in a ratio of co-financing of more than 5:1, which is high for adaptation, especially when targeting the most vulnerable. This will mean the GCF investments will benefit from existing ADB finance management and procurement systems, and streamlined transaction costs for implementing GCF funds will therefore be extremely low (and highly efficient).

The use of GCF finance to fund the climate additionally on top of existing ADB funds will also be extremely efficient because it will allow for a much larger scale of adaptation interventions. This will lead to large-scale conversion of inputs to outputs and outcomes. This means the GCF funds will deliver a very large number of benefits at relatively low cost.

The projects that are put forward for financing under CRPP IF will be scrutinised against strict economic and financial viability prior to funding, and only those that pass these criteria will be taken forward. The exact (EIRR) threshold is not yet determined as it depends on the specific project stage or type of intervention or individual pipeline projects. However, a review of the economic benefits of community-level adaptations, as well as the three main proposed outcomes areas where GCF funds will be used, was undertaken. The conclusions are summarised below.

**Community level adaptation:** There are a wide set of interventions that can help community-based resilience, including local and community-based adaptation planning and financing. Evaluations of these projects have shown that they can deliver significant benefits in terms of cost-effectiveness (e.g., post-analysis of 23 field test pilots). It is also highlighted that interventions that target the most vulnerable are more likely to be effective because they are more likely to be implemented with greater attention paid to account in economic appraisal (in cost-benefit analysis) by using equity (or distributive) weights to adjust project costs and benefits. Using equity weights, though such an approach would further increase the benefits of the proposed interventions below.

**Output 4: Improved information and systems for delivering applied climate-risk-informed investments at scale:** These activities are primarily focused on integrating climate risk information into development projects and programmes. A number of tools have been developed to support this process and have been recommended as a form of climate sensitive development for small and frequent shocks in developing countries. The CRPP IF will include the following: (i) an analysis of existing tools, including one review study looking in detail at 8 studies (4 ex ante, 4 ex post) found BCG funds that were as high as €6.71; (ii) a review of the potential for scaling up these tools and their application to other climate-related activities. Numerous studies have identified the advantages and benefits of making social protection programs more climate-resilient. However, there is also evidence that such interventions can sometimes undermine social protection program effectiveness. The intervention will include the use of climate information, including weather and climate services (including early warning), for social protection. Analysis


---
**Page 88**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 84

**D**

of such approaches find high benefit to cost ratios (BCfRs) and recent reviews identify on average a benefit to cost ratio of 9:1.* Benefits increase with increasing climate change (and shock) and thus these interventions are highly efficient as well as effective.

Output 5: Climate resilient pre-poor livelihoods investment. These activities are focused on additional agricultural productivity and price stability investments. These include a wide range of types of interventions but are often focused on agriculture. They can include activities that build the resilience of current farming systems, such as improved irrigation infrastructure, climate smart livestock, and climate smart agriculture; and these interventions have been found to have high benefit to cost ratios across 32 country-level projects, up to x:1 (and EIRRs between 15% and 30%).** The investments can also include activities that move existing livelihoods away from areas most exposed to climate change threats. This may include moving to new resilient varieties, or developing new climate resilient livelihoods, which are also found to generate positive benefits to cost ratios.

Output 6: Climate adaptation pro-poor infrastructure investment. This activity is focused on climate adaptation and resilience of infrastructure. These investments have been found to have very positive economic returns. A systematic review*** of flood risk management appraisal (27 studies, mix ex ante and ex post) found a BCR of 10.5 and an EIRR of 34%.**** The review also found that the average benefit-cost ratio from 32 studies and databases in 16 countries***** found a mean BCR of 6. These economic benefits have been found to be particularly high for small-scale infrastructure projects, such as those supported by the GCF funds will be targeting green infrastructure for resilience (ecosystem-based adaptation). These projects can include activities that build the resilience of existing infrastructure and systems, flood risk management, and even small-scale local schemes. The review of the literature finds these have high benefit to cost ratios, up to 10:1 (and EIRRs between 25% and 37%).******

Importantly, these schemes have high co-benefits, which include environmental benefits (including carbon sequestration), and these can be particularly high for local ecosystem-based community-based adaptation.

## LOGICAL FRAMEWORK

### E.1 Project/Programme Focus
- **Reduced emissions (mitigation)**
- **Enhanced resilience and adaptive capacity**

### E.2 GCF Impact level: Paradigm shift potential (max 600 words, approximately 1–2 pages)

#### Dimension Description Rating
**Scale**
- **Description:** Large-scale investments in the poorest and most vulnerable communities living in areas with high climate risks. These countries are highly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change but are not yet able to adopt measures to reduce their exposure because finance for adaptation is limited and is not targeted at the poorest communities.
- **Rating:** Low

**Replicability**
- **Description:** Investments in climate information systems, climate-resilient livelihoods, and climate-smart infrastructure that benefit poor and vulnerable communities have been made in various degrees in GCF projects. However, knowledge and evidence on the successful implementation of local level adaptation measures that are systematically documented and shared across countries is limited.
- **Rating:** Low

#### Potential target scenario (Description) How the project/programme will contribute (Description)
**Scale**
- **Description:** The GCF aims to mobilize US$370 million in domestic and international resources over 2015–2020 to improve the climate resilience of at least five countries in Africa, three countries in Latin America and the Caribbean, and seven countries in the Asia and pacific region. This will be achieved by using this large-scale investment to scale up adaptation measures for the poorest and most vulnerable communities, while also supporting transformative development beyond the program’s usual investments.
- **Rating:** Low

**Replicability**
- **Description:** The GCF aims at leveraging and influencing the allocation of domestic budgets, private sector, bi-lateral and multilateral funding organizations, thereby multiplying the impact of these resources. The programmatic approach of the GCF will allow for the pooling of resources and dissemination of lessons learned, best practices, knowledge and capacity for further investments and scaling up adaptation measures across different countries as well as regions.
- **Rating:** Low

## Sustainability

### Low

- **Sustainability**: Weak institutional and community capacity means they are unlikely to plan and implement adaptation measures at the local level that build resilience for poor and vulnerable communities, protecting them from increasing climate shocks and stresses.

**Paradigm shift would involve climate risk-informed development becoming the norm in all sectors. It will also improve capacity of institutions to design and implement adaptation resources to the local level for targeted interventions for the poorest and most vulnerable people in different geographic and sectoral settings.**

- **behavioural change making decision-makers responsive to promoting a green investment implemented under the GCF and strengthen the policies and regulatory frameworks that support climate-resilient and sustainable development, further facilitating replication and scaling.**

**Through its investments in climate information services, the GCF will promote climate change considerations into development policies. It will use climate information in national planning processes and on-going decentralization processes to inform local decision-making. Science-based local climate assessment tools will be developed, equipping decision-makers with the knowledge and information needed to inform local adaptation strategies.**

- **By strengthening national and local institutions, the GCF will ensure that its investments promote policy coherence around climate change and are aligned by relevant agencies. Countries ownership of climate change dialogue engagement and capacity building at all levels – government, civil society organizations, and private sector.**

| In many GPP countries, climate information is not systematically integrated into government programming. The flow of information between and sectoral policies or development plans often hinders decision-making at the national and sub-national levels. | Different districts, sectors and communities in the region. Communities and governments lack the capacity to access, interpret and use climate information to inform policy planning decisions at the national level and to design and provide advice on adaptation as part of climate resilient development strategies. Poor and vulnerable people, especially women, are often excluded from decision-making processes relating to investments and climate resilient sustainable development projects. Most investments in climate change finance fail to meet their specific needs and priorities for climate resilient sustainable development. | GCF Investments in climate information services under the CRPP IF component will support institutional capacity building for decision-makers at all levels of planning processes and programming, including the availability and use of climate information. |
| Investments from the CRPP will provide poor and vulnerable people and include women with greater human capital and capacity to participate in decisions on adaptation and sustainable development. |

E.6 Project programme activities and deliverables

| Output 1. Knowledge and evidence on climate data-informed pro-poor local solutions strengthened |
|---|
| Activity 1.1 Knowledge and evidence generation | This activity will focus on carrying out research to strengthen knowledge and evidence base for pursuing climate change adaptation strategies and programs in education, health, livelihoods, social inclusion, and livelihoods-related investments in the context of transformational adaptation investment needs and are aligned with national climate priorities. |
| Sub-activity 1.1.1 Undertake macro level climate risk and vulnerability assessment | - 4 macro level climate risk and vulnerability assessment reports. |
| Sub-activity 1.1.2 Carry out analysis on policy, institutional and regulatory barriers to designing and implementing climate change adaptation strategies and policies and programs that address the needs of the poor and vulnerable | - To support rapid scaling up findings from research studies. |

This activity area will support national governments in the development of the climate risk information systems (protocols and processes) and tools. #### E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

Monitoring, Evaluation and Learning (MEL) are considered cornerstones of the CRPP, and the first detailed MEL framework and implementation plan will be developed concurrently with the CRPP. The focus on monitoring the progress of each individual sub-project and the program as a whole according to the results framework, but to use the CRPP as an opportunity to advance the collective understanding of how to design and implement effective project management systems. This will be achieved by developing a detailed MEL plan that is aligned with the CRPP implementation plan and will parallel the development of the detailed MEL framework and implementation plan based on a detailed study undertaken as part of the CRPP scoping activities. A detailed review of large-scale climate adaptation financing mechanisms was carried out, and best practice and lessons learned were considered in developing the MEL arrangements to guide MEL activities under the CRPP. The arrangement for monitoring, reporting and evaluation are presented in more detail in Annex 11.

Guiding principles: The key principles that will guide the MEL activities include:

- INTERNAL This information is accessible to ADB Management and staff. It may be shared outside ADB with appropriate permission.


---
**Page 125**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.01 PAGE 121

## Participatory Approach

The procedures for monitoring will involve communities and local stakeholders, including civil society organizations, at all stages of the project/program cycle from the beginning. This will not only build the capacity of local actors in monitoring and reporting techniques, but create by-in to output projects (thus supporting sustainability) and foster learning and awareness raising in terms of climate change adaptation. Evaluation will be an integral part of the project/program cycle, with a focus on how well the project/program is achieving its intended outcomes and decisions and improve performance. Key to promoting the maximum utility of the evaluation outputs, will be the engagement of the primary user groups. Under the CRPP IF, the primary user groups are the Executing Entity (EE), the National Designated Authority (NDA), ADB and GCF.

## Accountability

Accountability is a key element in the CRPP MEL approach. It must strike an appropriate balance between reporting against quantitative indicators that tell us what milestones are being achieved, and a more qualitative focus on how these milestones are being achieved and why these milestones are not being achieved.

## Practicality

The CRPP MEL activities will have to balance and reconcile multiple and competing demands: risks and constraints of monitoring and evaluation; the need for transparency in trade-offs between cost, reporting burden, and a desire for methodological rigor and comprehensive learning as acknowledged, is as well as the need to accept reality on the ground in the project countries. These factors are to be considered when developing the draft MEL plan and managed accordingly.

## MEL Approach

The following points identify key of the key pillars of the CRPP MEL approach (further discussion on the CRPP MEL approach is included in annex 11).

### A multi-level approach: Monitoring and evaluation under the CRPP will be carried out at two levels: project level, and program level. Despite the differences in scope of M&E at different levels, in order to achieve a sufficient level of evidence generation for reflection and learning for the benefit of all stakeholders involved, it is important that there are common principles, methods, tools, and indicators across these levels. The shared frameworks will be guided by a common vision of the program across all levels of implementation. This will greatly facilitate the ability to carry out cross-level analysis and comparisons. It also allows for more consistent reporting on progress towards the overall objectives. The link between the project, CRPP program, and GCF fund level is considered to be especially critical to generating comprehensive and consistent data that can be used to measure the contribution of the program to transformative change and the assessment of key assumptions that provide the logic for action.

### A flexible and adaptive approach: Responding and adapting to changing contexts and the evolution of knowledge and understanding is a key enabling process for building resilience, and thus a learning approach must also involve adjusting the processes, results framework and indicators, of the M&E system. The flexibility and adaptability of the M&E system should be expected. Thus, the M&E frameworks need to allow for continual adjustments based on the emerging body of knowledge and experience with the monitoring and measuring of results. It is important that the MEL frameworks should not be static; rather a should be tailored to the implementation stage by, for example, evolving towards broader evaluation questions as the program moves through its lifecycle.

# Implementation arrangements: A CRPP MEL Expert will be part of the CRPP secretariat and will be responsible for the coordination and management of all MEL activities under the CRPP and ensuring independent and impartial review of the project activity results within the CRPP program.

Full roles and responsibilities of the MEL expert will be established as part of the initial role out of the CRPP.

## MEL Implementation Guidelines: At the CRPP inception, using the CRPP Trust Fund incubation resources, a MEL workshop will be held involving key stakeholders. The workshop will review the project design, including the baseline and targets for each project, the monitoring processes and future outcomes. The discussions from the workshop will feed into the development of the CRPP MEL plan and guidelines which will provide direction on how to implement the MEL framework within the CRPP program.

A comprehensive data management system will be established to collect and collate information from projects. This integrated approach will minimize reporting burdens, while ensuring that the CRPP results framework and GCF reporting requirements are adequately covered.

## Establishing the baseline: Baselines and targets for the sub-project MEL frameworks will be established. In addition to the related CRPP program indicators, project specific indicators will also be developed. These will include baselines and targets for paradigm shift and enabling environment indicators as required for the GCF IMF. Qualitative baselines will establish a narrative which articulates the current context in which each project is operating and how it will contribute to the overall CRPP program. The quantitative baseline will provide an indication of what the project will support, including how the project will specifically contribute to that change. This will be aligned with the overall program narrative, but with specific focus on the project’s contribution.

The baseline will also include a description of the project’s context, including its location, the country economy and the type of sub-project proposed in the country as per the IRMI guidelines. Qualitative baselines will be developed for each paradigm shift assessment dimension and enabling environment indicators using the three-point scaling rating in the corresponding scorecard.

## Monitoring and Reporting Arrangements: Sub-project level monitoring and reporting under the CRPP will combine ADB policies and protocols with GCF MEL requirements, while minimizing project burden. The CRPP MEL plan will provide guidance on how to meet these requirements without extra reporting burden. ADB project monitoring systems will be followed to track implementation progress. Standard ADB policies, during the loan implementation period, require the submission of quarterly reports by individual projects prepare:

1. Semi-annual progress reports which include:
   * a. progress achieved by output as measured through the indicator’s performance targets in the design and monitoring framework.
   * b. key implementation issues and solutions
   * c. challenges to implementation

2. Consolidated audited and entity financial statements;

3. Semi-annual environmental and social progress reports, including subprojects’ environmental management plans, gender action plans; and

4. Project Completion Report within 3 months of physical completion of the project.

In line with GCF MIE policy, annual performance reports (APRs) for the CRPP will be undertaken by the project management units under the guidance and supervision of the CRPP MEL Expert. The project level APRs will include a narrative report (with supporting data as needed); on

## IMPLEMENTATION PROGRESS BASED ON THE CRPP RESULTS FRAMEWORK (WHICH EXPLICITLY COVERS THE GCF RESULTS FRAMEWORK) AND CONSIDERATIONS ON THE ONGOING PERFORMANCE OF THE PROGRAM AGAINST THE GCF IMPLEMENTATION FRAMEWORK, INCLUDING UPDATES ON THE INDICATOR AND A REPORT ON ESS AS WELL AS ANY OTHER RELEVANT REQUIREMENTS IN ESS AS IN THE APPENDIX. THIS WILL BE PROVIDED BY THE CRPP MANAGEMENT UNIT (CMU) IN ORDER TO ENCOURAGE PARTICIPATORY MONITORING, AN ANNUAL PARTICIPATORY REVIEW FOR LOCAL STAKEHOLDERS, NOTABLY PROJECT-AFFECTED PEOPLE AND COMMUNITIES, INCLUDING THROUGH A CONSULTATION MEETING WITH THE CMU. THIS WILL BE FOLLOWED BY AN EVALUATION OF THE PROGRAM IMPLEMENTATION BASED ON THE PROJECTS ON THE PROJECTS. ADB WILL PREPARE PROGRAM LEVEL CRPP ANNUAL REPORTS DETAILING a) ACTIVITIES CONDUCTED DURING THE YEAR, AND PRIORITIES FOR THE NEXT YEAR b) POTENTIAL ISSUES AND SOLUTIONS c) PROGRESS AGAINST TARGETS AND INDICATORS GIVEN IN THIS PROPOSAL.

### EVALUATION ARRANGEMENTS

The evaluation arrangements will consist of mid-term reviews and final evaluation reports for each sub-project with investment from GCF as well as the CRPP program as a whole. As with monitoring and reporting, the GCF evaluation plan will align GCF policies, especially the GCF Evaluation Policy, with ADB’s goals and protocols to ensure that there is a coordinated and coherent approach to evaluation, that maximizes learning and accountability for results.

#### Mid-term review (Projects): The CRPP MEL Expert will be responsible for overseeing the Mid-Term Review (MTR) and the Terminal Evaluation (TE) of the GCF funded activities. The MEL Expert, in coordination with the project management units, will oversee the process of hiring external independent evaluators to conduct a mid-term review of each project. This will include an assessment of progress against milestones, including financial performance, and any corrective actions required for the project to achieve its intended outcomes by project completion.

#### Mid-term review (Program): ADB will conduct a mid-term review of the program in the 5th year of the program implementation to take stock of results and implementation of the first phase of investments under the program. This program level evaluation will be undertaken by an independent entity and will include an assessment of progress against milestones, including financial performance as part of the program efficiency and effectiveness criterion. The evaluation will determine progress being made towards the achievement of outcomes and outputs and will identify course correction if needed. It will include a range of appropriate quantitative and qualitative indicators.

8 Evaluations Monitoring is one of a number of innovative approaches to MEL that was established in the UK Government funded Building Resilience and Adaptation to Climate Extremes and Disasters (BRACED) program, which has been used by ADB in other projects.

**Page 128**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.01 PAGE 124

## Terminal Evaluation (projects): A Terminal Evaluation (TE) will take place at the end of each sub-project implementation period, according to ADB and GCF established policies. The TE will be an assessment of project performance against standard evaluation criteria (e.g., strategic relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, likelihood of impact and enabling environment). The draft TE report will be shared with project stakeholders during a commencing meeting managed by the Evaluation Office and the CRPP MEL Expert. Formal comments on the report will be shared in an open and transparent manner. The evaluation reports will be publicly disclosed and will be followed by a recommendation to the Board.

## Terminal Evaluation (Program): An independent Terminal Evaluation (TE) will take place at the end of programme implementation according to ADB and GCF established policies, especially the Evaluation Policy. The TE will be an assessment of programme performance against standard evaluation criteria (e.g., strategic relevance, effectiveness, efficiency, likelihood of impact and enabling environment). The draft TE report will use the progress shift and enabling environment scorecards to report on targets identified in the baseline. The draft TE report will be shared with programme stakeholders for comment and subsequently disclosed publicly.

## Knowledge management

The implementation of systematic MEL activities in the CRPP will generate information that will advance understanding, not only of how countries can respond effectively to climate change, but also organizational performance and the relevance, effectiveness and efficiency of the approach taken for the management and implementation of a large-scale climate adaptation fund. The CRPP will systematically record and disseminate lessons learned from its experience in implementing the CRPP. These lessons will be used to inform future climate adaptation programming and can be used to continuously improve MEL practice in the context of resilience. In order to ensure that lessons learned from the CRPP have maximum utility and contribute to shared and outward learning on climate resilience programming, a knowledge management strategy will be developed as part of the cut-off of the CRPP and will include a clearly defined work program and accompanying budget.
