# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Implementation Delays (mitigation measures: none, see pages)

The level of key risks that will affect the CRPP performance are generally low to medium and expected to be mitigated with the ADB's established rules and procedures. Key risks identified are related to implementation delays due to COVID-19 related lockdowns and travel restrictions.

### Selected Risk Factor 1: Implementation delays due to COVID-19

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description:** Projects face implementation delays due to COVID-19 related lockdowns and travel restrictions.

#### Mitigation Measures:

COVID-19 has caused delays in the preparation of feasibility studies for many ADB projects. It has affected schedules for feasibility studies, implementation of detailed designs, and initiating construction. In order to keep project teams engaged during this period, it is important that there are alternative ways available such as use of online consultation meetings, and maximize engagement of external local experts to do sections of feasibility studies that require ground work.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: Lack of prioritization for projects

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Low | Medium |

**Description:** Borrower governments' fiscal burden may not allow for additional borrowing, and/or governments may choose to invest in non-resilience projects with external financing.

#### Mitigation Measures:

The program will work closely with the government to support project ownership and help them understand and prioritize resilience projects. This can be done through supporting policy dialogues to influence for technical assistance which aims to strengthen institutions and capacity to access funding, prepare and implement adaptation measures. The program will also raise awareness among governments for COVID-19 recovery will also be raised with governments during ADB country programming discussions as part of project origination and project identification processes.

### Selected Risk Factor 3: Procurement (including local project procurement)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |


---
**Page 130**
---

# Description

Lack of understanding and compliance with ADB procurement rules.

## Selected Risk Factor 4 | Project level: ESS and GESB alignment

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

### Description

Beneficiaries' limited capacity to comply with national regulations and/or ADB and GCF environmental and social policy requirements and gender and inclusion requirements.

## Mitigation Measure(s)

To ensure that environmental and social management plans (ESMP) supported applications are managed, an Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) is in place that will apply to any project that utilizes GCF funds through this program. The ESMF will be implemented and maintained by CRPP IF as part of the CRPP IF ESMP. The CRPP IF ESMP will be integrated into the ESMF, which will ensure compliance with ADB safeguard requirements for any ADB SPS, GCF's Environmental and Social Policy (ESP), Gender Equality Strategy (GESB) and other relevant policies. The CRPP IF ESMP will include a set of procedures and designated staff responsible for implementation detailed in the ESMF will guide CRPP IF in ensuring compliance with all applicable environmental and social management requirements as well as adaptation investments. The set of procedures integrated in the ESMF include screening and categorization, consultation, stakeholder engagement, disclosure, monitoring and reporting.

Furthermore, both ADB SPS and GCF's ESS standards recognize stakeholder engagement and disclosure, among others, as compliance benchmarks on ensuring broad multi-stakeholder support and participation throughout project implementation. Strengthening benchmarking will ensure compliance with relevant local authorities' ADB and GCF environmental and social policy requirements and gender inclusion requirements which will be integrated in the CRPP IF ESMF.

## Selected Risk Factor 5 | Financial

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credit | Low | Low |

### Description

Lack of ability of borrowers to meet repayment of debts from the program.

ADB's comprehensive due diligence and technical support will mitigate the risk that the borrower may have related to inability to service the loan due to insufficient cash flow to meet the debt service. In addition, all loans from the program will be aligned with ADB sovereign loans and thus be covered by the standard terms of ADB sovereign loans. The risk of loan default on repayment of program funds is low and related only to risks of the sovereign itself.

Selected Risk Factor 6 Money laundering, terrorist financing and prohibited practices

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Prohibited practices | Low | Low |

Description

GCF financing through CRPP IF results in money laundering, terrorist financing or other prohibited practices.

Mitigation Measure(s)

ADB conducts integrity due diligence (IDD) on non-governmental counterparties and recipients of GCF funds. ADB also provides oversight and support to the recipient country’s anti-corruption and integrity policies and required due diligence that apply at project level, which will mitigate this risk.

Furthermore, all projects must comply with ADB's Anticorruption Policy (2016), as amended to date, and any alleged corrupt, fraudulent, collusive or coercive practice relating to the projects and recipients of the grant/fund shall constitute a violation of such investigation and extend all necessary assistance for satisfactory completion of such investigations.

In terms of financial management assessment, in accordance with ADB’s Charter, all projects must take necessary measures to ensure that the proceeds of any loan made, guaranteed or participated in by ADB are used only for the purposes specified in the project agreement and in a manner consistent with good governance and efficiency. Accordingly, ADB undertakes financial due diligence and financial management assessment (FMA) as part of its pre-award review process. The FMA is an evaluation of the entity’s systems to assess gaps and mitigation measures for financial and management controls, including internal control systems, loan disbursement and cash flow management arrangements, and governance and anticorruption measures.

ADB defines “executing agency” as the government agency with overall responsibility and oversight for the carrying out of a project. The executing agency is responsible for ensuring that all conditions in the agreement for the project are met. A project may also include one or more “implementing agencies,” which are governmental or other bodies responsible for implementing specific activities under a project.

The proceeds for projects under the program will be disbursed in accordance with ADB’s loan disbursement Handbook, and the executing agency is responsible for ensuring that all conditions of the agreement and procedures for the release of funds to the borrower. Public disclosure of the audited project’s financial statements, including the auditors’ report on the financial statements, shall be made available upon request by any interested party.

ADB's OIG serves as the initial point of contact for all allegations of integrity violations in projects and other activities of ADB. Allegations are independently and objectively assessed and, where necessary, investigated pursuant to ADB’s Accountability Mechanism. The OIG reviews investigations to determine whether they require further investigation.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G1 Environmental and social risk assessment

GCR IF is proposed in accordance with GCF’s Environmental and Social Policy (2018)[7], Revised Environmental and Social Policy (2021)[8] and ADB’s Green Policy Statement (2009)[9]. While being consistent with the above policies, GCR IF will also address new environmental and social risks involving investments through financial intermediaries (this, including other emerging concerns that are not common to both policies, will be addressed as compliance measures in the CRIF). The GCR IF proposes a risk-based approach for categorizing investments into three categories: Category A (GCF) and ADB donor financing (and potentially other multi-donor co-financing) to adaptation investments that are expected to have significant positive impacts on climate change mitigation, adaptation or transformation; adaptation for the poor and vulnerable groups. ADSC through SSDC, will host and manage the GCR IF. The GCR IF is proposed as a Category I financial instrument (FI) with the objective to support investments in selected countries. The GCR IF is categorized as Category FI (financial intermediary) under the Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESG) Policy. It includes relevant environmental and social policies, and applicable requirements; b) guidelines and procedures for project identification, preparation, implementation, monitoring and evaluation of projects to be financed; c) identification of organization capacity and competency; d) risk management and mitigation; and e) guidelines for managing non-compliance. Screening and categorization of projects are based on the following criteria: a) the extent of the environmental or social risks associated with specified in Safeguard Requirements (SRs) 1 on Environmental Safeguards; SR 2 on Involuntary Resettlement.

The GCF’s environmental and social risk category determines the extent of the due diligence required, the scope of the mitigation measures and the resources necessary to implement environment and social management plans. The following table provides a summary of the proposed categorization for investments through financial intermediaries. The GCR IF is rated as Category I or 2 depending on the environmental and social risks associated with the project. If the project is not expected or is expected to include, financial exposure to category B activities, GCF defines category B activity as moderate risk activities that include those with risks and impacts that are less adverse than Category A but more adverse than Category C. The magnitude of these risks and impacts is considered limited, and the magnitude is expected to be low to moderate. The safeguards’ risks and impacts are generally mitigated through good practices and measures that are not necessarily irreversible, and readily mitigated through generally accepted mitigation measures and good international industry practices.

Category I or 2 is proposed for GCR-IF on the basis of the investment it will support, that falls under the following: a) adaptation investments that support implementation of information and systems for decision-making; b) climate resilient pro-poor livelihoods investments (directly linked to attainment of Output 5); and c) Information on climate resilience pro-poor infrastructure (directly linked to attainment of Output 6). Category I is expected to be applied where the investment falls under SRs 1 or 2 as defined in Output A. An ESMS is required for the GCR IF where specific investments and their location have yet to be clearly defined.

When project details are available, appropriate environmental and social due diligence should be prepared. The CRIF ESMS will guide and provide the framework for managing environmental and social risks associated with the investment. This will include a risk assessment at different stages. This is to ensure that financial intermediation operations result in projects that are environmentally sound and socially acceptable. The GCR IF will be consistent with ADB’s environmental and social regulations, ADB SPS and GCF’s ESP, and Good International Industry Practices.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 130

The establishment of the ESFM is in line with ADB SPS and GCF revised ESP and will be maintained by the CRPP IF to guide the implementation, assessment, management, and monitoring of the environmental and social risks associated with ongoing and future investments, excluding abuse and harassment. The Revised Environmental and Social Policy (2021)) This process is critical to support decision-making on investment projects that are in line with ADB SPS and GCF revised ESP and will be maintained by the CRPP IF to guide the implementation, assessment, management, and monitoring of environmental and social risks and impacts and to identify potential co-benefits associated with CRPP-IF investments.

The CRPP IF ESFM includes the following processes:

* **Screening and categorization:** Screening is undertaken to determine if project falls on the list of prohibited investment activities list (SPS, Appendix 5). The project will not be pursued it an exclusion applies. Categorization is used to identify the significance of a project’s potential environmental and social risks and impacts. Categories are defined by the risk category identified in the screening and categorization procedures.
    * **Category A:** For activities with significant adverse involuntary resettlement impacts and significant impacts on indigenous peoples, require the preparation of an environmental impact assessment and development of a resettlement plan and indigenous peoples plan;
    * **Category B:** For activities with significant adverse involuntary resettlement impacts that are not deemed significant and limited impacts on indigenous peoples. The project will be pursued but requires an environmental examination, resettlement plan and indigenous peoples plan;
    * **Category C1a:** For activities with minor or no adverse environmental risks or impacts and no significant adverse involuntary resettlement impacts on indigenous peoples require no further action; and
    * **Category C1b:** For the direct or indirect investment of ADB funds to or through a financial intermediary with safeguard requirement include the need for the FI to develop an environmental and social management system.
* **Due diligence and risk assessment:** Due diligence and risk assessment of environmental and social risks associated with sub-projects will be undertaken, and the application of the mitigation hierarchy (avoidance, minimization, and, where ever offsettable residual impacts as far as practicable) will be identified. The level of due diligence is defined by the risk category identified in the screening and categorization procedures.
* **Implementation of safeguards:** Implementation of safeguard measures will be integrated into the ESFM, including procedures for the implementation of corrective actions to address non-compliance and unanticipated environmental and social impacts.
* **Grievance redress mechanism (GRM):** ADB requires the borrowers clients to establish and maintain a grievance redress mechanism in place and procedures to address SEAH. In case of occurrence of Sexual Exploitation and Abuse and Sexual Harassment (SEAH), ensure that there is an effective, accessible, and confidential process for reporting and addressing grievances.
* **Accountability Mechanism:** ADB Accountability Mechanism is also in place to provide an independent forum and process whereby

# G.2. Gender assessment and action plan

Women have a critical role to play in advancing investment in building resilience to climate change, especially at the local level, and are recognized as vital drivers of resilient development. However, women face significant barriers, such as limited access to information and early warning systems, as well as gender-based power imbalances in CRPP countries, often limiting women's leadership and participation in decision-making, and contributing to their vulnerability.

### Women’s limited access to information

Women typically have less access to all types of information including climate information and early warning. This is related to gender-based disparities in literacy rates and access to education that are prevalent across many CRPP countries, particularly in rural areas and in some countries more than others. In Pakistan for example, less than half of women are literate (51% compared with 87% of men), and in Cambodia, the gender gaps in education are most prominently seen in rural areas.

### Significant gaps remain in social protection programs reaching women

In Indonesia, insufficient use of up to date sex-disaggregated data for monitoring and targeting of beneficiaries, complicated information systems, and lack of awareness among local government officials have led to inadequate support to complete enrollment procedures. As important issues in Pakistan, the gender gap is also evident in the Ehsaas Emergency Cash Program (EECP) where it has been found that women are at risk or being disproportionately excluded from the EECP.

### Women’s exclusion from decision-making

Women in many CRPP countries are largely excluded from decision-making and are absent from leadership positions. This is particularly true at the local level. This is often due to discriminatory customary laws, cultural and structural barriers that prevent women from participating, such as in Vanuatu where women have no direct access to land ownership. In some cases women are invited to attend but decisions are already made by the men; or in Timor-Leste, women are not included on village committees which are responsible for the right of equal participation of women and men in political life.

### Gender gaps in accessing agricultural extension and rural advisory services

Gender gaps in access to agricultural extension and rural advisory services are prevalent in many CRPP countries. Women represent only 10% of agricultural extension services despite comprising 51% of the primary workforce in subsistence agriculture and 57% of the workforce in commercial agriculture.

### Social, cultural and legal barriers prevent women from accessing financial services

Social, cultural and legal barriers prevent women from accessing financial services in many CRPP countries such as Pakistan, PNG, Timor-Leste, and Vanuatu. Women have limited ownership of productive assets, lack access to credit, and are often excluded from the labor force. This represents a barrier for women’s access to formal financial services. Other barriers include: lack of collateral, low levels of financial literacy among women, high transaction costs, low mobile phone ownership, that deter women's outreach through digital financial services.

### Significant disparity between men and women in earnings

There is a significant disparity between men and women in earnings, the quality of jobs, and voice and representation in leadership positions in many CRPP countries, and women’s economic


---
**Page 136**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 152

activities that involve low value-added activities. In Papua New Guinea, for example, there is a **strong gender gap** in employment with only 14% of women as wage workers compared to 33% of men and 86% of men employed in agriculture. Among the urban poor (aged 20–59 years) in Lao PDR, 95.5% of women are informally employed and dominate small-scale rural enterprises.

Under-employed workers are also more likely to be women as they juggle employment and family responsibilities. Women in Bangladesh spend an average of 14 hours per day on unpaid domestic labour (4 hours a day on average) compared to men (30 minutes per day).

## Women's adaptive capacity is also limited by the fact that they often face barriers to accessing and receiving climate information, as well as decision-making power over resources such as water and natural resources management, such as forest user groups, water user associations, and environmental conservation projects. Due to these disadvantages, women can be less likely to benefit from projects that promote nature-based solutions.

Women are often reluctant to use emergency shelters, and there are frequent examples of women refusing to use emergency shelters due to either design concerns or because of their bad experience with past sheltering. In many countries, women face a lack of safe spaces such as dedicated spaces for women, and private space for pregnant woman and nursing mothers are rarely found in disaster shelters. Women who have experienced gender-based violence (GBV) and girls are at greater risk of gender-based violence (GBV), including sexual harassment and domestic violence. Women also face additional risks during disasters due to lack of access to safe emergency shelter design and inadequate coordination by disaster responders.

Building women's resilience to climate change and empowering women through transformational investments in climate adaptation at the community level are core pillars of the CRPP, reflecting ADB’s commitment to gender equality and women empowerment. Empowering women is a key component of women's resilience to climate change will emphasize women’s role in resilience as “active stakeholders” and not just as victims. The CRPP recognizes that structural factors such as poverty, lack of education, and gender inequalities are driving women’s risk in CRPP countries, and so a gender transformative approach is essential for the CRPP. Gender equality is an important element of the CRPP, with a central element of CRPP investments in climate resilience themselves representing an investment in gender equality. The CRPP will address the structural factors that drive women's functions, systemic discriminations and the power imbalances between women and men, which intensely influence their vulnerability to climate change. These structural factors are not only gender-based but go beyond mainstreaming gender considerations into programming, delivering specific outputs for strengthening women’s adaptive capacity, and changing the structural socio-cultural norms that drive women’s vulnerability.

Examples of gender interventions that are identified in the CRPP Program Level Action Plan, and address some of the specific issues identified above include:

- Undertaking action research studies that address gaps in understanding around women and resilience in CRPP countries and make the case for women-focused adaptation investments.
- Support innovative adaptive initiatives and pilot actions at the community level to identify and test solutions that can be scaled up or replicated, with lessons learned being shared widely in the future.
- Carry out policy dialogues and forums for exchange at the regional, national, and local levels that bring together grassroots women’s organizations, government ministries from various sectors, the private sector, academic institutions, and other civil society organizations, providing a platform for exchange and collaboration between actors that may not traditionally work closely together.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 133

## CRPF will seek to tackle the limitations of existing social protection schemes in terms of reaching women, ensuring that social protection programs include specific identification of and outreach strategies for women.

### Recognizing that financial inclusion is key to strengthening women's resilience to climate related shocks and stresses, the CRPP will seek to expand women’s access to instruments such as index-based insurance, microfinance, and other financial services. The CRPP will also support investment projects that can be paired with capacity building on savings and investments, supporting preparedness to shocks while empowering women financially, socially and politically.

### CRPP investments will seek to address gender-related factors that influence women’s ability to participate in climate change adaptation activities. These include increasing women's access to land and engagement in agricultural entrepreneurship oriented activities, deepen women's chances to increase their income through the sale of agricultural products as well as diversifying their income-earning opportunities, and provide women with leadership roles.

### Investments will also integrate initiatives that reduce women’s time poverty and burden of care in ways that reduce and redistribute labor and time commitments to benefit them directly. 

### The CRPP will integrate gender considerations in related natural resource management and disaster risk reduction activities. This includes ensuring that women are represented in meetings where public works are designed and selected, including capacity building activities that support women’s participation.

### CRPP supported investments in safe shelter will ensure that shelter design includes features that respond to specific needs of women, and increases their access, ensuring that women of all ages, including those with disabilities, can safely access shelters. Shelter designs should consider the needs and that designs respond accordingly. Women will also be equally represented in governance structures such as shelter or community committees, to support empowerment through participation.

The CRPP Gender Assessment and Action Plan are presented in detail in Annex 8. The Gender Assessment provides a program level overview of some of the key gender issues that contribute to the vulnerability of women and girls to climate change impacts and how these issues may affect their ability to adapt than men. Detailed Gender Assessments will be undertaken as part of the detailed design and preparation phase for each project, with the results informing the CRPP Gender Action Plan. Similarly, the CRPP Gender Action Plan presented in Annex 8 will provide an overarching framework for gender equality that will be further developed by project specific Gender Action Plans to be developed during detailed preparation phase.

## G.3 Financial management and procurement

The CRPP supported projects will adopt ADB systems for financial management: disbursement, audit and reporting. The CRPP will ensure that all projects comply with ADB’s policies in all its applications including those concerning anticorruption measures, anti-money laundering, counter terrorist financing, safeguarding, gender, procurement, consulting services, and disbursement.

Financial Management All project's financial accounting, disbursement procedures and auditing requirements will be developed during project preparation and will be included their respective grant.

## Disbursement
Project disbursements will follow ADB's Loan Disbursement Handbook (2017, as amended from time to time) and detailed arrangements agreed upon between the government and ADB.

## Audit
Public disclosure of the audited project’s financial statements, including the auditor’s opinion on the financial statements, shall be made in accordance with the requirements set out by the GCF.

## Procurement
All procurement for CRPP supported projects will be done in accordance with ADB's Procurement Guidelines and Procurement Regulations for ADB Borrowers (2017, as amended from time to time). The GCF Secretariat will provide guidance on the implementation of these guidelines, including procurement processes and offer training, as needed, to the executing agencies in the completion and review of procurement-related documents. Procurement plans for the respective projects shall be in accordance with their respective grant implementation manuals. A detailed procurement plan for use of GCF is included in Annex 10.

### Disclosure of funding proposal
No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF; no confirmation is being provided in confidence.
If any portion of the funding proposal or related documents are to be disclosed in part only, the following is required to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:
- Full copy for internal use
- Redacted version where such redaction portions are marked accordingly; a confidentiality note regarding the said portion and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity’s disclosure policy; and
- Redacted version to be disclosed on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s)

**KINGDOM OF CAMBODIA**

National Council for Sustainable Development

No. 006 NSCD Phnom Penh March...2021

Mr. Yanick Glemarec Executive Director Green Climate Fund Secretariat Songdo International Business District G-Tower, Yeomju-gil, Cheon-dong, Yeonmu-gu, Incheon 22004 Republic of Korea

Re: Funding Proposal for the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Project Preparation Facility by the Asian Development Bank regarding the Community Resilience Partnership Program (CRPP)

Dear Mr. Glemarec,

We refer to the Community Resilience Partnership Program (CRPP) covering multiple countries in Asia and the Pacific for the Green Climate Fund (GCF) to include Cambodia in the program which is currently under preparation by the Asian Development Bank.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the Ministry of Environment and the National Designated Authority and the focal point of the Kingdom of Cambodia.

Pursuant to our national procedures, including those of which we have knowledge to be reviewed, we hereby communicate our no objection to the program as included in the CRPP funding Proposal.

By communicating our no objection, it is implied that:

(a) The Royal Government of Cambodia has no objection to the program as included in the GCF funding Proposal;

(b) The program as included in the GCF funding Proposal is in conformity with Cambodia's national priorities, strategies, and plans; and

(c) In accordance with relevant environmental and social safeguards, the program as included in the GCF funding Proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no objection to the program as included in the GCF funding Proposal has been duly followed.

We also communicate our no objections to all GCF-financed activities to be implemented within the scope of the program.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Yours sincerely,

ASIAN DEVELOPMENT BANK

9 MAR 2021 CAMBODIA RESIDENT MISSION

Subject: Funding Proposal for the GCF by Asian Development Bank regarding Climate Resilience Partnership Program (CRPP)

Dear Mr. Glemarec,

We refer to the program of Climate Resilience Partnership Program (CRPP) as included in the funding proposal submitted by Asian Development Bank (ADB) to us on 8 March 2021.

The undersigned is the Chairman of Fiscal Policy Agency, Ministry of Finance as the National Designated Authority of Indonesia.

Pursuant to GCF decision B.08/13, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no objection to the program as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating this no objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government of Indonesia has no objection to the program as included in the funding proposal;

(b) The program as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with Indonesia's national policies, strategies and plans;  
(c) In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards, the program as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also state that our national process for ascertaining no objection to program as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.  

We also confirm that our no objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the program.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Yours faithfully,

[Chairman's signature]  
Febrio Nathan Kacaribu  
Chairman

# Secretariat's assessment of FP215

## Proposal name: Community Resilience Partnership Program

### Accredited entity: Asian Development Bank

### Country(ies): Cambodia, Indonesia, the Lao People’s Democratic Republic, Papua New Guinea, Timor-Leste and Vanuatu

### Project/programme size: Large

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The proposal presents a unique and comprehensive approach to scaling up locally led adaptive solutions that address the needs of the poor and vulnerable populations in seven countries across Asia and the Pacific region. This approach is well aligned with updated Strategic Plan-2 priorities (targeting individual projects in the target countries). | Limited private sector involvement is foreseen in this programme as it focuses on government-led initiatives. This aspect will be explored on a case-by-case basis at the individual project level. |
| The ability of GCF to offer grant finance will drive the mobilization of up to USD 150 million (USD co-financing into the most transformational and poverty-focused projects) targeting large scale, targeting the poor and vulnerable populations. GCF contribution of USD 273 million (of which USD 155 million of ADB financing and USD 75 million of finance mobilized through the Community Resilience Partnership Program Trust Fund), representing more than five times the amount of every United States dollar of GCF financed invested. | The secretariat of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) has strong expertise and a proven track record in the region in implementing these activities, which will ensure sustained results for effective climate change adaptation. |


---
**Page 163**
---

## Summary of the Secretariat’s report

### II. Project background

#### 2.1 Project background

a. Countries in Asia and the Pacific region are highly exposed to the damaging impacts of climate change. Between 2000 and 2019, the region experienced 3,068 disaster events accounting for 42 per cent of global events. Adverse impacts of climate change will be larger manifold in the future, with women being disproportionately affected by climate change, with women typically lacking equal preventive and coping capacity due to pre-existing gender inequalities. The project aims at addressing this issue on a multi-country basis at both national and regional level.

b. In responding to financial gaps and needs, the proposed project (the Community Resilience Partnership Program (CRPP)), is a regional programme of the Asian Development Bank (ADB), aims to scale up adaptation measures by mobilizing climate investment at large scale at the community level in seven countries in Asia and the Pacific region. The CRPP will address three key climate change, poverty and gender inequality across seven countries (Cambodia, Indonesia, the Lao People’s Democratic Republic, Pakistan, Papua New Guinea, Timor-Leste and Vanuatu) in Asia and the Pacific region.

c. This proposal is a useful and important programmatic concept for scaling up local adaptation measures at scale with regional and global lessons learned from similar programs, especially in Africa, funded by the Pilot Programme for Climate Resilience under the Climate Investment Funds (CIF). The CRPP will focus on four key areas: (i) strengthening the enabling environment for knowledge, finance and implementation of local adaptation measures; (ii) the mobilization of climate investment at scale through a combination of public and private sector stakeholders; and (iii) mitigation of the exposure of poor and vulnerable people to climate shocks and stresses.

d. The CRPP comprises two separate but interlinked components: the CRPP Trust Fund (TF) focuses on upstream support to strengthen the enabling environment required for implementation of adaptation measures at scale, while the CRPP Investment Fund (IF) focuses on the actions for rolling out local adaptation measures through downstream investment (subprojects), which will be co-financed by ADB and GCF. The CRPP will be carried out under a special vehicle established by the ADB (Continuing Resilience Financing Partnership Facility).

e. The CRPP TF will provide resources to implement outputs 4, 5 and 6 of the wider CRFPP programme (described below).
(i) Output 4: Information and systems for delivering applied climate risk informed interventions.
(ii) Output 5: Climate-resilient poor livelihoods investments implemented; and
(iii) Output 6: Climate adaptation pro-poor infrastructure implemented.

f. The CRFP IF will have a total of USD 120 million (USD 100 million grant and USD 20 million loan) to finance subprojects, which will be co-financed by ADB and GCF (between USD 10 million and USD 40 million) and will leverage at least USD 55 million from ADB’s resources and USD 75 million from GCF. The CRFP IF will support the implementation of up to five subprojects in each country) from 2023 to 2030. Subprojects seeking financing from the IF will need to be approved by the ADB Board between 2024 and 2017 and complete implementation by 2030.

g. The Trust Fund will be fully capitalized by bilateral donors and financial institutions, including UK Aid, Nordic Development Fund, and GCF. The Investment Fund excludes the potential contribution from respective governments that will be determined at the individual project level.


---
**Page 164**
---

## Component-by-component analysis

The overall goal of the proposal will be to deliver transformational adaptation solutions through large-scale investments explicitly targeted at the poor and vulnerable population.

There are two main and complementary components to deliver its objective:

### Component 1: CRPF-TE - supporting countries to develop knowledge, build capacity, and structure information systems for climate risk-informed policy making and investment

This component will be financed through the CRPF TF and focus on three outputs:
(i) strengthening knowledge and action research on climate risk-informed poor and vulnerable communities;
(ii) building country-level institutional capacity in climate risk assessment and adaptation; and
(iii) developing a pipeline of climate-resilient investments (USD 12,000,000).

The activities will support the design and implementation frameworks to be supported by the IFI. It will develop climate risk assessment and decision support tools, conduct training and capacity-building on designing and implementing climate-resilient projects, and provide technical assistance for climate-resilient investment projects, and pilot investments in transformational solutions. These activities will generate a pipeline of climate-resilient investments.

The activities under component 1 are also complementary to the activities planned under component 2. CRPF TF supports the development of the climate rationale and project preparation phase (including the identification of adaptation measures) for component 2.

### Component 2: CRPF Investment Fund (IF) - supporting implementation of local adaptation measures (information systems, resilient pro-poor livelihoods, adaptation infrastructure) through a diversified portfolio of investments

This component will be financed through the CRPF IF and focus on four outputs:
(i) strengthening information systems for delivering climate risk-informed adaptation at scale;
(ii) improving resilience to climate risks in vulnerable communities;
(iii) increasing access to finance for adaptation; and
(iv) improving information and systems for delivering adapted climate informed action.

These outputs will be co-financed by the ADB and GCF. The focus will be to deliver:
- (i) improvement of information and systems for delivering adapted climate risk-informed adaptation at scale;
- (ii) increasing access to finance for adaptation; and
- (iii) improving resilience to climate risks in vulnerable communities.


---
**Page 165**
---

investment scale (total cost: USD 118,000,000; GCF grants USD 25(600,000)) (c) implementation of climate-resilient pro-poor livelihoods investments (total cost: USD 236,000,000; GCF grants USD 40,000,000); and (iv) implementation of climate-resilient pro-poor information systems (total cost: USD 512,000,000; GCF grants USD 80,000,000 and loans USD 20(000,000)).

### Output 4. Information services and systems delivering applied climate risk informed investments at scale

The GCF grant and loan for the information services and systems for delivering climate risk informed decisions is targeted to address information failures that prevent the most vulnerable groups from accessing climate risk information assistance provision (which is eligible for grant finance under B6/07) to cover the additional costs of strengthening climate services at local level, to integrate climate risk information into social safety nets, and to mainstream climate change. The proposal also highlighted that these activities will cover activities that explicitly target the most vulnerable groups.

- Activity area 4.1: Climate information services for key sectors to meet local adaptation needs;
- Activity area 4.2: Climate risk informed social protection systems; and
- Activity area 4.3: Climate risk information systems for decentralised policies.

### Output 5. Climate resilient pro-poor livelihoods investments implemented

The GCF grant and loan for the climate resilient pro-poor livelihoods investments focus on addressing risk perceptions for new climate-resilient livelihoods or markets, and covering the incremental cost of the risk premium required to make these new livelihoods viable. The activities in this output will also target at the most vulnerable groups.

- Activity area 5.1: Climate-resilient agroecological systems;
- Activity area 5.2: Information technology and risk management services for climate-resilient livelihoods; and
- Activity area 5.3: Climate-resilient agriculture supply chains.

### Output 6. Climate adaptation pro-poor infrastructure implemented

The GCF grant and loan for the climate adaptation pro-poor infrastructure is targeted at the additional costs of green, resilient infrastructure, necessary to deal with climate risks in a way that benefits poor and vulnerable groups. The primary focus is to invest in ecosystem-based adaptation, as this provides multiple co-benefits (including environmental and social benefits) in reducing non-market costs (providing environmental and social benefits).

- Activity area 6.1: Ecosystem-based infrastructure;
- Activity area 6.2: Water and landslide protection/infrastructure; and
- Activity area 6.3: Multi-purpose climate resilient shelter.

The estimated impacts are considered significant and entail substantial adaptation benefits as well as support the resilience of poor and vulnerable populations to climate change. The proposed programme will also contribute to the resilience of local communities while contributing potential emission reductions from activities related to climate-resilient livelihoods, agriculture, and infrastructure.

The activities are also closely aligned with the four pillars of the United Nations' newly launched Early Warnings for All (EW4A) Initiative, which stipulates that every person in the world should have access to early warning information by 2030. The programme will support multi-hazard early warning systems (MHEWS) in over 100 countries. The proposed programme will

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### Impact potential: Scale: High

15\. The individual projects will align with the objectives of the CRFP and contribute to specific climate change adaptation and resilience-related measures. They will be financed by the ADB and GCF, with GCF resources specifically supporting local-level adaptation actions in line with the CRFP’s investment strategy. The following table sets out this country is summarized below with details annexed to the funding proposal.

(a) Cambodia: The Climate Resilient Smallholder Development Project aims to strengthen community resilience through a participatory and gender-sensitive approach that drives development of climate-smart agricultural practices, rehabilitate and climate-proof infrastructure, and enhance access to finance. The project will also contribute to climate change, and develop financial risk management mechanisms such as crop insurance.

(b) The Lao People's Democratic Republic: The Building Resilience and Reducing Rural Poverty Project aims to enhance the resilience of rural poor communities by expanding non-cash social safety nets in vulnerable areas through a community-driven approach. It will focus on areas of high climate risk, integrating early warning systems with the social registry to expand access to services for the most vulnerable groups. The project will also provide an economic inclusion program tailored to address climate vulnerabilities through skills training and financial services provided linked to conditional cash transfers.

(c) Pakistan: The Climate Change Resilience Project aims to improve the resilience of coastal area livelihoods and infrastructure through integrated water resources and flood-risk management, as well as by promoting sustainable land use practices and natural solutions. The project also emphasizes the role of women in building coastal resilience. The project directly contributes to the activities under output 6 of the CRFP.

(d) Papua New Guinea: The Climate Change Resilience Project aims to strengthen disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation through education and Training for Climate Resilient Employment Project aims to strengthen climate resilience through technical and vocational education, training, and capacity building to support women and informal workers. Through the technical and vocational education system, the project will develop climate-resilient agriculture, climate-resilient construction practices, and disaster risk reduction measures that can be integrated into climate-resilient livelihoods. The project directly contributes to the activities under output 6 of the CRFP.

(e) Timor-Leste: The Agriculture Value Chains and Water Harvesting Project aims to enhance the sustainability of rural livelihoods by increasing the resilience of small-scale agriculture, livestock production, and fisheries through improved water use, forest management, and enabling smallholder producers to participate in agricultural value chains. The project directly contributes to the activities under output 6 of the CRFP.

(f) Vanuatu: The Climate Change and COVID-19 Project aims to build comprehensive resilience in poor and vulnerable groups. It focuses on creating climate-responsive social safety nets through a community-based approach, as well as financial risk management mechanisms, particularly for women. The project totally contributes to the activities under output 4 of the CRFP.

## 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

Scale: Medium to high

The core paradigm shift potential of this project centres on the enhanced mobilization of climate investment at scale in the region. It will allow scaling up and replication, creation of an enabling environment for policy dialogue and exchange of knowledge and lessons learned.

Scaling up and replication: The GCF IFP will only support individual projects that demonstrate a high likelihood to be replicated within other countries or other regions in the region. By empowering government and communities with the knowledge and skills to take appropriate actions, scaling up and replication can be achieved through additional investments, and proving their effectiveness through pilots, the individual project will build the foundation for further investment. The scale-up of successful projects will thereby drive a paradigm shift towards climate-resilient sustainable development. GCF funding will be used together with the ADB investment to enable the individual project to deliver at scale.

20. Creation of an enabling environment (and sustainability of outcomes). The CRFF will support outputs and outcomes that are closely aligned with national climate priorities. The CRFF TF will therefore catalyze impacts beyond a one-off project investment and deliver a paradigm shift in how countries approach climate change adaptation, mitigation, and resilience overall.
21. GCF financing will be used through the CRFF to support rolling out of such climate-sensitive development projects at scale, across multiple sectors and regions. By strengthening national and local institutions, the CRFF will ensure that its interventions will contribute to building policy coherence around climate resilience and it takes up nationwide by relevant stakeholders.
22. Contribution to the regulatory frameworks and policies: With a key focus on mainstreaming climate change adaptation into development planning and financing into policies and decision-making processes. The CRFF programming will capitalize on ongoing decentralization processes to support governments build robust, science-based local climate assessments and adaptation plans. The CRFF will provide technical assistance in developing with the data tools, guidance and information needed to inform local planning and investment.
23. Innovative solutions and approaches: The CRFF will draw on the lessons learned from the implementation of GCF-funded projects, which have demonstrated innovative financing measures and approaches. The CRFF will support financing of adaptation projects. In particular, it will focus on innovative financing mechanisms that can help to overcome information finance, institutional and gender-related barriers in an innovative way.
24. Sharing of knowledge and learning: Learning is critical to operationalize adaptive management practices at scale. Lessons learned from the diverse climate change solutions emerging from GCF-funded individual projects under the CRFF are with shared wider stakeholder communities. The CRFF will provide a platform for sharing lessons, with large potential for learning and replication, with the transfer of experience between programmes.
25. In addition, the proposed GCF-CRFF-facilitated outputs will complement past and ongoing ADB support to countries in developing national adaptation strategies and the submission on Climate Change-related funds and contribute to the paradigm shift in the region.
26. Guidance for project design: The CRFF will provide guidance and technical assistance for effective national development planning, comprising agriculture and food security, water, health, disaster risk reduction, and energy. Depending on response capability of communities to hazards from climate change, the CRFF will support projects targeting vulnerable groups such as for densely populated flood-prone areas, and ZICOOL for heat waves in urbanized cities. By targeting these most vulnerable groups, the CRFF will provide a platform for ADB and GCF support for building resilience of multi-sectoral livelihoods of most vulnerable communities.
27. Although the specificities of the social, economic and environmental benefits of early warning systems vary across countries, they can be used to reduce vulnerability in a multi-hazard context for a range of priority hazards. ADB should explore the use of the regional, national and local level early warning systems as an important tool that can help to produce impact forecasts for priority sectors of the communities. This can be done during the implementation of the project.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

28. Through investments in local level adaptation, the proposal directly targets the pillars of sustainable development - environmental, social and economic gender. Thus, the CRFF will deliver several against several of the SDGs including SGD 1 no poverty; SGD 3 good health and well-being; SGD 5 gender equality; SGD 9 reduced inequalities; SGD 11 sustainable cities and communities; and SGD 13 climate action.

**Page 169**

### Needs of the recipient

**Sole: High**

The poverty and vulnerability of the countries and target beneficiaries and their adaptation needs are elaborated throughout the proposal with descriptions of the scale and intensity of each country's exposure to climate risks. It highlights the increased socioeconomic vulnerabilities in these countries, which are already exposed to high levels of climate change impacts as well as the recurrence of climate hazards in these countries. Furthermore, the vulnerability of women, especially women in rural areas, is also well acknowledged in the funding proposal.

The least developed country (LDC) in Southeast Asia with about 90 per cent of the poor living below the poverty line and high inequality is Cambodia. The Lao People's Democratic Republic as a landlocked country is one of the LDCs with high vulnerability and low resilience to climate change impacts, especially those related to water scarcity and flooding lines. Palau is one of the countries most vulnerable to climate shocks in the world today with high water stress and poor access to clean drinking water. The Solomon Islands ranks 10th in the human development index with 39–40 per cent of poverty rates. While Timor-Leste ranked as one of the least developed countries, it is also exposed to high levels of climate risks, especially tropical cyclones.

Most of the target countries are LDCs with high levels of vulnerability to climate change and climate-related disasters. The proposal highlights the lack of a pre-climate information system, public infrastructure, and social services as the major constraints in these countries. Lack of access to reliable data and information is also seen as a constraint to building capacity of government, local governments, communities and vulnerable groups, especially women.


---
**Page 170**
---

3. Country ownership

Scale: High

The proposal describes alignment with national adaptation priorities of participating countries, international commitments, and relevant initiatives such as the Paris Agreement, the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction, and the SDGs.

Furthermore, the CRPP will contribute to the AIF's climate targets and its ambition of providing a more efficient and effective response. The project is designed to be directly linked to the nationally determined contributions of beneficiary countries and builds on the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change’s call for increased flexibility and effectiveness of adaptation finance.

The proposal also aims at promoting an integrated approach, which includes differentiated responses based on climate risk vulnerabilities across a range of biophysical and socioeconomic dimensions. By strengthening national and local institutions, the proposal seeks to ensure that investments will contribute to building policy coherence around climate resilience and be taken up nationally by relevant stakeholders. It also aims at increasing stakeholder engagement and capacity-building at various decision-making levels – government, civil society organizations, and communities.

The ADB has demonstrated its strong track record as a credible AE and EE by working with the target countries in the region to address related challenges. It has been developing and implementing climate change projects for over 30 years, drawing on its extensive experience and expertise in implementing such projects in the region, including GCF projects in the past years. The ADB will also leverage its work on climate change to ensure national specialisation as part of the project team for each subproject.

The project was designed on the basis of rigorous consultations with relevant stakeholders at country level. A stakeholder engagement plan was submitted as an annex to the funding proposal.


3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

Scale: Medium to high

Of the project’s total proposed budget of USD 750 million, GCF funding represents 16 per cent of the estimated project cost, with co-financing at 84 per cent. The GCF financing comprises a grant component of USD 239 million and an equity component of approximately USD 535 million in senior loans with variable, per-country loan values based on proposed interventions. The project will be implemented by the ADB, which has received substantial donor funding including USAID, Nordic Development Fund, Agence francoise de development will deliver grants of USD 107 million (for more details, please refer to annex 6).

The project is designed for implementation in four countries; therefore, the full spectrum of economic and financial appraisal of proposed individual projects was not possible. Instead, an Economic Financial Appraisal (EFA) was conducted on a subset of selected pilot projects (annexes 3). It was pursued as part of the internal ADB policy guidelines, all other interventions destined for funding under the CRPP IF will be scrutinized in accordance with the same EFA methodology to ensure that they obtain proper economic net present value (ENPV) and expected internal rate of return (ERR).

The results of EFAs of individual pilot projects (tables 2, 3 and 4 below) were pursued over 30 years (table 2) and 25 years (tables 3 and 4), starting from 2014. With a 6-year implementation period for the project, the results are presented in tables 2, 3 and 4 below for detailed EFA estimates.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

The CRFP IF project’s EFAs were carried out in accordance with the GCF EFA guidelines and were conducted in financial and economic terms on a per-project basis. The results of outlined IRRs and EERs are presented below, which are based on the net present value from the economic point of view as ENPV and ERR measures suggest the economic viability of proposed interventions. For example, ENPV is positive and ERRs are higher than a conventional discount rate of 9 per cent, indicating that the project is economically viable. The proposed indicative investment will benefit the entire economy as the benefits of proposed interventions will outweigh costs. The results of the analysis are consistent with the findings from the pilot test and are additionally supported by convincing evidence from the ex-post analysis of 23 field pilot tests that tested the effectiveness of various types of climate change adaptation measures, including community-based adaptation planning.

The proposed structure and programmematic approach are assessed to be adequate and reasonable in terms of addressing vulnerability and poverty of the target beneficiaries in the region. It is expected to deliver high efficiency and effectiveness as the GCF funds will be leveraged from other sources (e.g., USD 640 million will be invested, USD 951 million will be invested) USD 640 million will be leveraged from ADIs and other bilateral donors. This results in a value for money of 2.38 times the amount of GCF funds. The project is also assessed to be benchmarks in similar/average environments in similar regional contexts, targeting the most vulnerable.

The use of GCF finance to fund the climate adaptationality on top of existing AID funds is also assessed as effective. The GCF investment will benefit from the existing financial, governance and institutional framework of action, which ensure low risk and high conversions of inputs to outputs and outcomes. Hence, the marginal transaction costs for implementing GCF funds will be extremely low.

### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

#### Project background
The CRPP is part of a regional partnership facility established by the ADB to up invest in climate change adaptation projects at community level explicitly targeting the nexus between climate change, poverty and gender. The programme proposed for the GCF financing aims to provide grants and loans funded through the CRPP Investment Fund (IF) to support investments in climate resilient infrastructure and services in Bangladesh, Bhutan, Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos People’s Democratic Republic, Pakistan, Papua New Guinea, Timor-Leste, and Vietnam.

#### E&S risk category
The AE assigned the programme medium level of intermediation, equivalent to the GCF E&S risk category 12. The Secretariat confirms the proposed E&S risk category is within the accreditation level of the AE. Activities are categorized as moderate-risk activities and that Category A activities of potentially significant adverse E&S risks and impacts associated with the portfolio are subject to a higher level of due diligence; and set monitoring compliance procedures to manage E&S risks and impacts associated with the portfolio in accordance with the AE’s safeguard policies and the requirements of AE’s Safeguard Policy Statement (SPS).

#### Compliance with GCF environmental and social safeguards
The paragraphs below provide a brief description of the programme's compliance with the ISS standards.

##### ESS 1. Assessment and management of E&S risks and impacts.
As the specific details of the proposed CRPP IF investments are still under development, the AE’s SPS outlines the reference framework to be complied by the programme; the E&S management procedures will be developed in close collaboration with the programme. The environmental social impact assessments (ESIA) and E&S management plans (ESMP) to address the potential risks and impacts associated with the proposed CRPP IF investments are currently under development.

##### ESS 2. Monitoring, review, and reporting
The ESMP includes a monitoring plan that will be developed in close collaboration with the programme. The AE’s SPS outlines the reference framework for the environmental social impact assessment checklist, various outlines, and monitoring template; and (c) SPS review and update policy are currently under development.

##### ESS 3. Independent third-party verification
The CRPP IF investments will be subject to independent third-party verification of high-risk activities, the AE is recommended to substantiate the indicators on the degree and significance of adverse environmental social risks and impacts associated with the proposed and foreseen portfolio of the CRPP IF, especially in relation to the envisioned climate adaptation infrastructure investments.


---
**Page 174**
---

The AE will ensure that the programme design includes appropriate mitigation measures that will be taken to comply with those obligations and national laws. The AE will ensure that the programme design includes appropriate mitigation measures that will be taken to comply with those obligations and national laws.

### ESS 2: Labour and working conditions

Financing subprojects on climate-resilient agricultural systems, climate-resilient agriculture supply chains, and the pro-poor cluster adaptation activities will include a risk assessment of potential adverse impacts during construction and the employment of workers to operate and maintain the infrastructure. The programme design will also incorporate the environmental safeguards policy principles (SFS Safeguarding Principles 1), which require the borrower/client to provide workers with information on their rights, responsibilities, and obligations under the particular sector and specific classes of hazards in the borrower's/client’s work areas, including physical, biological and geological hazards, as well as to take steps to prevent accidents and injuries during construction. The borrower/client is also required to adopt preventive and protective measures consistent with international good practice for the protection of workers’ health and safety throughout the course of work. The borrower/client is also required to adopt preventive and protective measures consistent with international good practice for the protection of workers’ health and safety throughout the course of work.

### ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution control

The EMF triggered this safeguard standard because it covers requirements for pollution prevention, resource conservation, energy efficiency, waste, chemicals, hazardous substances, and trade in endangered species. The programme design under ESS 3 covers requirements for pollution prevention, resource conservation, energy efficiency, waste, chemicals, hazardous substances, and trade in endangered species. Environmental Management and Investment Activities Lists also include the production or trade of any product or activity deemed to be a threat to biodiversity, including those listed on Appendices I and II of CITES and agreements or subject to international pacts or bans, such as (a) pharmaceuticals, pesticides, and herbicides; (b) ozone-depleting substances; (c) polychlorinated biphenyls and other hazardous substances; (d) species listed in Appendix I of CITES; (e) trade in endangered species on International Trade in Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora; (e) transboundary trade in waste or hazardous substances. In addition to the general requirements in the ESMF, the programme will require incorporation


---
**Page 175**
---

relevant mitigation measures, such as conserving raw materials and energy, reducing project-related greenhouse gas emissions, reducing water usage, and putting in place solid and hazardous waste management and integrated solid/waste vector management, within the ESIA and ESMF. The ESIA will also include a description of how the requirements under ESS 3 during the programme implementation at the subproject level to make sure the above-mentioned safeguards requirements can be effectively applied to the CRFP’s investments.

### ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The ESIA triggered this standard when it identified that land acquisition and involuntary resettlement may result in physical and economic displacement in the case of installation of MHEW and equipment and information technology systems. The ESIA will assess the need for a resilient and climate-resilient agriculture supply chain; investment in ecosystem-based infrastructure, flood and landslide protection measures, and build-up of multi-purpose reservoirs, etc. As part of the programme’s ESF, the ALM will be developed to determine whether they involve involuntary resettlement. For a project involving involuntary resettlement, a resettlement plan will need to be prepared as per ESS 5.

The degree of the impacts shall be determined by the scope of physical and economic displacement and the extent of land acquisition. The ESIA will assess under Components 2 (Involuntary Resettlement) in the SPS provides for the scope, objectives and requirements of involuntary resettlement plans. The details of the resettlement Action Plan to be detailed where necessary at the subproject level.

### ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The ESIA will assess whether the proposed project activities and associated adaptation infrastructure may be located in sites near or within areas of natural habitat that may or are likely to be affected by the project. The ESIA will also include a description on how it assesses the significance of project impacts and risks for biodiversity and natural resources as an integral part of the environmental assessment, including the identification of potential adverse threats to biodiversity which include destruction of habitat and introduction of invasive alien species, and on the use of natural resources in an unsustainable manner. The borrower/client will need to provide information on how it will assess and manage these impacts and related risks and, as a last resort, propose compensatory measures such as biodiversity offsets, to achieve no net loss or enhancement of biodiversity and ecosystem services. This includes detailed provisions for assessing and mitigating impacts on the natural habitat (including protected areas) and associated adaptation infrastructure (e.g., flood protection projects and required screening and assessment of potential impacts on biodiversity. The ESMF also requires that where there is a significant risk to biodiversity, and where it is not possible or feasible to minimize impacts and compensate and restore biodiversity and ecosystem services will need to be implemented. For activities that have potential adverse effects on the environment, an environmental action plan (EAP) will be mandatory. The ESMF provides an outline of the BAP as referenced in Linked

published Good Practice Note No. addresses SRM risks in its operations focusing on leverage projects involving civil society work. The AE will also ensure that adequate information related to CRPP IF SEAM safeguarding is provided to GCF to enable review. The programme will address SEAM risks through the development of a gender-responsive GM and the establishment of an independent redress mechanism. ### Implementation arrangements

The programme will be managed by the AE, which also serves as the CRPP Secretariat. The AE will work through the Department of Climate Change and Sustainable Development (DCSD) to manage and operate the programme. At the programme level, the AE will ensure that all necessary planning and implementation are performed in accordance with the transaction rules for the AE's subprogramme. The Facility Manager of the CRPP’s subprogramme will verify that the AE has conducted the necessary planning and implementation activities as required by the transaction rules, and confirming that the project aligns with the relevant safeguard policies of both the AE and the GCF at each stage of the project cycle. This includes ensuring that the AE engages with project developers as needed, as part of the AE’s broader SPs initiative aimed at strengthening country capacity to manage SEAM risks in their projects. The AE will also maintain a roster of consultants to contribute to the attainment of the CRPP and GCF objectives.

### Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure

The AE provided a stakeholder engagement plan for the CRPP, which includes details on the stakeholders’ consultations conducted during the preparation and development of the CRPP concept and the present project proposal. The AE will also provide an overview of the CRPP’s structure, an explanation of the AE's policies and procedures relevant to stakeholders' participation, and the required procedures to be implemented for key phases in the project cycle. The AE has concluded consultations with relevant national and local implementation partners in developing the project concept and with other stakeholders as part of the preparation phase of the project proposal. The AE will secure no objection letters for the programmes. For the subprojects that will be supported under the CRPP, the AE will ensure that there is adequate planning and ongoing consultation with affected people and other concerned stakeholders, including civil society, and facilitate their participation in the project cycle as appropriate. The AE will also seek to commemorate with the impacts on affected communities. However, the details of subprojects will be provided by the implementing partners. The information disclosure will include the funded activity agreements (FAA) the disclosure and communications process of ESS documentation for Category B subprojects.

#### Gender Management

The AE will establish a project-level GM that will address the affected people's concerns and complaints including on SEAM. The ESFM requires that the affected people’s concerns and complaints are addressed promptly, using an understandable and transparent process in which gender responsiveness is appropriate and readily accessible to all segments of the affected people. The GM will be accessible by the affected people through a dedicated website or other means as required. Through the GM and the GCF's independent redress mechanism, the AE maintains its own accountability mechanisms for addressing complaints related to SEAM. The AE will also provide support for people adversely affected by projects financed by the AE, including in case of allegations of violations of the GCF’s indigenous peoples policy or the GCF’s indigenous Peoples Policy. The GCF indigenous peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

**Page 178**
---

### Gender policy

The AIF has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan therefore complies with the GCF Gender Policy. It has also provided a programme-level gender assessment and gender action plan.

#### Gender assessment

The gender assessment is undertaken through desk review and stakeholder consultation, including interviews with women’s groups and representatives of women’s groups. Further assessments are planned at subproject level with a gender equity theme and taking effective gender mainstreaming into consideration. This will be done to ensure key gender-related issues are addressed in the project design phase and throughout implementation based on country circumstances.

The programme-level gender assessment describes the gender issues at the country level and indicates that all seven countries are committed to gender equality and women’s empowerment. The constitutions of all the countries guarantee equality for women and men, and provide for equal rights and opportunities.

Many of the countries have ratified international agreements such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). They have also introduced various policies and national frameworks to promote equality. Many of the countries have national policy environment and supportive legislation such as national climate change policies, National Adaptation Programmes of Action (NAPAs), National Development Plans (e.g., Vanuatu), and Strategic Development Plan and National Adaptation Programme of Action (Timor-Leste).

The assessment also shows that though the seven countries are committed to gender equality and women’s empowerment, the ranking on the gender index varies and levels of equality vary across different sectors. The assessment indicates that all seven countries commit their governments to gender equality and ensure that, as a population more vulnerable to climate change, women should be included in decision-making processes.

The assessment has also demonstrated that violence against women, inequality in access to education, start gaps in economic and labour force participation and limited participation of women in public life are some of the major challenges faced by women. Countries demonstrate gender-based barriers to women in areas of literacy and education, ability to participate in decision making, access to credit and financial services, burdens of domestic and reproductive roles. Yet women play a critical role and contribute in all facets of each country’s economy and society. Gender-sensitive policies and programmes have been provided for each country.

The programme gender action plan (GAP) provided by the AIF includes actions with corresponding indicators and targets to be achieved within three years. The activities and grant resources that will develop the capacities and knowledge of various actors to be targeted in the seven countries include: strengthening the capacity of women’s organizations in women, building women’s knowledge and capacity to identify investment opportunities; and preparation of women-focused investment projects.

The programme also aims at improving access for women’s needs in accessing climate information. This will be done by ensuring gender issues are mainstreamed in climate information and systems to deliver local investments that apply climate-risk information at the project level.

# 4.3 Risks

## 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

75. GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 109 million and a senior debt of USD 20 million for the CRPP covering seven countries. The AE is co-financing a senior debt of USD 535 million and a grant of USD 20 million. Bilateral donors are providing a total of USD 75 million in the form of grants.

76. The CRPP consists of two separate but interlinked funds, namely CRPP TF providing technical assistance to support GCF investments and CRPP LF providing funding for local adaptation measures under domestic investment projects. The GCF funds will be used to finance CRPP IF only. Although projects supported under the TF may be financed by the IF and countries can use the LF as a source of co-financing, there is no direct link between the LF and the AE. Therefore, the LF is not considered eligible for IF investment and there is no direct sequencing between CRPP TF and IF.

## 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capable to execute the current programme (low risk)

77. The AE will be both AE and IF for the programme. The CRPP is operationalized through the Community Resilience Financing Partnership Facility (CRFPF) which has been administratively closed in April 2012. GCF funding for the CRPP was re-licensed within an AEiN GCF TF. The AE has a track record of implementing more than 40 trust funds supporting programmes and technical assistance in the region.

## 4.3.3 Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

78. Demand risk: Since only one project per country will be financed under CRPP, however the allocated funding may not be fully utilized if projects are delayed or phased out at a later stage. It will depend on the AE to source projects that meet the eligibility criteria. In case there is a lack of pipelines, the target size for the programme in some countries might not be fully utilized. Currently there is no limit in terms of the extension of the CRPP.

79. Funding risk: The CRPP targets reaching a size of USD 750 million including USD 675 million GCF TF and USD 75 million GCF LF, with an additional USD 280 million from other AEiNs by 2030, meaning individual projects will be likely to be approved between 2024 and 2027 and completed in the period up to 2030. The AE has committed to providing USD 150 million under AE and USD 75 million from other co-financiers. However, the term sheet does not include any timeline for the target fund size to be reached. Comfort can be derived from the covenants for
