## 4.4 Fiduciary

### a.
The ADB will play the dual role of AE and EE in the programme.

#### i.
As AE, the ADB will manage and operationalize the CRPP through the CRPF. The ADB will host the CRPF within its Climate Change and Sustainable Development Department (CCSD), acting as financial intermediary to ADB developing members for providing funds from GCF alongside ADB funds to support investments in selected countries.

#### ii.
As EE, the ADB will be responsible for implementing the entire programme, particularly the provision of technical assistance support under the CRPF TF (Component 13) and the provision of grants and loans for subprojects under the CRPP TF & Component 2.

#### iii.
GCF funds for the CRPP will be ring-fenced within an ADB GTF which was created pursuant to the GCF Agreement. The GTF will have its own legal personality, rights and immunities as ADB resources. Usage of GCF funds will be based on criteria outlined in the funding proposal which will be included in dedicated programme implementation guidelines to be developed by the ADB in consultation with the CRPP TF and Component 2.

#### iv.
Under Component 13, technical assistance support for CRPF TF projects will be made on an annual basis in accordance with the expected GCF approval dates, and projected disbursement plans and timelines of the projects in the CRPP portfolio. Payments of interest and principal for GCF-funded loans under Component 2 will be made by the ADB to the respective borrower, and any unused funds or income will be subsequently returned to GCF.

### b.
The ADB will offer concessional loans and grants to sovereign borrowers to be funded by GCF. For each CRPF TF project, the ADB will sign separate loan and/or grant agreements with the sovereign borrower in accordance with the terms of the respective CRPF TF project as implemented in compliance with ADB policies and CRPP requirements.

### c.
Disbursements for Component 2 projects under the CRPP TF will be made on an annual basis in accordance with the expected GCF approval dates, and projected disbursement plans and timelines of the projects in the CRPP portfolio. Payments of interest and principal for GCF-funded loans under Component 2 will be made by the ADB to the respective borrower, and any unused funds or income will be subsequently returned to GCF.

### d.
The ADB will establish and implement an ADB system for financial management, disbursement, and audit procedures. Implementation of the programme and projects will also conform to all applicable ADB policies. In particular, all subjects project financial accounting, disbursement, and audit procedures will be consistent with the CRPP TF and Component 2 project preparation and will be included in their respective grant implementation manuals. The ADB shall ensure that all CRPP TF and Component 2 projects are subject to appropriate project financial management and analysis of projects. Project disbursements will follow ADB’s Loan Disbursement Manual. Financial management, including the audit procedures for projects agreed upon between the government and the ADB. Public disclosure of the audited project's financial statements, including the audited project's annual financial statements and project financial statements, will be guided by the ADB Public Communications Policy of 2011.

## Results monitoring and reporting

As an adaptation initiative, the programme aims at generating diverse adaptation results across all four GCF adaptation result areas. A minimum of 321,940 direct beneficiaries and 4,645,000 indirect beneficiaries are expected to be reached by 2020. This represents a target coverage for 0.63 per cent and 0.87 per cent of the total population of seven target countries respectively.

Definitions and broad methodologies have been provided by the AE to estimate the adaptation benefits and impacts. To ensure that the programme can provide comprehensive, reliable, and detailed information about individual projects, it was recommended for the AB to develop and submit a project-specific monitoring plan at the time of project submission or upon commencement.

The theory of change (TOC) diagram adequately captures different levels of expected changes in the relationships between them from the goal statement to the proposed activities. It is helpful that the TOC diagram identifies co-benefits which can be achieved along the way and also highlights the barriers and risks that may hinder the achievement of these changes.

The theory of change (TOC) diagram adequately captures different levels of expected changes in the relationships between them from the goal statement to the proposed activities. The barriers and risks that may hinder the achievement of these changes are identified, and the mitigation strategies have been incorporated into the project TOC components are well embedded into the project TOC.

The logical framework has been developed in accordance with the GCF integrated results orientation approach. The programme will have huge capacity building skill potential across all seven target countries in all three dimensions: scale, replicability and sustainability.

It will contribute to all four GCF adaptation result areas with the GCF TF and if which will be established with the programme, and which will finance various individual projects. Four outcome indicators are chosen for each of these four GCF adaptation result areas and core supplementary indicators are chosen to monitor the results of the programme; however it is highly recommended that the programme should also develop a set of intermediate outputs and targets for each indicator once the detailed Monitoring, Evaluation and Learning Plan will be developed. At the GCF TF level in the “Transition environment”, two GCF criteria have been identified: institutional and knowledge generation, capture and sharing.

Given the relatively low or medium baseline of each indicator, the programme is expected to build a comprehensive monitoring system for all indicators.

The programme has identified relevant project-specific indicators for project outcomes and outputs. It is useful that the programme will measure co-benefits with specific indicators; this will ensure that the programme can capture the results which are not directly captured by the Secretariat to capture non-climate results that are worth monitoring and reporting.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP215

## Proposal name: Community Resilience Partnership Program

### Accredited entity: Asian Development Bank

### Country/[ies]: Cambodia, Indonesia, the Lao People’s Democratic Republic, Pakistan, Papua New Guinea, Timor-Leste and Vanuatu

### Project/programme size: Large

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

This is the first submission of "the Community Resilience Partnership Program (CRPP)" to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) for its approval under the Project Approval Process (PAP). The accredited entity (AEB) is the Asian Development Bank (ADB). The funding proposal covers seven countries: Cambodia, Indonesia, the Lao People’s Democratic Republic, Pakistan, Papua New Guinea, Timor-Leste and Vanuatu.

The CRPP aims at helping country and communities in Asia and the Pacific region scale up investments for climate change adaptation by increasing investments at the community level, and targeting the climate change poverty-gender nexus.

### 1. The funding proposal has been prepared as a response to three challenges: (i) the increasing frequency and intensity of extreme weather events; (ii) the growing number of already vulnerable populations in the participating countries that are already suffering climate impacts and require immediate action; and (iii) the need for increased public and private sector finance, which is critical for facilitating adaptation action by those highly vulnerable social groups.

ADB expects to reach a minimum of 3,219,400 direct and 4,465,800 indirect beneficiaries, and cover 70 per cent (30 per cent) of the population in the participating countries and communities (30 per cent); health and well-being, and food and water security (30 per cent); infrastructure and built environment (20 per cent); and ecosystems and ecosystem services (20 per cent).

The CRPP will be operationalized through the Community Resilience Financing Partnership Facility (CRFFP). The CRFFP is a multi-donor trust fund that ADB has established to finance two separate but interlinked components: the CRFFP Trust Fund (TF) and the CRFFP Investment Fund (IF). CRFFP is not a separate legal entity, but an facility arrangement written down with and by ADB.

Components of the CRPP are designed in such a way that they complement each other and enable the environment required for implementing local adaptation measures at scale. Components of the CRPP include: (1) climate risk information and early warning systems; (2) adaptation measures through a downstream investment programme. According to its budget, the funding proposal includes an additional component dedicated to programme implementation, monitoring and evaluation and learning (MEL) support.

The expected outputs from the CRPP are: (1) knowledge and action research on climate risk information and early warning systems that will strengthen national and community capacity to develop and deliver climate adaptation investments at community-level strengthened; (2) investments supporting inclusive pro-poor, community-level adaptation

### Direct beneficiaries are considered to be those who are specifically targeted by the intervention and participate in it, or are recipients of goods (e.g., agricultural extensions), services (e.g. off-grid electrification) or inputs (e.g., seeds, tools, livestock) provided as a result of the intervention (e.g. water harvesting, agroforestry). Indirect beneficiaries are those located in the coverage area and benefit from the intervention through its effects on their environment, livelihoods, or infrastructure.

Due to the diversity of country contexts and the range of activities that the CRPP is able to implement.

**Page 187**
---

The proposal includes two levels: a programmatic level and a project-specific level.

### Programmatic Level

- **Programmatic Assessment**: This provides a climate profile for each of the participating countries (annex 2.1). The profiles are based on information from the World Bank Climate Change Knowledge Network (CCKN) and include data such as exposure and vulnerability at the country level.
- **Country Profiles**: Each profile includes climatic information, e.g., average monthly temperature and rainfall, documented observations about past evolution of climate-related risks, and a description of adaptation measures. These profiles are based on four representative concentration pathways.

### Project-Specific Level

- **Project Selection Criteria**: The proposal outlines specific socioeconomic conditions of the participating countries including a preliminary analysis of the link between social conflicts and climate change. Based on the climate change-related information and considering the socioeconomic conditions, the project selection criteria are defined.
- **Adaptation Scenarios**: The profiles present the evolution of climate policy in each of the participating countries.

### Funding Proposal

The funding proposal explains existing barriers to scaling up adaptation at the livelihood level and how the proposed projects will address these (more detail see annex 1.2 “Livelihood shift potential” and 1.4 “Needs of the region”).

In terms of monitoring and evaluation, the funding proposal includes a preliminary version of the M&E framework for the project portfolio. The framework is based on five principles of participation, focus on learning and practicality (annex 13). The M&E will combine a multilevel monitoring and evaluation approach (project and programme level) with a theory-based approach (country level).

### Project Level

The funding proposal includes a two-structured framework for selecting projects at the project level. This comprises five overarching principles, eligibility criteria, project selection process and assessment tools, as well as the governance structure that clarifies roles and responsibilities in selecting projects in host countries (annex 2.4).

### The Five Principles are:

1. Respond to current and future climate risks in the context of changing socio-economic development.
2. Promote adaptation measures that address underlying drivers of poverty and gender inequality.
3. Promote selection of adaptation measures based on the principle of subsidiarity.
4. Promote adaptation measures in the context of physical, ecosystem-based, financial, and/or social and institutional resilience; and
5. Promote cross-sector linkages and mainstreaming into development.

This is because climate change impacts are experienced to a great extent by underlying socioeconomic inequality and vulnerability. Hence, the CRPP’s explicit target of reducing poverty and vulnerability is reflected in this eligibility criterion.

1.2 Paradigm shift potential

**Scale: Medium**

The funding proposal claims a "contribution to transformational change" by mobilizing and scaling public investments that support community-level adaptation for poor and vulnerable people; (ii) developing national and local policies, plans, and programs that promote financing for pro-poor adaptation projects; and (iii) increasing meaningful participation of poor women and men in climate-related decision-making (Output 1.1).

The funding proposal identifies four barriers to strengthening the climate resilience of poor and vulnerable communities in the Pacific Region:

- Lack of suitable, locally relevant and actionable climate information to inform resilience investments targeted at the poor;
- Limited access to adaptation financing for implementing local solutions at scale;
- Weak institutional and policy capacity to mainstream climate adaptation in policy planning and implementing adaptation solutions; and
- Negative perceptions and risk aversion around community-level actions.

As a response to these barriers, the funding proposal proposes three pathways of change: (i) strengthening enabling conditions for developing and delivering pro-poor local adaptation measures at scale in the participating countries (Outputs 1 and 2); The second pathway is implementing large-scale pro-poor adaptation solutions that build long-term resilience across multiple sectors (Output 3); and the third pathway to change is implementing large-scale pro-poor adaptation solutions to build long-term resilience across multiple sectors (Output 4).

According to the funding proposal, the components of the project are aimed at operationalizing these pathways of change. The first component, the funding proposal CRPP Trust Fund, will provide financial resources for strengthening enabling conditions and securing the enabling conditions in the participating countries and securing a regulatory framework and policies that support the implementation of the three outputs etc.

Knowledge and action research on climate risk-informed poor community-level solutions strengthened;

Institutional capacity to develop and deliver climate adaptation investments at community level strengthened; and

Inclusive pro-poor adaptation investment projects identified and prepared.

The budget allocates USD 72.5 million for activities related to these outputs. Even if the amount of funding is less than the total cost, the project will still provide a comprehensive framework that fully enables adaptation to climate change at the national, subnational and local levels; however, the TAF is less confident that this will lead in all cases to the implementation of projects that are financially viable for both the subnational and local levels, where the second component of the CRPP will invest.

The second component of the funding proposal is the CRPP Investment Fund, where the CGI Funds will be placed. This component will finance the implementation projects promoting:

- Information and systems for delivering applied climate-risk informed investments at scale;
- Climate resilient pro-poor livelihoods investments implemented; and
- Climate adaptation pro-poor infrastructure implemented.

**Page 191**

### Sustainable development potential

**Scale:** High

Funded in 1966, the ADB has a long tradition of promoting sustainable development in its member countries. The current funding proposal is based on and uses both lessons learned and complementary approaches from previous projects. This is reflected in the design of the funding proposal as addressing the interface between the Paris Agreement, Agenda 2030 and the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction.

**Programmatic focus:**

Based on the suggested project concepts included in the funding proposal, the ITAP assesses that implementation of the proposed program can contribute to achieving several Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The specific projects will address SDG 10: reduced inequality; and SDG 13: climate action. Contribution to further SDGs can be achieved through these projects, for instance the concept for Papua New Guinea will address SDG 4: quality education.

**Economic co-benefit:** The funding proposal aims at facilitating access to finance for poor people in rural areas by supporting small-scale and micro-enterprise economic activities including construction or agriculture. Therefore, the CRF will secure income to vulnerable households during implementation of the projects. According to the exit strategy of the specific projects it is possible that such income may continue in the long term.


---
**Page 192**
---

### Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

#### e1. Co-benefits: The CRFP focuses on reducing the vulnerability of people affected by poverty, with special attention to vulnerable women. Depending on the characteristics of the specific projects, the CRFP can promote additional social co-benefits including access to decision-making processes and resources, improved protection against climate change through training or reduction of disaster risks and, last but not least, securing life through access to multipurpose emergency shelters.

- Environmental co-benefit: Several activities included in this proposal can have positive environmental co-benefits including biodiversity conservation, soil protection or promoting and securing sustainable land use. For example, the CRFP could support the development of agroecological systems (Option 5) or the use of ecosystem-based infrastructure and nature-based adaptation measures (Option 4). In addition to these general co-benefits, depending on the specific design of the projects, flood and landslide protection infrastructure can contribute to protect important ecosystems.

- Gender equity mainstreaming: The promotion of gender equality is reflected in the funding proposal through its focus on the climate-poverty-gender nexus. Consequently, a stand-alone eligibility criterion for gender equality has been included as an additional requirement through the CRFP IF, which will benefit from the GCF contribution. Furthermore, projects within the CRFP have to be categorised as Gender Equity Thematic or Effective Gender Mainstreaming. At the programme level, the CRFP IF will establish a Gender Assessment Tool (GAT) and a Gender Monitoring System (GMS). The GAT is based on the GEF Gender Assessment Framework. The Gender Assessment includes a detailed analysis of gender issues per country, providing a good information base for the CRFP IF to make informed decisions about its projects in line with the proposal. It is highly recommended to strengthen the assessment, the gender criterion for project selection and the monitoring system at the programme level through the GMS system, so that progress in addressing gender-related inequalities can be perceived throughout programming.

- The ITAP defines the climate-poverty-gender nexus as a promising approach for addressing climate change adaptation and vulnerability.

#### Portfolio level

e1. According to the funding proposal, the CRFP IF will establish and maintain an effective environmental and social management system to identify, assess, manage, and monitor the potential environmental and social impacts and risks associated with its portfolio of investments (see Box 2). The CRFP IF will use the Environmental and Social Management Framework. This framework is built on the AfDB Safeguard Policy Statement and follows the GCF revised safeguard policy statement. It includes a set of environmental and social safeguard requirements and clarifies that during implementation of the CRIF IF environmental and social safeguards (e.g., involuntary resettlement, land acquisition, abuse and harassment [SEAH due diligence]) will be undertaken by the specific project’s implementer.

e2. As part of the Environmental and Social Management Framework, the CRFP IF will respect the principles of free, prior and informed consent (FPIC) for indigenous peoples. The Environmental and Social Management Framework includes a list of templates and checklists that should facilitate due diligence and monitoring of environmental and social impacts in a consistent manner across all projects.

e3. Considering the above, the ITAP assesses the potential contribution of the CRFP to sustainability as being high, as long as the Recommendation from the IFP is adhered to, monitoring, reporting and assessment of gender issues at the programme level followed.

## Notes:
- With its focus on the climate-vulnerability-gender nexus, the CRFP is well designed for contributing to the reduction of gender-based development concerns in the participating countries.
- Furthermore, in the examples of possible projects at the country level included in the funding proposal, the ADB shows how these projects can address the specific characteristics of the needs at the national level.
- The proposed initiatives limited access to adaptation finance for implementing local solutions and strengthening institutions. For example, the CRFP proposes a total investment of over 50 per cent of the total budget in implementing adaptation measures (27.5 per cent civil works and 23.5 per cent equipment), the GCF will certainly facilitate financial means for these projects.
- The inclusion of "needs of the recipients" as an eligibility criterion formalizes the assessment of local development barriers and to strengthen the capacity of local institutions at the project/portfolio level.
- Further, several activities in the CRFP aimed at strengthening institutions and implementing adaptation measures are not co-funded by the GCF contribution. These activities contribute to satisfying the needs of the recipient, even though these are not co-funded by the GCF contribution.
- Based on the analysis above, the TAP assesses the response of the CRFP to the needs of the recipients as high.

In the climate policies of all seven countries there is an explicit or implicit mention of the climate risks and vulnerabilities, and how to reduce climate risks of the population affected by poverty, with special consideration of women (and youth). Other, more specific examples of corresponding adaptation actions are provided in the NDCs and NAPs submitted by the TAP, for instance:

- In its Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC) in 2020, Papua New Guinea identifies the need to promote both the understanding of climate risks and to facilitate raising the value of ecosystem-based adaptation as promoting adaptation actions;
- In its National Adaptation Programme of 2021, Cambodia specifically talks at gender and age-related characteristics, including women’s and children’s resilience or insurance schemes for reducing climate and disaster risks; and
- The National Adaptation Programme in Pakistan (2023) identifies disaster risk management, ecosystem restoration and coastal and marine protection as priority adaptation areas.


---
**Page 195**
---

The ADB has currently 12 projects approved by GCF with a volume of funded activity financing of USD 947 million and in different stages of implementation. These figures demonstrate the implementational capacity of the ADB.

However, the TAP could not assess the capacity of executing entities because these have been identified on an ad hoc basis in a non-systematic manner and no assessment of the capacity of possible executing entities for the proposed ideas has been submitted. The funding proposal is subject to approval by the GCF Board. The implementing agency, which may be the Host Country, acting through its relevant ministry or department, may be the executing agency and/or responsible for project implementation. In some cases, the host country may have shown capacity at the ministerial level to be expected. Where the capacity of the executing or implementing agency is not sufficient, the TAP recommends that a sectoral deputy director or other support should he added to project implementation, as appropriate.

A further reason for concern is the level of engagement with the civil society organizations (CSOs) and local communities in the preparation of this funding proposal. The ITAP recognizes the consultations related to the proposal preparation and the stakeholder mapping and engagement activities that have been conducted or are expected to be conducted.

According to information in the proposal, most of the consultation and dialogue meetings focused on national government representatives, international donors and interested parties at the central level. Some CSOs were also involved in these meetings as well as in some countries with the participation of representatives of the national level. The TAP notes that there is no evidence provided by the funding proposal or elsewhere if they had any influence on the project design or will have in future. The ITAP also notes that engagement with representatives of CSOs for preparation of this funding proposal has been limited.

The language relating to the Stakeholder Engagement Plan (annex 7) is very vague and does not provide sufficient detail about how the TAP will ensure the ultimate representation of key stakeholders would be secured and their participation financed during the design of the projects or how/which subnational authorities will be included, or how/what role they will play in the project design. The same applies to their role in the design and implementation phases.

The main point of contact for CSOs and local communities will be the ADB tier representatives. This poses the risk that local community representatives and organizations will have less access to prepare and/or contribute to project proposals to be submitted to the CRFP Investment Committee.

Considering above, the ITAP assesses the country ownership as medium, and recommends that the envisaged future stakeholder consultations during the design of the counterpart projects should be strengthened urgently.


### 1.6 Efficiency and Effectiveness Scale: Medium to High

Out of the USD 20 million GCF funds, USD 20 million will be lent in United States dollars under Government Credit (non-component). The GCF loan will be disbursed on a line with the project implementation. For the Pakistan project, the project implementation is assumed to develop over a period of 15 years and the disbursement profile is as follows: 30 per cent in year 4; 70 per cent in year 8. The GCF loan would be for a period of 40 years, including a grace period of 10 years. The loan will be priced at a fixed interest rate of zero per cent (Bank high concessionally).

### II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

76. The ITAP notes that the CRPP mirrors the GCF investment criteria as their own eligibility criteria for projects to be funded through the Investment Fund. The ITAP highlights that the level of stringency used when applying and assessing these criteria prior to funding decisions is a major advantage for increasing the viability of the programme, and hence its long-term sustainability.

78. The ITAP recognizes that stakeholder consultation is an important part of the envisaged programme activities, and that the lack of stakeholder engagement constitutes a barrier in the implementation phase. The ITAP recommends that the forthcoming stakeholder consultations and engagement in the respective countries ensure inclusion of all relevant stakeholders to ensure their institutionalized stakeholder involvement processes throughout programme implementation.

79. Further, the ITAP noticed the shared concern with the secretariat regarding the monitoring of CRPP projects. The ITAP endorses this proposal for Community Resilience Partnership Program (CRPP) for approval and recommends that it be approved subject to the following conditions:

**Condition precedent to second disbursement:**

# ABOUT THE ANNEX

This annex presents a gender assessment for the Community Resilience Partnership Program (CRPP). The Gender Assessment describes the CRPP's guiding principles and approach for responding to the specific needs of women through its investments in climate change adaptation before presenting an overview of the program concept in Climate Countries. This document develops a detailed understanding of key issues related to the types of investments that are likely to be supported by the CRPP, and how such investments can address specific issues that contribute to women's vulnerability. The gender assessment also provides a set of key action areas that provide the basis for the Gender Action Plan, which identifies specific actions, design features, and monitoring indicators and targets that will be implemented through the CRPP as part of its supported investments.

The CRPP program level gender assessment presented in this annex has been informed by extensive desk-based research carried out as part of the CRPP scoping studies, as well as consultations with key stakeholders. The CRPP is a large-scale climate change adaptation and implementation partners such as the Huairou Commission, a women-led social movement of grassroots women's organizations working to empower women in development.

As the CRPP is a large program that will consist of a number of sub-projects that are still at concept stage, the aim of the assessment presented in this annex is to provide an overview of key issues related to gender and climate change adaptation. This information can be used as a starting point for developing more detailed and project specific gender assessments as part of the sub-project design and preparation.

# INTRODUCTION

Women play a critical role in playing a vital role in advancing investment in building resilience to climate change, especially at the local level, and must be recognized as vital drivers of resilient development. However, social and political inequalities, as well as patriarchal power structures, remain significant barriers limiting women's leadership roles in decision-making, and contributing to disproportionate levels of vulnerability and low adaptive capacity compared to men.

This is particularly the case in poor communities in low income countries as multidimensional poverty limits women's ability to confront climate change. In Asia, women account for 80% of all people living on less than $2 per day*, and their economic activities are often highly concentrated in agriculture and other informal sectors that are most vulnerable to climate change impacts. For example, women represent 73% of the agricultural labor force in Pakistan, and 70% in Papua New Guinea. Women's vulnerability is further compounded by gender inequalities, which combine with household work and caring for children, the sick, and the elderly; women do not always have the same rights as men to land; women often have less access to financial services, technology, information, and decision-making power.

*For further details on the specific vulnerability context of women, see Haddad (2016); Mies and Wiedmann-Pfister (2017); and Neumayer and Plumper (2007).


---
**Page 204**
---

While it is crucial to recognize and address women’s vulnerabilities to climate change and understand their structural causes for them, it is fundamental to refrain from categorizing women as innately weak, passive receivers of protection and support. In the past, this approach has prevented women from being recognized as active agents in resilience-building programs. The adoption of a gender perspective in disaster risk management (DRM) approaches exposes them to climate change shocks and disasters, as well as hinders the realization of women’s rights to full participation in DRM processes. Women’s diverse experiences, knowledge, skills, and perspectives mean that they can contribute positively to the identification of appropriate adaptation measures and ensure that they are implemented effectively and efficiently. Women can also be key agents for promoting gender equality within their families and communities; in turn this can give them recognition within the community and promote their position in the family and wider society.

The CRPP is a multi-year, multi-country initiative that aims to build on existing promising examples of grassroots women organizations closely collaborating with local governments to make use of their knowledge and capacity to assess, prioritize, negotiate, and influence resource allocation for resilience building at all levels of vulnerable communities. 

### 3. ADB’s GENDER POLICY FRAMEWORK

Accelerating progress in gender equality in Asia and the Pacific is one of the seven operational priorities in ADB's Strategy 2030. ADB recognizes that gender equality is critical in its own right as well as a means to achieve other development goals, including poverty reduction, achieving a prosperous, inclusive, resilient, and sustainable region. It is imperative that ADB contributes to the efforts of accelerating gender equality outcomes in the region in five areas: economic empowerment; access to education and health services; participation in decision-making processes; and resilience to external shocks. As such, the CRPP contributes towards the gender operational priority by (i) increasing the number of women’s organizations involved in ADB projects (10% of the number of ADB's committed operations (on a 3-year rolling average, including both sovereign and non-sovereign operations)) will promote gender equality;” 

ADB has established an institutional framework on gender policy and development—consisting of its Gender Policy Framework (GPF) and the Gender Operational Plan (GOP). The Gender Operational Plan, 2013-2020 is the latest action plan to operationalize the GPAD policy. The plan adopts a twin approach of gender mainstreaming and targeted investments in women and girls.

### 4. CRPP AND GENDER EQUALITY

Building women’s resilience to climate change and empowering women through transformational investments are two key objectives at the project level, which are core pillars of the CRPP, reflecting ADB's commitment to gender equality. This is demonstrated in the CRPP program architecture. The CRPP is operationalized through the Community Resilience Financing Partnership Facility (CRFPF), which consists of: (i) the proposed CRFPF Trust Fund, which provides technical assistance and grant resources for improving knowledge and understanding, and strengthening institutional capacity to support women’s organizations in building their social and political resilience; and (ii) the proposed Investment Fund, which will be co-financed by GCF and administered through ADB. The CRFPF Trust Fund will provide grants and technical assistance as part of ADB financed investments (sub-projects) in seven countries. The CRFPF Trust Fund includes a dedicated Gender Window that will support the identification, development, and implementation of gender-sensitive projects and programs aimed at strengthening the resilience of women to climate change. As such, support will be provided through (i)

INTERNAL This information is accessible to ADB Management and staff. It may be shared outside ADB with appropriate permission.


---
**Page 205**
---

knowledge and support technical assistance for identifying investment opportunities for building women’s resilience and creating an enabling environment for promoting women-based adaptation initiatives (i.e., through translation of technology assistance for preparation of housing investment projects; and (iii) grant financing on a selective basis, for implementing investments identified as gender transformative).

Through these two funds, CRPP will be able to invest in climate adaptation and resilience measures that build on the existing capacities of poor women and implement specific women-centred adaptation initiatives that are designed through inclusive processes where women are engaged at all levels.

The approach toward implementing investment activities that build women’s resilience to climate change will emphasize women's role in resilience as “active stakeholders” and “game changers” rather than as passive victims. This is because the social, economic, and political gender inequalities driving women’s climate risk in CRPP countries, and so a gender transformative approach that aims to change structural inequalities and address issues of climate justice*, will be a critical component of the CRPP investments. These investments, which are gender transformative, represent an opportunity to promote a positive change towards gender equality and challenge socially constructed norms that perpetuate inequality between women and men, which intensify the social and economic barriers faced by women to adapt to climate change. This is achieved through a combination of policy reforms (e.g., revising land tenure systems); revising regulations (e.g., the need to provide collateral for accessing housing microfinance); information management (e.g., ensuring that women have access to information on climate risks and adaptation measures). As we go beyond mainstreaming gender considerations into programmes, delivering specific outputs for strengthening women’s resilience, we will also be challenging structural socio-cultural norms that drive their vulnerability through transformational investments.

The CRPP's approach to gender is closely aligned with both the GFCS’s gender policy and ADB’s gender mainstreaming framework. This is reflected in the following principles for gender that have been identified as part of CRPP Investments, and are reflected in the CRPP Gender Action Plan. CRPP sub-project will:

- Ensure women’s equitable participation in projects, including public consultations;
- Promote increased participation of women in decision-making and leadership roles;
- Include capacity-building components that are either gender responsive or specifically targeted at women;
- Integrate gender considerations into all parts of the project management, including in the monitoring and evaluation framework;
- Document lessons learned specifically relating to gender to support future learning and replication;
- Follow the principles of an intersectional approach.

The portfolio CRPP sub-projects will include outputs that:

- Support employment and income generation opportunities for women;
- Promote women’s access and control over resources;
- Increase women’s access to relevant technologies;

* More information on the CRPP's Gender Window and the types of activities it supports can be found [here](#).

INTERNAL This information is accessible to ADB Management and staff. It may be shared outside ADB with appropriate permission.


---
**Page 206**
---

- **Build the capacity of different actors to mainstream gender:**
  - ADB uses four gender categories—gender equity theme (GET), effective gender mainstreaming (EGM), some gender elements (SGE), and no gender elements (NGE). GET projects have outcomes that are explicitly focused on reducing or eliminating gender disparities. By contrast, EGM projects need to have several outputs that contribute to gender equality in addition to the core project objective. SGE projects may be categorized as GET or EGM under gender mainstreaming category (as described in CRPP IF eligibility criteria in Annex 2.4) and follow ADB guidelines on gender mainstreaming.
- **Project specific gender assessments will be undertaken as part of detailed sub-project design stage in line with ADB policy related to GEM and EGM projects:**
  - The assessments will include field visits, focus group discussions, key informant interviews, and consultations with stakeholders representing women. The specific groups that will be consulted will vary according to the sub-project design.
- **The CRPP program level gender assessment is considered a living document and will be updated and evolve over time as the CRPP sub-project gender assessments are carried out:**
  - Giving more specific details about target areas within CRPP countries and in the context of sub-project outcomes.

### The Gender Context in CRPP Countries

#### Cambodia
- **Cambodia is the second poorest country in ASEAN and among the poorest in Asia with nearly 4.5 million Cambodians living close to the poverty line. In 2017, Cambodia ranked 116 out of 169 countries on the Gender Inequality Index (GII) at 146/189 on the Human Development Index.**
- **The Government adopted its National Strategic Action Plan 2014–2018 and identified climate change mitigation and green growth as one of its strategic focus for women’s economic empowerment:**
  - The Ministry of Agriculture, Fisheries and Forestry’s Gender Mainstreaming Policy and Strategy (2015) aims to improve the access of women farmers to land tenure and tenure security; secure tenure in agriculture administrations; access to agricultural extensions services; access to farming equipment and technologies; and access to financial services, land, and markets as key challenges facing women in
  - *An international approach will be a core element of the activities supported by the CRPP’s Gender Window that are discussed in the subsequent sections.*


---
**Page 207**
---

The agriculture and natural resource sector. Women’s predominance in the agriculture sector, their reliance on income earned in this sector, and their more limited access to education and information, however, make them particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change.

Education. The largest gender gaps in education are seen in rural areas (over 10% with females in rural areas among the ages of 6-24 years the least likely to attend school at 55.5%). Inequality in education is also reflected in differences between men and women in secondary school completion rates for upper secondary levels (over 21% for girls and 19% for boys in SY’16/17), however, the gender gap remains significant. The gender gap between men and women in the age group remain between region*. 5%. of women in the labour force had post-secondary education in 2017; and only .7%, at most, of women in the labour force had completed upper secondary school. Nearly 19% of women were working in agriculture (and only 2%), compared to men who worked in primary school.

Women earn substantially less than men in Cambodia, but the earnings gap narrows with the attainment of upper secondary and tertiary education. The gender earnings gap in 2014 was as high as 30% among those with low education and drops to .7% among college graduates. Within the same sector, women are more likely to work in lower paying jobs than men, particularly at lower skill levels, particularly of older women, perpetuating the gender earnings gap. Participation of females in public TVET is also very low (10%). Women are less likely to be enrolled in higher education and poorer females are now enrolled in equal proportions to men. However, women are more likely to be enrolled in the shorter, lower-level and less-technical courses and in a more gender-stereotyped courses.

Gender based violence. The National Survey on Women’s Health and Life Experiences in Cambodia, conducted by the Ministry of Women’s Affairs and UNFPA in 2015 revealed that 38% of Cambodian women reported experiencing physical or sexual violence at some point during their lifetime. In addition to physical and sexual violence, 47% required healthcare, only .3% ever accessed it. The Domestic Violence Law, enacted in 2005, criminalizes physical and sexual violence against women, but does not define what constitutes domestic violence or who can be a victim.

Cambodia has high levels of economic participation of women with over 80% of working age women* working in the informal sector, particularly in rural areas. Women are concentrated in low paid positions of which nearly half were in the garment industry. Women, through their wages and the remittance they send to their families, are a major source of income for Cambodian households, with women sending a higher proportion (20%) of their salaries back to their families than men*.

Significant gender disparities can be seen in earnings, the quality of jobs, and voice and representation in leadership positions. Women earn 35% less than men on average, and are employed in jobs that pay as much as women than men working age are not in the labour force. The definition of employment in Cambodia includes subsistence farming and unpaid family members. These activities often involve low value-added production and low productivity. In addition to this, nearly 40% of women aged 15-24 years are underemployed, significantly higher than official figures. Underemployment is highest in rural areas with many women working part-time or seasonally.

Nearly 90% of the Cambodian population lives in rural areas and a large majority of them are involved in farming activities*. Despite widespread gender inequalities in access to productive resources, services and markets, women’s share of employment in agriculture (and food production) was .42% in 2017; nearly 40% of working women (.35% of working men) were employed in the agricultural sector* and agriculture is the target sector of employment for both men and women.

* Working age is defined as between the ages of 15-64 years.


---
**Page 208**
---

# Indonesia

The population of Indonesia is slightly higher than 273.5 million, about 50.1% of which (134.4 million) are women[1]. According to the World Economic Forum (2020), Indonesia ranks 85th on the Global Gender Gap Index. Both the Asian Development Bank and the World Economic Forum (2020) reported that Indonesia made relevant progress in reducing gender differences and this effort has led to a growing participation of women in economics and in a consistent increase in access to both health and education.

Indonesia has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women. It has also promulgated Presidential Instruction No. 9/2000 regarding Gender Mainstreaming. The government has taken several steps to promote equality between Indonesian women and men in accessing and obtaining development benefits, as well as to increase their participation in decision-making processes at all levels. These efforts are carried out by Ministerial Decrees in Most Ministries. Gender priorities highlighted in the National Medium-Term Development Plan For 2020-2024 include: (i) gender equality and justice in education; (ii) life expectancy of women and men; (iii) access to health services for women and men, including the eradication of domestic violence; and (iv) tackling climate-related challenges (access to water, energy, food security, and disaster risk reduction). The following strategies have been identified as being to be implemented include: (i) strengthening policy and regulation; (ii) accelerating gender mainstreaming; (iii) increasing funding; (iv) enhancing role and capacity of women in development; and (v) improving network coordination among stakeholders, all of which are part of National Strategy for Acceleration of Gender Mainstreaming Implementation. The Long-Term Development Plan (2015-2045) and the Medium-Term Development Plan 2020-2024 include these strategies as a basis for addressing gender issues in climate change adaptation[2]. Indonesia's National Strategy on Climate Change Adaptation (2016-2030) also includes gender equality. Gender climate change in all development sectors will be crucial in implementing the policy and that enhancing the role of women in development and strengthening women’s capacity and leadership in climate change is essential.

Despite the comments presented in these policy documents, the country is still facing significant challenges in a number of areas: the economic gap between males and females persists; female literacy rates are low; maternal mortality is extremely high; female access to political participation is exceptionally low[3].

Indonesia ranks 36th in the 2016 World Risk Report[4] and, with a score of 10.24%, is considered a country at "high risk". The situation of Indonesia is extremely precarious since it is categorized as a country that is highly vulnerable to natural disasters. This categorization takes into account (exposure, vulnerability, susceptibility, lack of coping capacities and lack of adaptive capacities).

Indonesia is prone to disasters and women are more impacted by natural disasters than men. For example, in the case of the Indian Ocean Earthquake and Tsunami which led to the loss of many lives in Indonesia, it was reported that the percentage of women deceased was three to four times higher than for men[5]. The government has taken several steps to protect women from disasters. Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies[6] in Indonesia has a solid legal framework that protects women and vulnerable groups. The 2007 Law on Disaster Management and the Regulation 21/2008 on Disaster Management, Indonesia's law to protect "vulnerable groups" in emergency response, including disability.

**Page 209**

# Health and Gender-Based Violence

According to the 2020 Global Gender Gap Report, the health gender gaps have nearly closed (score of 97.4%), showing a significant improvement in the last decade. Nevertheless, Indonesia is still facing significant challenges, like maternally mortality rates that are among the highest in Southeast Asia and the second-highest number of child marriages in the world; the 2018 Socio-economic National Survey (Susenas) reports that 11.1% of women aged from 20 to 24 married underaged.

In the last decade, the country saw a substantial growth in reported cases of violence against women and girls. The 2020 Indonesian National Women's Life Experience Survey (SIPRIN) reports that one third of the women interviewed experienced some form of gender-based violence during their lifetime, with this figure even worse during the COVID-19 pandemic; as gender abuse have raised by 12% in most cases. Violence against women and girls has been a persistent problem since at least 2013, more than 95% of the case were of domestic violence (96% in 2010 and 98% in 2013).

## Education

The 2020 Global Gender Gap Report shows the existence of a narrow gap in terms of literacy rates: overall, 94% for females and 97% for males. However, there remains substantial differences in levels of access to advanced education with 59% of PhD graduates are men and 41% women.

## Political participation

In 2008, the government approved a set of electoral laws that introduced a 30% minimum quota of female candidates in the electoral lists of every party. In a study on women's representation in Indonesia's legislative bodies, it was found that despite this quota, the ratio of female elected officials lie in the lack of "political experience, networking and financial support". Furthermore, the low number of women in politics is also due to the fact that male candidates is dependent on a culture that depicts women as having less capacity than men.

## Economic participation and labor

Indonesia is among the five most improved countries in terms of economic participation and opportunity and it is ranked 88th in the respective index. According to the World Bank, Indonesia's female labor force participation rate has increased by more than 5% and jumping 28 positions up. Currently, 55% of leadership and senior positions are held by women professionals. Not only is Indonesia one of the six countries in the world where women hold more leadership and senior positions than men, it also obtains the highest share of women in such roles.

Despite these outstanding improvements, Indonesia is still facing relevant challenges. Specifically, women's participation in the labor market remains low at 58% compared to 74% for men. In the last 6 years the share of working women increased by about 4%. Most of women work in small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) that are often informal or family-owned, with a high concentration in the non-agricultural sector and on average, they get paid 50% less than men. Millions of Indonesian women work in foreign countries in the domestic sector, and in many countries, they face "protectionism" and discrimination due to their gender and ethnic background and human conditions. 35% of small and medium businesses are owned by female entrepreneurs, but mostly they have limited access to credit and other financial services as well as training programs that they have to face a significant lack in knowledge of business management, poor access to financial intermediaries and the high cost of obtaining loans from government institutions. Overall, the 2015 Indonesia's Gender Analysis reports that the most important challenges that women among the labor market and on the micro-level derive from family and state care.
