**Page 210**
---

The outbreak of COVID-19 pandemic can have disastrous consequences on women, who make up the majority of caregivers and health workers in Indonesia, saving millions of lives while putting themselves at higher risk from the SARS-CoV-2 pandemic.

McKinsey and Company (2018) provided a strategy aimed at advancing women’s equality in Asia Pacific and specifically focusing on Indonesia. The strategy was based on four main points. First, it advised to improve access to education for girls by providing scholarships and reforming the transportation infrastructure in light of the needs of working mothers. Second, the strategy outlined the necessity to expand training programmes for women such as business training for entrepreneurs, financial literacy, and life skills training. Third, it recommended to work on public awareness aimed at changing attitudes towards females and their roles in the society. Fourth, it suggested that a social protection programme aimed at allowing women to actually work (e.g., part-time work; parental leave; discrimination in hiring procedures) and for more protection for women from abuses and discrimination10.

### 5.3. Lao People’s Democratic Republic (Lao PDR)

Lao PDR is a landlocked country with a population of 7.4 million of which women accounted for 50.1%2, 3. The majority of the population live in rural areas and 35.7 % of the population is urban2, 3. Changes in rainfall and temperature have consequences on ecosystems, agriculture, and livelihoods that depend on them2, 4. Losses of different crops are due to environmental disasters such as floods, droughts, and men-ethnic minority groups in a different way. Female-headed households are likely to be particularly impacted by these changes5.

Households headed by women with children under the age of five years old or elderly people in Lao have average land size 16% smaller than male-headed households2, 3. A high proportion of food production is done by women and they are responsible for feeding their families. Women are responsible for raising livestock, which are crucial for women’s livelihoods, especially in disasters and emergencies. Unpredictable floods regularly destroy crops, severely impact women’s income opportunities, and increase the vulnerability to poverty2, 3.

Women face many challenges such as lack of access to education, land ownership rights, and women need to adopt extreme coping mechanisms, which include migration and child marriage6.

The Constitution of Lao PDR guarantees equality between men and women in politics, economy, culture, society, and the family7. In the voluntary review to the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development the government identified women’s empowerment as a crosscutting strategy to achieve SDG targets8. The government has developed a National Strategy on Women’s Empowerment (NSWE)9. Among other things, it includes five main strategies in the implementation of its sustainable development plan: (1) Improving the implementation of gender equality and women’s empowerment policies; (2) Strengthening national capacity to generate gender statistics and enable gender-sensitive planning; (3) Promoting women’s participation in decision-making processes with full potential of Lao Women’s participation in the market economy as entrepreneurs, employees, and consumers; and (4)Addressing the issues that prevent adolescent girls from reaching their full potential. The NSWE also includes a strategy to address the challenges or opportunities related to gender challenges or opportunities in relation to climate change10-13. The same is valid for the National Strategy on Climate Change.

Gender inequalities persist, particularly among rural households, poor households and ethnic minorities, especially in the highland areas where a large number of ethnic minorities live. Women in Lao play important roles in agriculture, small-scale businesses, manufacturing and provision of basic services such as health care and education. However, they face many challenges related to access to land, and costs of transportation. In rural areas and among ethnic communities, men exercise control over


---
**Page 211**
---

over the social life of their family which impacts on women’s freedom of movement, access to information and access to financial services[63].

With 49 ethnic groups, Lao PDR is one of the most ethnically diverse countries in the world. Poverty rates are highest among rural and ethnic communities. Ethnic women suffer from lack of opportunities in education and income generation, low mobility, time poverty, lack of access to land and productive assets, and gender-based violence.

### Women’s Participation in Decision Making
According to the World Economic Forum (2020), Lao PDR is ranked 135 out of 145 countries on women’s political empowerment. Women hold only 7% of seats in parliament, with 27.52% of women elected in the national parliament, which is above the world average[64]. However, women are underrepresented at lower levels of government and there are no women governors[65]. At the village level only 1.7% of village chief are women[66].

### Education
The gender gap in enrollment in primary and secondary education in Lao is closing: in primary education, 89.9% of boys and 89.4% of girls are enrolled; in secondary education, 37.0% of boys and 25.1% of girls are enrolled[67]. However, the gender gap persists in rural areas, particularly in the rural and poorest areas of the country, due to household labour, financial constraints, language barriers, and lack of access to schools for girls. Girls also have less time available to study due to safety concerns such as sexual harassment and gender-based violence, mobility constraints and child marriage[68]. Data also suggests that families prefer to educate boys instead of girls, particularly in rural areas where the cost of education is high[69]. This disparity is reflected in gender differentials in literacy rates: while the male literacy rate is 89.96%, only 79.09% of women are literate.

### Health and Gender-Based Violence
Gender-based violence is common in Lao, and 30% of women reported that a husband is justified to beat his wife[70]. Lao has one of the highest rates of child marriage in the region, with 39% of women married before age 18 in rural areas[71], including through arranged marriages. The prevalence of gender-based violence increases following a disaster. Gender-based violence in Lao increases after disasters; for example, following the floods and landslides in 2011, 45% of women reported experiencing episodes of rape and 43% reported knowing someone injured by sexual and gender-based violence[72]. The Law on Protection against Domestic Violence was adopted in 2018, but legal systems need to be strengthened in order to protect women and children from violence.

### Women’s Access to Healthcare
Women's access to healthcare is limited due to financial barriers, language, remote access, domestic responsibilities and restricted mobility, especially for women living in rural areas[73]. In the review of the 2018-2022 National Strategic Plan on Health, it was noted that there are poor coverage and quality of health services, highlighting that pregnancy rates, low contraceptive use and high fertility rates contribute to incidence of maternal mortality. The level of adolescent pregnancy is one of the highest in the region.

### Economy
Women’s labor force participation in Lao PDR is amongst the highest in the region at 79.5% for women and 81% for men, however the division of labor is strongly gendered and wage gaps persist[74]. Women work longer hours (36 minutes per day on average) compared to men (30 minutes per day)[75]. This suggests that women have also less time to engage in paid productive work.

Women are more likely to be informally employed (98.5%) than men (91.7%), and unpaid family workers (42% of women compared to 27% of men). Women dominate small-scale retail enterprises. The most common sectors for women’s micro and small enterprises are retail,


---
**Page 212**
---

verdiers, guesthouse and restaurants). These enterprises are more likely to be home-based. The Government recognized that promoting SMEs will be especially critical for women. It adds that "the legal framework for doing business does not have gender discrimination elements but in practice, household responsibilities and lack of mobility due to personal duties makes it more difficult for women."

The largest sector in Laos is agriculture, which employs 69% of women and 66% of men[28]. Women in agriculture mainly undertake tasks such as planting, harvesting and tending to smaller livestock. Women are also largely employed in the service sector and in garment factories, which employs more than half of all female workers among whom 85% are women aged 17-25. Normally they are hired on unskilled or semi-skilled roles[29].

Although women and men have equal status for land tenure, property ownership and inheritance rights, women generally have less benefit from these rights in terms of access to credit, life, customary laws and discriminatory practices that prevent women's access to land[30]. Join entitlement are not common in rural areas. Women tend to have more access to financial services. For example, more women than men bank account: 31% of women compared to 29% of men[31].

## Pakistan

Pakistan is the fifth most populated country in the world, and women make up 48.5 per cent of the Pakistani population[32]. Women suffer from pervasive gender-based violence, domestic abuse, honor killings, sexual violence, institutional discrimination, lack of access to sex and reproductive health services, and limited access to education, employment opportunities and other issues[33]. The International Monetary Fund estimates that, by addressing the gender parity, Pakistan would gain $9.1 billion per year.

The Government stated that it has four responsive policy frameworks: (1) Monitoring progress on the implementation of gender-responsive policy frameworks; (2) Create structures to support women in the labour market; (3) Establish systems and structures to develop, implement, monitor and evaluate policies and programs for women's empowerment; and (4) Strengthen structures to encourage women entrepreneurs; and (5) strengthen structures to combat violence against women[34]. Similarly, the Government stated that "women's economic growth is to increase women's labour force participation by investing in skills development programmes, ghid education and creating a conducive environment for women's employment"[35].

The National Climate Change Policy developed in 2012 stated that one of its goals is to "promote pro-poor adaptation strategies and policies that are gender-sensitive and inclusive" with specific cutting areas such as "disaster preparedness, poverty, gender, capacity building, technology transfer and climate change education"[36]. The National Climate Change Policy 2014-2020 included a paragraph on the gender dimensions of climate risk and resilience, which notes that gender differentials are critical to the success of any adaptation program and recognizes women's role in agriculture. Women play an important role in managing their families and their livelihoods; they do not enjoy equal authority, decision-making power and resources needed to adapt to climate change[37]. The policy also states that women are more vulnerable than men to climate change, due to their limited access and control over decision-making and economic resources. It states that the government is "determined to support gender integration into various efforts to address climate change"[38].

The National Sustainable Development Strategy recognizes the important role that women play in agriculture, including livestock raising, small cottage industries and feeding their households.


---
**Page 213**
---

remarks women’s participation at all levels of the crop production process, such as sowing, transplanting, harvesting and post-harvest operations. However, women do not have equal rights to land ownership and development.

### Political Participation

According to the Global Gender Gap Report 2020 Pakistan ranks 93 out of 152 countries for political empowerment of women[8]. Quotas are used to ensure a minimal presence of women in elected positions. For example, at least one-third of seats in the Senate are reserved for women. It was reported that women participate actively as political party members, however they are not able to secure leadership positions within parties, with the exception of some women leaders.

### Health and Gender Based Violence

Violence against women and girls, including domestic violence, rape, so-called “honour” killings, acid attacks, forced marriage and frequent is pervasive. 21% percent of married women in Pakistan have experienced physical violence by an intimate partner. 40% of ever-married women have experienced physical violence and 40 per cent of ever-married women have suffered from spousal abuse[9]. Gender based violence occurs widely in communities even before emergencies, and it is likely to increase during the emergency period. Lack of access to safe shelter and insecure physical environment adds further risks for women. Oxfam estimated that levels of threats, harassment and sexual violence against women increased by 20% in Pakistan during the 2015 floods[10]. Furthermore, Pakistan has one of Asia’s highest rates of maternal mortality, and malnutrition severely affected the most vulnerable, including children and women of reproductive age.

### Education

Pakistan has a gender gap in education of almost 20%. There are striking gender disparities in education, across the entire country. Less than half of women are literate (46%), compared to 75% of men[11]. Women’s access to education is systematically lower than the share of men across primary, secondary and tertiary education[12].

### Economic Participation and Labour

The levels of women's financial inclusion in Pakistan is low. The market is underserved and there would be significant potential to expand women's access through financial inclusion initiatives. In 2019, the government launched its National Financial Inclusion Strategy that sets targets to pursuit universal financial access, it aims at providing access to formal accounts to 50 percent of adult population by 2025[13].

Only one-quarter of women participate in the labour force compared with 80% of men. It is estimated that only 47% of women are economically active and 69% of them are informally employed versus only 27% of men[14]. Often women do not accept paid employment due to lack of childcare facilities, transport or because they are married[15]. Almost 40% of women who are not working reported that the main reason for this is that their male members do not permit them to work outside the home[16]. Those who work, are mainly employed in farms.

Women have less access than men to livelihood and productive assets such as land, employment and livestock and lower control over these resources. In emergencies, access to and control of livelihood become even more limited.


### 5.5. Papua New Guinea

Papua New Guinea (PNG) population is 8.3 million; 51% men and 49% women[17]. Notably, the population is one of the most diverse in the world due to the mountainous geography of the country and remoteness of many islands, which have kept communities separate. Its social


---
**Page 214**
---

# Gender equality and women empowerment are significant challenges in PNG

Systemic violations of women’s rights exist throughout the country. The country scores low on all major gender inequality indices, including the UNDP’s Human Development Index (HDI), the World Economic Forum’s Global Gender Gap Report, the UNDP’s Gender Inequality Index (GII) and the 127th position out of 153 countries in the Global Gender Gap Index.

Violence against women is extremely high and PNG's social norms often exclude women from leadership roles. Women are underrepresented in all levels of government, with only one woman in the cabinet (in 2016) where there aren’t women in the parliament. Deep-rooted discriminatory norms reinforce patriarchal systems and biases; women in PNG are subjected to harmful practices, such as child marriage, early pregnancy, and female genital mutilation.

The National Framework of Action 2017-2030 acknowledges the importance of women’s participation, calling for an inclusive engagement without discriminations and for the promotion of women’s empowerment. The framework also calls on all sectors to integrate gender equality into their policies, plans and programmes and mentions that “adequate capacity building measures need to be taken to empower women for preparedness as well as build their capacity for alternate livelihoods.” However, the National Framework of Action 2017-2030 is not effectively incorporated into the climate change adaptation and disaster management regulations and documents, which are in place at all levels.

## Health and gender-based violence

Gender-based violence including domestic violence is pervasive. 65% of women in PNG has been hit by their husbands[8]. PNG has one of the highest rates of maternal death in the world, only slightly more than 50% of women and girls give birth in a health facility. The infant mortality rate is 124 deaths per 1,000 live births; 100 infants die from pregnancy related causes; and the adolescent birth rate is 57.2 births per 1,000 women aged between 15-19 years in 2016. The Government adopted a National Strategy to Prevent and Respond to Gender Based Violence 2018-2023, the response to end violence remains inadequate and fragmented.

## Education

More than a third (37%) of the population is illiterate with the majority living in rural areas, which are often far from schools. There is a persistent gender gap in school enrolment, mainly driven by social cultural norms that place greater value on boys’ education. Only 40% of girls aged between 15 and 19 old enough to contribute into the household or get married. 36.8% of women adolescent are out-of-school, compare don’t only 18% of men[9]. With regards to primary education, 71% per cent of women in PNG do not complete their primary school education compared with 50% for men and 29% and 35% men. Many girls leave schools after puberty due to the lack of schools, sexual harassment and early marriage. The gender gap is also evident at secondary level where gender inequalities are also present through tertiary and technical and vocational education: university enrolments comprise 61% men and only 39% women[10]. Furthermore, in universities there is a strong sex segregation with women being concentrated in the fields of education, nursing, and commerce.


---
**Page 215**
---

Political participation. Cultural and structural barriers prevent women from participating in political life and currently in PNG there are no women in parliament. The country ranks 17th (out of 53 countries) in the Gender Political Empowerment index [2]. In 2017, for the first time in 25 years no woman was able secure a seat in the parliament, despite more female candidates running than ever before.

Economic participation and labour. According to the most recent national census conducted in 2011, about 88% of the population lives in rural areas, relying subsistence agricultural and informal cash crop activities [3]. PNG has the highest poverty rate in the South Pacific, with 40% of its population living below the poverty line. The lack of access to financial services impact women, limiting their access financial services and products as well as their access to livelihoods opportunities. Women are more likely than men to be engaged in informal economic activities [3]. Only one woman is waged workers compared to 33% of men, and 85% of women are in vulnerable employment compared with only 40% of men. Women are also less likely to be business owners in the informal sector and engage in crafts, small-scale farming and trading. They face relevant barriers to access adequate and small loans to finance their businesses [3].

Many rural women are subsistent farmers, responsible for producing food for household consumption and selling livestock such as pigs and chickens [4]. In the coastal region of the country, women use to catch small fish and harvest various shellfish for household consumption and local sale [5].

The formal financial inclusion gender gap is 29%, the highest in the South Pacific. This is reflected in a relatively low level of female ownership of bank accounts compared with male ownership of financial products than women[6]. Socioeconomic norms limit women’s control over their financial resources and property, and women are often solely responsible for household chores and expenses, with husbands or fathers placing demands on women's finances, leaving a very limited opportunity for them to save money[7].

5.6. Timor-Leste

The Democratic Republic of Timor Leste has a population of 1-3 million people, 49% of whom are women [8]. The majority live in rural areas (80%) and there is a significant socio-economic divide, with rural women and men having lower education, less employment and more poverty[9]. Rural populations in Timor-Leste have significantly lower levels of access to a range of services, such as water supply, sanitation, electricity and health care [10].

Timor Leste Strategic Development Plan 2011-2030 calls for reforms on the areas of human capital, infrastructure, market formation, economy diversification and regulatory development. The plan also highlights the importance of commitment to gender equality and women empowerment, setting the goal of making Timor-Leste a “gender-equal society” where women’s dignity and women's rights are valued and protected[10].

The National Adaptation programme of action on climate change includes provisions on gender equality and women’s empowerment as part of its cost of climate change. It stresses the importance of improving both institutional and community capacity, including women and vulnerable groups, and increasing their participation and representation in decision-making [11]. A new practice of integrating gender into water management, in particular for addressing vulnerable people's rights including women in water related legislation making and its implementation[12]. The programme aimed at reducing the vulnerability of communities to climate change was supported by the Green Climate Fund (2019) noted that it is crucial to have a gender-focused approach to address this.


---
**Page 216**
---

# Political Participation

Timor-Leste is a country where women’s political rights and men in power are still lacking, implying that "the direct and active participation by men and women in political life is a requirement of, and a fundamental instrument for consolidating, the democratic system" and stressing that "the law shall promote equality in the exercise of civil and political rights and non-discrimination on grounds of sex." Women's representation in parliament has been increasing over time but still faces many challenges in attaining political equality due to lack of family support, limited confidence in their own abilities, and fear of being targeted for violence. The 2015 election showed that in East Timor there is a strong and institutionalized domination of the political arena by men, especially in the National Parliament. This was partly due to the electoral law which introduced the "elokot system" introduced in 2006, which requires that women comprise at least one-third of listed candidates. Currently there are 40% of women in parliament. However, women have almost no representation in government positions or in the executive branch. The law on the Election of the National Parliament ensures women's representation with a quota that requires one in every three members to be female. However, this does not guarantee women’s political influence and leadership. In turn, women do not frequently occupy key positions in the government, such as ministers or secretaries of state.

# Education

Gaps in the education system generated low levels of education and literacy among the adult population. According to the 2015 census, only 48% of adults aged 15 years old and above have completed primary school. The proportion of women who have completed secondary or tertiary education has increased from 37% to 46%. In 2015, 49.4% of women are literate, compared to 71.87% of men[1]. Women are less likely than men to have participated in formal education and are more likely to be illiterate or semi-literate, resulting in being less well suited post primary education. 58% of women older than 25 have never been to school, compared with 43% of men; and only 16% of women in the same age group have completed secondary education[2]. The reasons for this are mainly due to high dropout rates remain high, due to lack of school readiness and language barriers[3]. Notably, sexual harassment is a major problem that affects girls' access to education. Women are under-represented in technical and vocational education. Women (56%) are normally enrolled in administration, finance, and information technology programs; while men are commonly enrolled in technical programs such as construction and agriculture.

# Health and Gender-Based Violence

Gender-based violence remains widespread and is one of the highest in the world with 59% of ever-partnered women aged 15-49 years experiencing violence by a current or former partner. The majority of cases are domestic violence and early marriage. The traditional patriarchal system has a negative relevant impact to the empowerment and well-being of women. The idea that women are subordinate "property" originates from this system, which is still prevalent in many communities today. Limited access to justice is exacerbated by limited capacity of the formal criminal justice system, and cases of violence against women are reported only rarely.

Timor-Leste has a high birth rate accompanied by high maternal and child death rates; maternal mortality is still amongst the highest in the world at 57 deaths per 100,000 live births[4]. 60% of women have reported problems in accessing healthcare[5]. The percent of teenage girls (age [15-19]) are among the highest in the world and many of them still marry before reaching the age of 18.


---
**Page 217**
---

# Economic participation

Women in Timor-Leste have limited awareness and resources to control productive assets, such as financial services, land, marketing facilities and information. Although women are responsible for most of the agricultural production, it is always in the hands of men and passed down to the men of the family [139]. The division of labour in Timor-Leste is also gendered, with women being responsible for childcare and care work. Women and girls bear responsibilities for household work, reproductive work and caring for the elderly and disabled. This limits their ability to participate fully in economic activities such as internal small-scale trading, and home-based industries. This is due to gender-based barriers such as lower levels of literacy, education and training; lack of professional skills in the formal sector; low support from family members for women’s work outside reproductive obligations; and mobility constraints due to safety and security concerns [141-142] [150-156].

Women's participation in the labor force is substantially lower than men's participation: 56% of men are employed as compared with only 38% for women. Women are concentrated in wholesale and retail trade sectors (25% of employed women) and in education, health, arts, and other low paid sectors [120]. Women are also more likely to be employed in the informal sector than men: 47% as compared with only 38%. The informal sector is dominated by the service sector (60%), followed by services and sales [158-159].

The activities performed within these sectors are largely unskilled, repetitive and low paid. Women work in a wide range of jobs that are often more intensive and paid better, such as working in rice fields, coffee growing and selling livestock. On the contrary, women common activities include planting, weeding, and harvesting [157]. Women play an important role in food production: they produce 80% of all vegetables grown for home consumption; they are responsible for 90% of all fruit trees planted on farms; and they contribute to 60% of the total agricultural production. In addition, women produce 15% less per hectare of land compared with men [157].

# 5.1 Vanuatu

The Republic of Vanuatu (Vanuatu) is an island country located in the South Pacific Ocean and inhabited by 296,434 people (50.8% men and 49.1% women), out of which three-quarters live in rural areas [157]. Its economy is mainly based on small-scale agriculture, fishing, offshore financial services and tourism. Vanuatu’s economy is also heavily dependent on the export of a few commodity exports, vulnerability to natural disasters, and long distances from main markets [158].

The National Constitution of Vanuatu recognises the rights and freedoms of all individuals without discrimination on the grounds of sex, race, place of origin, religious or traditional beliefs, political opinions, language, property ownership, social status, disability, age, marital status, nationality, or advancement of females, children and young persons, members of under-privileged groups or inhabitants of any particular area [159]. The Government of Vanuatu recognises that gender equality and women’s empowerment are critical to the achievement of the


---
**Page 218**
---

national development vision: "a just, Educated, Healthy and Wealthy Vanuatu". One of the key 'society goals' or policy objectives identified in the National Sustainable Development Plan for 2016 to 2030 is an "inclusive society which upholds human dignity and where the rights of all Ni- Vanuatu including women, youth, the elderly and vulnerable groups are supported, protected and promoted". In 2018, the Government of Vanuatu launched the National Gender Equality Policy (NGEP), to 'promote equal rights, opportunities and responsibilities for men and women'. The NGEP was developed in response to gender-based discrimination and violence against women and girls[17]. It was one of the first policies to explicitly address gender inequality in the country. Following endorsement by the Council of Ministers, the NGEP was launched on 20th March 2018 [18] it lapsed in 2019, and was replaced by the National Gender Equality Policy 2020-2030.

Women in Vanuatu are more affected than men to climate change due to intersectional factors, such as their lower levels of education, in terms of literacy and decision-making bodies, low access to financial services and productive assets, and a high rate of gender-based violence. This differentiated vulnerability is recognised in the Vanuatu Climate Change and Disaster Risk Reduction Strategy 2018-2030[19]. The strategy recognises that climate change adaptation, disaster risk reduction efforts and gender and social inclusion are considered as one of the country's ongoing strategic priority issues.

While the country’s Constitution and key national plans and strategies articulate the importance of equality and rights, significant disparities continue to exist between men and women. Vanuatu Global Gender Gap Index is low; as the country scores 126 out of 153[20].

Health and gender-based violence: The 2011 National Survey on Women’s Lives and Family Relationships in Vanuatu, conducted by the Department of Statistics and Research, highlighted that violence against women is pervasive and occurs across all socio-economic backgrounds, with peaks in rural areas[21]. Approximately 60% of women who have ever been married or in a union report having experienced physical or sexual violence. Women experienced both physical and/or sexual violence in their lives; of whom 21% were left with permanent injuries and 65% were left with psychological trauma.

Women experience violence before, during and after disasters: it was reported that domestic violence increased by 300% after two tropical cyclones in 2011[22]. UN Women found that; "98% of victims of violence do not access the formal justice system, for lack of economic resources or for the fear of further victimisation". The lack of awareness about their rights and the absence of gender and lack of a gender inclusive frameworks prevent policy-makers from recognizing and tackling violence against women[23].

Education: Literacy rates vary between urban and rural areas, and between ages. There aren’t strong gender-based differences in the adult population where the literacy rate for adult men stands at 87% and for adult women at 86%. However, only 87% of youth female (aged 15-24) are literate[24]. In rural areas, only 30% of women aged between 15 to 24 years have any completed primary education or have had no schooling at all[25]. In rural areas 80% of women reported that they do not have access to formal education. Often, older people can only communicate using the local vernacular language. Notably, the education curriculum includes the skills and gender-based violence awareness and prevention.

**Page 219**

# Political participation

Women are excluded from decision making and are absent from leadership positions. Vanuatu is one of the two countries in the world (together with Papua New Guinea) where there are no women in parliament since its independence in 1980, only 5 women have been elected into the national parliament[102].

The Constitution of Vanuatu recognizes gender equality as part of fundamental rights, recognizing that women and men enjoy equal rights. However, this provision provides affirmative actions for the advancement of women. However, women have a limited access to decision making process in formal governance structures; this is mainly due to women's care obligations and discrimination against women in the family[103]. Women are also excluded from the village decision-making body, the nakamal, or the basic unit of kastom[1] governance; in some cases women are not even allowed to enter the village meeting place. This is referred to as "women in me". This exclusion continues at higher levels, where regional and national government structures are normally male dominated.

Women's lack of direct participation in decision-making is reinforced by other barriers, such as low literacy rates, access to health and maternal mortality[104]. In turn, Vanuatu women are marginalized from discussions and decisions on areas of social and economic development, environment, governance and human rights at all levels. It is common, during public discussion focused on these topics, to note the under-representation of women and the silencing of their inputs.

At the policy making level there is limited technical capacity for gender analysis and gender-responsive budgeting. This is exacerbated by the lack in collection and analysis of sex disaggregated data[105].

# Economic participation and labour

Women represent 61% of the rural labor force compared to 79% for men, while only 32% of urban women are employed versus 71% of men and in rural areas. 13% of women and 28% of men are employed[106]. More than 40% of working-age women declared reported having no income from any source compared to less than 5% for working age men (Government of Vanuatu, 2016). Of the total workforce, 63.6% of women engaged in unpaid work and only 7.8% were employed in paid work[107]. Women are more involved in the subsistence economy[108], which makes them more susceptible to poverty, climate change, disasters and other livelihood stresses. Levels of economic development are low, but rural and coastal areas have been particularly affected by cyclones, floods and droughts leading to damages in property and food crops and reduced income from tourism[109].

Women are less financially included than men: only 32% of adult women reporting a bank account compared with 54% for men. Women are also much less likely to be formally employed and to have an account at a formal institution, women appear worse to deposit and withdraw more frequently than men do; with 65% of women reported saving in the past year, compared with 53% of men[107].

The contribution of rural women to the agricultural sector is significant: women are represent about half of the workforce in agriculture (49.5%) and spend more time than men on agricultural production and income generating activities[110]. The division of labour in agriculture reflect


---
**Page 220**
---

traditional gender roles: while both men and women practice daily farming, women are in charge of selling products, often among fishing communities**. For instance, women have a heavier workload than men due to the traditionally contracted norm that assigns to women, in addition to agricultural work, responsibilities for small-scale marketing, handcraft production, caregiving and housework**.

## 6. GENDER ISSUES IN THE CONTEXT OF THE CRPP

As outlined in the introduction, the CRPP comprises two separate but interlinked components: the Climate Resilience Program (CRP) and the Gender Window. The CRP is a financing environment required for financing and implementing local adaptation measures at scale; and the gender window is an enabling mechanism that will provide support to women’s resilience through downstream investments. The TW will support implementation of outputs 1,2 and 3 of the wider CRPP program. Output 4,5 and 6 are part of the CRP itself. The Gender Window will provide grant and loan resources to implement output 4, 5 and 6 of the wider CRPP program. Accordingly, this section is divided into two subsections: The first sub-section describes some of the key areas of intervention for the CRPP Gender Window, including its specific focus area, scope, and the types of interventions that will be implemented through the CRPP TW Gender Window. This second sub-section provides a more detailed description of the specific support provided to climate change in the context of the CRPP IF and its key areas of intervention, and the types of interventions that will be supported by the CRPP to build their adaptive capacity. The two sub-section provide the basis for the CRPP Gender Action Plan.

### 6.1. The CRPP Trust Fund

The CRPP Gender Window will support the identification, development, and implementation of investments that have the explicit objective of strengthening the resilience of women to climate change. As such, support will be provided through (i) knowledge support and technical assistance for identifying and preparing investment proposals; (ii) financial resources for financing women-focused adaptation measures; (iii) transactional technical assistance for preparing investments in the CRPP IF. The CRPP Gender Window will provide grant and loan resources to investments identified and prepared under the technical assistance. This section identifies some of the specific activities that the CRPP can support under these modalities, and the rationale for including them in the CRPP.

#### 6.1.1. Output 1: Knowledge and action research on climate risk-informed poor-pro community level solutions strengthened

Substantial gaps remain in many countries to generate the necessary policy will, and commitment to invest in women-focused adaptation measures that help tackle climate change. There is thus a need to generate a deeper understanding of women’s vulnerability context in CRPP countries, and generate an evidence base for demonstrating how investments can be made to address these vulnerabilities and building the resilience of women. Furthermore, there is a need to demonstrate how women’s contributions are being recognized and valued within the CRPP IF. This will help identify where women are implementing adaptation initiatives that are having positive impacts, how they can be scaled up.

To this end, the CRPP will support governments and communities in undertaking action research studies that address gaps in understanding around women and resilience in CRPP countries and make the case for women-focused adaptation investments. In doing so, the CRPP


---
**Page 221**
---

will contribute to a deeper and more contextualized understanding of the specific situation of women, their diverse conditions, and how they are impacted by climate change in both the short and long term, including identifying issues related to structural inequality between men and women in specific geographical areas that contribute to women's vulnerability to climate change. This activity will be supported through a gender window approach, which includes targeted adaptation, collecting data and information to build evidence, and compiling lessons learned from research and practice to inform policy and investment decisions on adaptation investments. In this turn should inspire political and community support for investing in women-led, local-level adaptation at scale.

As part of the evidence generating and lesson learning process, the CRPP will also support innovative pilot activities at the community level to identify and test women-focused adaptation measures that could be scaled up through larger investments in the future. These activities will be led by women's organizations rather than civil works, and they will be designed and led by women, providing a dedicated space for women to trial and test adaptation measures with them at the centre. This will provide an opportunity for women to share their knowledge of climate change impacts on their livelihoods and present their achievements to local governments so that, in future, women can increasingly be recognized as partners for resilience building by local governments.

Increased investment in local, women-focused adaptation could begin to shift the dominant trends in many CRPP countries. This will require key stakeholders to transform current business as usual practice. Work is needed to build the capacity of ministries and sector agencies, as well as civil society organizations, to support this transition. It also requires a focus on women-focused investments in climate resilience, and how this can be achieved. It is also important that women's voices are heard at all levels of decision-making, including in adaptation programming, and that their practical needs, interests, vulnerabilities, and capacities are represented in policy plans, and standards that inform women-centered adaptation investments.

The CRPP Gender Window will support the development of a gender strategy at local, national, and regional levels that bring together grassroots women's organizations, government ministries and sector agencies, civil society organizations, and other relevant stakeholders. Locally organized, providing a platform for exchange and collaboration between actors that may not traditionally work with each other. The CRPP Gender Window will also support multiple iterations of dialogue, CRPP support through the Gender Window can contribute to a change in culture within governments and civil society that is more supportive of women-focused investments in adaptation. This will be achieved by supporting the development of long-term objectives being to help create a movement for locally appropriate women-focused adaptation investment through the CRPP country, and to promote women's leadership in adaptation.

### 6.1.2. Objective 2: Institutionalizing community capacity to develop and deliver climate adaptation investments strategically

Creating policy incentives and procedures for the inclusion of women in adaptation decision-making at the local level and building their capacity to use participatory tools and procedures for climate change adaptation, including gender analysis, will be critical to ensuring that they can act on adaptation. The CRPP Gender Window will therefore support the development of climate risk assessment and decision support tools and training and capacity-building for decision-makers and implementers at all levels of government in the countries that address the specific needs of women, and integrate design features that promote women's engagement in adaptation decision-making. This will be achieved by supporting the development of long-term objectives requiring improved coordination and communication at all levels of government and also at the project level.

---
**Page 222**
---

This will require greater vertical integration among municipal provincial and national governments policies and practices that aim to build women’s resilience to climate change. In addition, ensuring the meaningful participation of women in adaptation decision-making beyond the program life-cycle will require strengthening the institutions and processes that provide the necessary enabling environment for women’s empowerment. The CRPP Gender Window will support governments to create new and/or improve existing policy frameworks and communication and coordination mechanisms to ensure gender equality and inclusion across gender, climate, and key sectors such as agriculture, land-use planning, and urban planning.

### 6.1.3 Output 3: Inclusive and pro-poor adaptation investment projects identified and prepared

The CRPP will seek to increase the climate resilience of women through leveraging sizable investments in adaptation projects and replicating effective interventions. As such, the CRPP Gender Window will support the preparation of large-scale investments that explicitly strengthen the climate resilience of women, particularly poor women, and/or adaptation investments that are led by women. The CRPP Gender Window will also seek to ensure gender equality at all levels of decision-making using inclusive design processes. A number of key design features and approaches have been identified as critical to ensuring that women are at the centre of the design process. These include:

- **The collection and analysis of sex-disaggregated data** to understand the specific vulnerability characteristics of women so that they can be addressed through the project activities.
- **Identifying outputs and related activities that address structural inequalities between men and women, including gender-based violence**, which are barriers to adaptation and contribute to transformative change (e.g. policy reforms; revision of regulations, information management; the provision of equal wages; ensuring participation of women in microfinance schemes).
- **Integrating approaches that aim to create a structural, cultural, and financial base that can provide women with greater access to resources** for climate adaptation and individual capacity across the public and private sectors and civil society; developing leadership skills among women and girls through education and training programs and supporting change makers in positions of influence that can drive change forward and advance the progress made under the interventions.
- **Including a gender assessment in the monitoring and evaluation frameworks** for all CRPP-supported projects and increase women’s capacity to assess the efficiency and effectiveness of adaptation measures and institutional learning and information generation practices.

### 6.2 The CRPP Investment Fund

The CRPP will provide grant and loan financing to seven selected DMCLs, namely, Cambodia, Indonesia, Lao PDR, Pakistan, PNG, Timor-Leste, and Vanuatu, to implement local adaptation measures that are designed to address the needs of women and the poor vulnerable population. The CRPP IF will focus on the efficient roll-out of local adaptation measures through downstream investments in response to climate change impacts. The gender assessment will assess gender concerns in the context of the key activity areas that will be the focus of each of the CRPP IF outputs. The gender assessment is structured according to the key activity areas, although many of the issues discussed are applicable to other sectors as well.

It should be noted that although the Gender Window is funded by the CRPP IF, it can be leveraged to enhance gender equality outcomes of the Investment Fund in CRPP countries. Examples include: (i) generating the necessary political will and commitment from governments and communities to invest in women-focused adaptation measures that help tackle climate change; (ii) supporting the development of participatory tools for climate risk information decision-making at the local level and building their capacity to use participatory tools and procedures, including gender analysis, development of gender-sensitive indicators which can empower local women to act on adaptation; and (iii) place women at the centre of the design of large-scale investments that explicitly strengthen the climate resilience of women, particularly poor women who are disproportionately affected by climate change.

Output 4: Information systems and tools for delivering applied climate-risk informed investments at scale improved.


A. Women's access to climate information and early warning

Women typically have less access to all types of information including climate information and early warnings[1]. As detailed in the previous section, gender-based differences in literacy rates and access to technology are a major driver of these inequalities with significant differences more pronounced in rural than urban areas and in some countries more than others. In Pakistan for example, women have less access to mobile phones (46%) compared to men (70%). Similarly, in Cambodia where gender gaps in education are more pronounced present in rural areas. These lower levels of awareness and literacy exacerbate women's exposure and vulnerability to climate hazards[2]. Understanding the link between gender equality and adaptation is critical as a lot to climate change requires a robust understanding of current and future risk as the starting point. Understanding how these risks vary by gender, age, location and other factors is key to recognizing the different impacts that climate change has on women and men thus informing the generation and delivery of climate-risk services[3]. This in turn generates a deeper understanding of vulnerability and vulnerability context in CRPP countries and forms an evidence base for climate adaptation interventions that are effective in reducing these vulnerabilities. The collection, analysis and use of gender-disaggregated data is critical to building this knowledge as it is critical to identifying the most suitable interventions to address climate risk. The collection and analysis of gender-disaggregated data to understand the specific vulnerability characteristics of women and men is a key part of the design and implementation of investments. The CRPP will also take specific steps to improve access to climate information as part of its investments in improving climate information services.


B. Women's access to adaptive social protection

Social protection programmes can increase resilience to climate change through enhancing early adaptation actions and livelihoods improvement and facilitates migration mitigation during climate shocks[4]. However, these programmes are often not gender-sensitive or present exclusionary mechanisms. In the long run, these programmes can support livelihood diversification, poverty and vulnerability reduction for women and men in CRPP countries. Women have been linked with apprenticeship schemes and skills transfer programmes and social and financial inclusion initiatives[5]. Despite increasing awareness of the need to explicitly target women beneficiaries, women are still under-represented in social protection programmes due to higher dropout rates and substantially lower benefit levels[6]. Experience from two CRPP countries with well-


---
**Page 224**
---

established social protection programs, namely Indonesia and Pakistan, show that significant gaps remain in reaching women. In Indonesia, insufficient use of up-to-date sex-disaggregated data for monitoring and targeting of beneficiaries, complicated information about protective measures and limited assistance available from official sources, and inadequate support to complete the registration process have all contributed to this gap. The fact that there are large gender gaps in mobile phone ownership and national ID possession, means that women are at risk of being left behind in accessing social protection programs such as the Cash for Work program, which was a key part of the government's Covid-19 response[25]. Experiential form Indonesia also shows that women must feel safe to travel to collect social protection payments and this must be considered when designing cash transfer programs. The proportion of women in relation to men in relation to GBV is known to increase in the aftermath of environmental shocks[26].

As part of its investments in climate risk informed adaptive social protection programs, the CRPP will seek to ensure that all social protection programs are gender-sensitive and inclusive, ensuring that social protection programs include specific identification of, and outreach to, women, using sex-disaggregated data for targeting beneficiaries. It will acknowledge and respond to differences in women's needs and vulnerabilities as a result of their different roles within households and the local communities they live in, and the services they need. Investments will seek to contribute to transformative gender equality by addressing the underlying causes of poverty and vulnerability that prevent women's socio-economic disadvantage, better responding to women’s rights. Women’s empowerment is an important element of this approach and a range of activities aimed at empowering women will be provided as part of the related activities. This can include, linking cash transfers to resilient livelihoods training and sensitizing beneficiary households, programme managers and service providers on gender equality and women's empowerment. The CRPP will also seek to ensure that women are involved in the design, implementation and monitoring of investments in adaptive social protection programs, including through the establishment of women’s groups and the strengthening of existing schemes. The CRPP will also seek to build partnerships between government and community-based women’s groups to effectively deliver social protection programs empowering these local organizations and strengthening the transformational potential of the social protection program.

### C. Women's participation in decision-making

The meaningful participation of women is crucial to the design and implementation of efficient resilience-building at all levels. The needs and priorities need to take into consideration the specific situation of women, their diverse conditions and how they are impacted by disasters and climate change in both the short and long run. However, as the review in the previous section shows, women’s voices are often not heard or taken seriously when decisions about resilience-building are absent from leadership positions. This is particularly true at the local level. This is often due to discrimination against women and their exclusion from decision-making processes. Women's voices are not participating, such as in Vanuatu were women are excluded from direct roles in the village decision making body, and in some cases women are invited to attend but decisions are already made by the men[27]. In Indonesia, women’s participation is also low at all levels of political engagement having a constitution that affirms the right of equal participation of women and men in political life. Even though women make up 30% of the population, they only hold 14% of seats in parliament entirely as party list members and a proportion of elected officials in the national parliament are women[28]. In Pakistan, women’s representation is even lower at just over 5%. The approaches that involving women in designing investment projects for resilience is critical, and so investments will be designed through inclusive processes where women are engaged at all levels of decision-making. This will ensure that women's voices are heard and their needs addressed to strengthen women's self-reliance, leadership and confidence. Experience from Indonesia shows

# 6.2.2 Output 5: Climate resilient pro-poor livelihoods investments implemented.

## D. Women’s access to agricultural extension services

The review presented in section 6 clearly highlights how women are overrepresented in agriculture and their critical role in climate resilience in CRPP countries, yet on subsistence farming for their livelihoods and food security. The agricultural sector is often the hardest hit by extreme weather events and this reliance on natural resource-based livelihoods makes women more vulnerable to these impacts. Women also face barriers when it comes to accessing environmental conditions. Therefore, strengthening the resilience of agro-ecological systems to climate change requires a gender-sensitive approach. This includes increasing the role of agricultural extension and rural advisory services as crucial for supporting women to adopt improved climate-resilient practices. However, despite their critical role in gender gaps in access to extension services are present in many CRPP countries, such as in Cambodia, where women receive only 10% of agricultural extensions services despite comprising 51% of the primary workforce in subsistence agriculture.

## E. Women’s access to microfinance for disaster resilience

Financial inclusion is central to managing risks as it means that individuals have access to useful and affordable financial products and services that meet their needs in times of crisis. Access to microfinance products that can provide financial relief is a critical component of women’s resilience against both the immediate impacts and long-term consequences of disasters and climate change. Microfinance institutions (MFIs) are increasingly recognizing this need, with the implementation of protection measures enhancing livelihoods and economic opportunities, supporting women in their recovery efforts after natural disasters and other shocks and stresses. Lower access to financial services is one of the factors that result in women being more likely to be affected, and to be least prepared for such shocks and stresses. As highlighted in the review of microfinance institutions’ cultural and social norms, many MFIs are excluding women from accessing these financial services in many CRPP countries, such as Pakistan, PNG, Timor-Leste, and Vanuatu. This is because women often lack access to formal identification documents and property and the consequent lack of collateral represents a barrier for women’s access to formal


---
**Page 226**
---

# D. Women’s role in agriculture supply chains

Informal employment is the dominant form of employment in Asia and women, especially from poor communities, are disproportionately dependent on informal employment that is characterised by low access to decent work with poor working conditions that can contribute to economic and welfare losses, damage to health and productivity, and forced labour migration. There is a strong gender bias against women’s participation in agriculture, which limits their voice and representation in leadership positions in many CRPP countries, and women’s economic activities are often undervalued and underpaid. Women in the informal economy are more likely to be employed in the informal economy (69%) compared to men (71%), and in Papua New Guinea (PNG), 80% of women work informally as compared to only 52% for men, while female workers comprised to 33% of men, and 85% of women are in vulnerable employment compared with 65% of men[9]. In Laos PDR, 85.5% of women are informally employed and dominate small-scale retail and vending activities such as street vendors, market traders, and informal food vendors. They juggle employment and family responsibilities. In Lao PDR, women spend significantly more time on unpaid domestic work than men (10 hours per day compared to 4 hours per day[10]). Agriculture is a sector characterised by informality and decent work deficits and the lack of social protection for workers in this sector are significant challenges.

Women’s role in agriculture and food security is not limited to planting, cultivation and harvesting. Women have significant involvement in storage, processing, logistics and sales, making them crucial actors in the agricultural value chain. Climate change impacts on women will be impacted by climate shocks and stresses. Heat extremes for example have been shown to cause exhaustion and dehydration among female workers who are involved in manual tasks inside the agricultural value chain. Flooding has been significantly disruptive informal food vendors’ sales and lead to heavy losses as perishable produce end up being sold at lower prices or discarded.

Sufficient and stable incomes and decent work are critical for climate resilience. Climate-induced loss of livelihoods, reduction in incomes, or deterioration of working conditions in the agriculture


---
**Page 227**
---

sector have particular implications for women. CRPP investments in increasing the resilience of agricultural supply chains provides an opportunity to address some of these specific structural inequalities and increase women's vulnerability to the impacts of climate change and strengthen women's economic empowerment. CRPP investments in this regard must seek to address gender-specific barriers that prevent women from accessing resources, increasing their access to control over income, promote women's engagement in agricultural entrepreneurship, and improve the quality of life for women through a greater share of their agricultural activities, as well as diversify their income-earning opportunities, and provide women with leadership roles. Investments should also integrate initiatives that reduce women's time poverty (e.g., water storage facilities) or increase their access to productive assets that can benefit them directly. Investments in labor-saving assets (e.g., running water, electricity) that allow for more efficient use of time can have a significant impact on the quality of life and results for their overall well-being and for climate resilience. Many of the interventions in this case may require physical/structural solutions such as wells, pumps, and day rooms in agro-processing units which employ engaged women and which can be included within CRPP project outputs.

### 6.2.3 Output 6: Climate adaptation pre-pro infrastructure implemented

#### E. Women's access and control over natural resources.

Through its investments in nature-based solutions for building resilience at the community level, the CRPP can promote women's access and control over natural resources. Women's reliance on natural resource-based livelihoods makes them more vulnerable when these resources are depleted or degraded by climate change. In addition to this vulnerability, women often face, due to social-cultural norms and patriarchal power structures they often face barriers to accessing and controlling natural resources. These barriers can be particularly pronounced in the planning and natural resources management, such as forest user groups, water use associations, and environmental policy-making. Due to these disadvantages, women can be less likely to benefit from projects that aim to improve access to natural resources or to implement cultural norms to make sure women and girls can access these resources is therefore essential to building resilience at the community level. In addition, CRPP investments in nature-based solutions can provide equitable benefits for women, and integrating gender considerations in initiatives related to water management, land use planning, and forest management is critical. If planned correctly, such investments can also have a co-benefit of reducing the daily workload of women associated with care and household work and reduce health and safety risks as well as production costs. CRPP investments should therefore aim at improving access to water, especially improving access to water for household use, making it easier and faster for women to collect water and reducing time spent on water collection and security risks on the way.

#### F. The participation of women in the identification, construction/installation and operation of small-scale adaptive infrastructure.

The identification, construction/installation and operation of small-scale adaptive infrastructure presents an opportunity to address gender inequalities and build the resilience of women beyond the project. In addition to this, specific measures to increase gender equality and women's empowerment will be included in the design and implementation of CRPP projects. However, women's participation in these activities remains extensive in many CRPP countries, primarily because of traditional attitudes about "appropriate" occupations for women and men. This is particularly true in the construction sector, where it is believed that women should not work with heavy machinery or in dangerous conditions. Bringing participation of women in meetings where public works are designed and selected, including

# Access to safe shelter

As the frequency and intensity of extreme climate events are expected to increase with global warming, exposure to food and cyclone hazards are likely to require increasing numbers of people to evacuate their homes in seek of safe shelter. Studies have shown that women are often reluctant to use emergency shelters, and there are frequent examples of women refusing to use emergency shelters because they are not designed for them. This is a problem that has existed since the past[1]. This is often because basic gender needs like separation toilet facilities, dedicated space for women to breastfeed their children or store their belongings are not taken into consideration during the design and management of emergency shelters. Evidence shows that women and girls are at greater risk than men in disaster situations due to lack of access to safe shelter and water, violence during emergency situations and in the aftermath of disasters in part because of poor emergency-shelter design and inadequate coordination by disaster responders[2][3]. In countries such as Pakistan and Bangladesh, women's shelters have been found to be unsafe and often sexually pervasive even before emergencies, insecure physical environments resulting from lack of privacy and security, and a lack of access to water and sanitation. The number of domestic violence increased by 300% after two tropical cyclones in 2014[4]. In Pakistan, Oxfam estimated that the number of women who were raped during Cyclone Aila in Bangladesh rose in Pakistan all increased during emergencies, and one of the major reasons for the increase in domestic violence during emergencies was said to be lack of proper shelter[5].

CRPP supported investments in safe shelter must therefore ensure that such design includes features that are specifically designed to meet women's needs rather than access. Collecting sex-disaggregated data as part of initial assessments and ensuring that women of all ages, including those from marginalized groups, are engaged in the process will help identify their specific needs and that designs respond accordingly. As such, women should be involved at all stages of the shelter programme, including settlement planning, beneficiary selection, design and implementation of shelters, and monitoring and evaluation. Women's participation in governance structures such as shelter or community committees, to support empowerment through participatory decision-making is also important terms of decision-making. Disaster preparedness and evacuation planning must also take into account women’s specific situation and ensure that appropriate measures are included.

# ABOUT THE ANNEX

This annex presents the gender action plan for the Community Resilience Partnership Program (CRPP). The accompanying Gender Assessment describes critical gender issues related to the types of investments that are likely to be supported by the CRPP, and how such investments can be designed to address these issues in order to contribute effectively to climate change. As such, the gender assessment provides the backdrop for the gender action plan, presented in this annex, which identifies specific actions, design features, and monitoring indicators to ensure that the CRPP is responsive to women’s needs and maximizes targeted investments.

## 2. INTRODUCTION

The CRPP is a regional partnership program of the Asian Development Bank (ADB) which aims to support governments and communities in Asia and the Pacific region scale up investments in climate adaptation, especially investments at the community level, that explicitly target the nexus between gender equality and climate change. The two main objectives of scaling up climate change adaptation at the community level are therefore core pillars of the CRPP: (1) operationalizing the Climate Risk Financing Facility (CRFF), which was established by ADB in August 2021 and comprises two separate but interlinked components, namely the Community Resilience Partnership Program Facility (CRPPF); and (2) creating an enabling environment required for implementing local adaptation measures at scale; and the CRPP will do this through a combination of upstream investments to create the enabling environment required for implementing local adaptation measures at scale; and the CRPP will do this through a combination of upstream investments to create the enabling environment required for implementing local adaptation measures at scale, and downstream investments through downstream investments.

The TF provides technical assistance and grant resources financed by development partners and administered by ADB’s Secretariat and/or its developing member countries (DMC) of the ADB to implement the following three outputs: (1) Knowledge and action research on climate risk informed investment in local adaptation measures at scale; (2) Strengthening country capacity to develop and deliver climate adaptation investments at community-level strengthened; and (3) Inclusive and pro-poor adaptation investment projects identified and presented for scaling up. The TF will also provide technical assistance and grants for identifying, developing, and implementing investments that are likely to be scaled-up in the future by other development partners or local adaptation investments led by women who have a specific focus on supporting women to build their resilience.

The IF will provide grant and loan financing to seven selected DMCs: namely, Cambodia, Indonesia, Laos PDR, Pakistan, PNG, Timor-Leste, and Vanuatu), to implement local adaptation measures at scale in order to reduce the vulnerability of the most vulnerable segments of the population. The IF will deliver three climate related outputs: (1) Information and systems for delivering applied climate-risk informed local investments at scale; (2) Climate resilient infrastructure investments implemented; and (3) Climate resilience building and adaptation infrastructure investments implemented.

It should be noted that although the Gender Window is funded by the CRPPF, it can be leveraged to support other CRPPF investments or outcomes of the Investment Fund in CRPP countries. Examples include: (i) generating the necessary political will, and commitment from governments and communities to implement gender-sensitive adaptation measures which help tackle climate change; (ii) creating policy incentives and procedures for the inclusion of women in adaptation planning and implementation at scale; (iii) developing and disseminating tools and procedures for climate risk informed development planning and implementation which can empower local women to act on adaptation; and (iv) place women at the centre

# ACTION PLAN IMPLEMENTATION, MONITORING AND REPORTING

The CRPP Gender Action Plan (CRPP GAP) will come into effect upon approval of the program by GCF. By including gender-related activities that are in line with the sectors of intervention of the program and related to program activities, the CRPP program level GAP presented in this annex is designed to be a living document. The CRPP sub-project teams will continue to formulate their own GAP according to ADB and GCF gender policies. The sub-project team GAPs will be submitted to GCF in batches on an annual basis along with the approved sub-project documents.

ADB as the Accredited Entity and Executive Unit, monitoring, report, and monitor the CRPP GAP implementation progress against the baseline data provided in the CRPP program level ADB program and project monitoring systems will be followed, in accordance with ADB’s policies, procedures, and guidelines. The CRPP GAP will also include a set of indicators to measure progress of the plan. Numeric targets have been included in the CRPP's Gap where possible to measure the Gap progress and the gender equality outcomes of the program. In some cases, country-specific data is not available for all activities at this time; therefore, discussion with the resident mission will be required before it is known which activities are feasible in each country.

If any activity is not feasible or if there is no baseline data to measure progress on a particular regard, some indicators may be improved to be more precise once sub-project Gender Analysis and Action Plan (GAAP) is completed. The CRPP GAP will also include an estimate at a program level how many women farmers may benefit from capacity building and agricultural extension services provided by the project. This will be updated as more information becomes available from activities as detailed sub-project development advances.

Current targets should thus be considered as indicative and will be updated based on the sub-project specific GAAP developed for each sub-project. The CRPP GAP will also include a list of indicators that will be prepared by ADB to GCF, which will capture the implementation progress of the program.


The CRPP investment eligibility criteria require all supported sub-projects to be classified as either of the following: (i) gender equality and empowerment; or (ii) women’s economic empowerment. Under ADB standard gender policy, this classification explicitly necessitates the development of a Gender Action Plan (GAP). The proposed gender action plan will be developed in consultation with the host country counterparts and will be aligned accordingly.

The proposed gender category is determined by the resident mission's country-level gender analysis. If no such analysis has been completed or if one is not in the pipeline, the proposed gender category is confirmed by ADB’s gender equality thematic group, which has a member in the CRPP secretariat. At that point, due to diligence, the last possible time for the resident mission to complete its own country-level gender analysis will be during the development of the GAP.

The gender analysis is based on desk research, gender diagnostics, consultations at the community level, household survey and national level data. Based on the findings of the gender analysis, the gender action plan activities and targets are identified, and the gender analysis report is submitted to ADB for approval. The resident mission will be responsible for implementing these targets. The GAP will also identify the relevant stakeholders responsible for implementation of each activity. The CRPP secretariat will provide technical assistance.

A budget for CAP implementation is always allocated, but the exact budget will vary according to the size and complexity of the project. In most cases, the typical budget range per host mission has been close contact with their country counterparts during this process, providing close collaboration in the design, implementation and monitoring of the GAPs


---
**Page 241**
---

ADB resident missions which lead the project design have gender specialists and there is at least one gender focal point for each department, sometimes two gender focal points, and these work full time on gender, as well as the gender specialist in resident missions who work full-time on gender. The gender focal points are responsible for ensuring gender completion reporting. Quarterly reports are submitted per project, and these are tracked by the regional gender focal points. Gender specialists (e.g., gender and livelihoods specialist) are usually contracted to conduct the consultations with the community which include consultations with women and women's groups and support the development of a gender action plan (GAP). The GAP is included in the Project Preparation Document, as it is a requirement that a gender specialist be hired. Budget is allocated from the project preparation resources.

Project level monitoring and reporting will combine ADB policies and protocols with GCF criteria to ensure complementarity to existing requirements without adding unnecessary extra reporting burden. Gender specialists are responsible for ensuring that gender is mainstreamed into sub-project GAPs, and gender specialists participate in team monitoring missions. Mid-term reviews of the project are conducted by the resident mission and the regional gender focal points. The GAP is included in the Project Administration Manual as well as in the loan covenants. No changes to the GAP are allowed without engagement from the Gender Equality Thematic group and only under very specific circumstances.

CRPP GENDER ACTION PLAN

Impact Statement: Climate resilient sustainable development in Asia and the Pacific region is advanced.

Outcomes:
(i) Enabling environment for knowledge, finance and implementation of gender-responsive and/or women-led adaptation measures is improved.
(ii) Finance for pro-poor, gender-responsive and/or women-led adaptation is mobilized at scale.
(iii) Food security and livelihoods are enhanced.
(iv) Exposure of poor and vulnerable women to climate shocks and stresses is reduced.

CRPP Outputs

Output 1: Knowledge and action research on climate risk-informed pro-poor community level solutions strengthened.

Activity 1.1 Knowledge and evidence generation.

1.1.1 Carry out research to generate knowledge and evidence that makes the case for women-led adaptation investments in at least 5 CRPP countries.
1.1.2 Support small-scale innovations and pilot projects focused on building women's resilience.

Timeline: Year 1
Responsibilities: CRPP Secretariat
Cost: $1,375 million

Activity 1.2 Evidence sharing and awareness raising.

1.2.1 Host multi-stakeholder policy dialogues and knowledge-sharing forums to build a culture conducive to increased investment in women's climate resilience.
1.2.2 Implement skills development trainings for women to build their capacity to effectively participate in multi-stakeholder policy dialogues and knowledge-sharing forums.

1.2.1 Stakeholders from at least 7 CRPP countries will be able to share their policy dialogue and knowledge-sharing forum experiences with other stakeholders, leading to increased investment in women's climate resilience.
1.2.2 Women from 7 CRPP countries will have the skills and confidence to effectively participate in multi-stakeholder policy dialogues and knowledge-sharing forums.

The CRPP Gender Window has an allocation of approximately 15% of the total CRPP Trust Fund budget. This is broken down between the three Trust Fund outputs as follows: Output 1 12.5%, Output 2 12.5%, Output 3 75%.

- 3.1.2 Carry out gender assessments and develop gender action plans for all CRPP IF sub-projects.
